DSA in Java — Concept Notes

Interview Q&A LeetCode Cheat Sheet

Data structures, algorithms and the problem-solving patterns behind interview questions, in easy words and full depth. Every solution is in Java and has been compiled and run. Print this page for a PDF.

Part 1 — Foundations

1. How to Approach a DSA Problem

In plain words

A coding interview is not a test of whether you have memorised the answer. It is a test of how you think: can you understand a fuzzy problem, find a correct solution, make it fast, and write clean code while explaining yourself?

The trick is to follow the same steps every time. A fixed routine stops you from panicking, stops you from coding the wrong thing, and shows the interviewer a calm, organised engineer.

Like a pilot's pre-flight checklist. Experienced pilots still walk through it every single time — not because they forget how to fly, but because the checklist catches the mistakes that confidence hides.

The eight-step framework

1 Clarify ask questions until the problem is unambiguous | 2 Examples write 2-3 inputs with outputs, including edge cases | 3 Brute force say the simplest correct idea + its Big-O (do not code yet) | 4 Bottleneck which part is slow or repeated? what work is wasted? | 5 Optimise pick a better data structure / pattern; state new Big-O | 6 Code clean, named variables, small helpers | 7 Dry-run trace your code by hand on one example | 8 Test edge cases: empty, one element, duplicates, negatives, max size

Budget roughly: 5 minutes for steps 1–2, 5–10 minutes for 3–5, 15–20 minutes to code, 5 minutes to dry-run and test. If you jump straight to step 6, you will often solve the wrong problem.

Step 1 — Clarify

Problems are left vague on purpose. The interviewer wants to see you ask. Good questions:

  • Input: What type and size? Can it be empty? Can values be negative, zero, duplicate? Is the array sorted?
  • Output: Return indices or values? Any valid answer or all of them? What if there is no answer — return -1, empty list, or throw?
  • Constraints: How big is n? (This tells you the target complexity — see section 2.) Does it fit in memory? Can I modify the input?

Step 2 — Examples and edge cases

Write a normal example and then attack the corners. An edge case is an input at the boundary of what is allowed, where code usually breaks:

Input typeEdge cases to try
Array / listempty, one element, two elements, all equal, already sorted, reverse sorted
Numbers0, negative, Integer.MAX_VALUE (overflow), Integer.MIN_VALUE
Stringempty "", one char, all same char, spaces, upper vs lower case
Tree / linked listnull root/head, single node, fully skewed (a "tree" that is really a line)
Graphdisconnected parts, cycles, self-loops, a single node

Step 3 — Brute force first

Say the most obvious correct solution out loud, with its complexity: "The simplest thing is to try every pair, which is O(n²)." This proves you understand the problem, gives you a fallback answer, and gives you something to improve. You usually do not code it — ask "should I code this or look for something faster?"

Steps 4–5 — Find the bottleneck, then optimise

Look at the brute force and ask: what work am I repeating? Most optimisations are one of these moves:

If you see…Try…Typical gain
"Have I seen X before?" inside a loopHashMap / HashSetO(n²) → O(n)
Sorted array, or can sortTwo pointers or binary searchO(n²) → O(n log n) or O(n)
Contiguous subarray / substringSliding window or prefix sumsO(n²) → O(n)
"Top k", "smallest", "next best"Heap (PriorityQueue)O(n log n) → O(n log k)
Same subproblem solved again and againMemoisation / dynamic programmingO(2ⁿ) → O(n²) or better
"All combinations / permutations"Backtracking (it is exponential; prune early)Cuts branches, not the class

A very common trade: spend memory to save time. A HashMap costs O(n) extra space but turns an O(n) search into an O(1) lookup.

Step 6 — Code

  • Use clear names: left, right, seen, maxLen — not a, b, x.
  • Pull repeated logic into a helper (isValid(r, c), swap(arr, i, j)).
  • Handle the edge cases you listed at the top of the method.
  • If you are unsure of a library method, say so and pick a sensible name: "I'll assume a helper that returns the neighbours."

Steps 7–8 — Dry-run and test

Do not say "I think it works." Pick a small example and trace the variables line by line, out loud. Then run through your edge cases. Finding your own bug is a positive signal — it shows you test your work. Finish by restating the time and space complexity.

How to talk while coding

  • Narrate intent, not syntax. Say "now I shrink the window from the left until it's valid again," not "i plus plus."
  • Announce trade-offs. "I'll use extra O(n) space for the map to get linear time."
  • Check in at decision points. "Does that approach sound good before I code it?"
  • It is fine to go quiet for 20–30 seconds to think — just say "let me think about this for a moment" first.

What to do when you are stuck

  1. Go back to a tiny example and solve it by hand. Watch what your brain does — that is often the algorithm.
  2. Ask "what if the input were sorted?" or "what if I had a HashMap of everything?"
  3. Walk the pattern table above: two pointers? sliding window? BFS? heap? DP?
  4. Solve a simpler version first (1D before 2D, no duplicates before duplicates, k = 1 before general k).
  5. Say what you are thinking. Interviewers give hints to candidates who are visibly reasoning, not to silent ones.
  6. If time runs low, code the brute force cleanly. A working O(n²) beats a broken O(n).

Worked example — Two Sum, end to end

Problem: given an array nums and a target, return the indices of the two numbers that add up to target.

1. Clarify. "Is there always exactly one answer? Can I use the same element twice? Can values be negative? Is it sorted?" Assume: exactly one answer, not the same index twice, negatives allowed, not sorted.

2. Examples. [2,7,11,15], 9 → [0,1]. [3,2,4], 6 → [1,2] (note: not [0,0] — cannot reuse index 0). [3,3], 6 → [0,1] (duplicates).

3. Brute force. Try every pair (i, j) with j > i. That is about n²/2 pairs, so O(n²) time and O(1) space.

// Brute force: try every pair. O(n^2) time, O(1) space.
static int[] twoSumBrute(int[] nums, int target) {
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < nums.length; j++) {
            if (nums[i] + nums[j] == target) return new int[]{i, j};
        }
    }
    return new int[]{-1, -1};              // no pair found
}

4. Bottleneck. For each nums[i], the inner loop is really asking one question: "does the number target - nums[i] exist somewhere I've already been?" That is a lookup, and we are doing it with a slow linear scan.

5. Optimise. Remember every number we have passed in a HashMap from value to index. Then the lookup is O(1) on average, and one pass is enough.

import java.util.*;

class Solution {
    // One pass with a HashMap. O(n) time, O(n) space.
    public int[] twoSum(int[] nums, int target) {
        Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();   // value -> index
        for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
            int need = target - nums[i];                 // the partner we want
            if (seen.containsKey(need)) {
                return new int[]{seen.get(need), i};
            }
            seen.put(nums[i], i);                        // store AFTER checking
        }
        return new int[]{-1, -1};
    }
}

Why "store after checking"? If we stored first, then for [3,2,4], target 6 the element 3 would find itself (6 - 3 = 3) and return [0,0]. Checking first means we only ever pair with an earlier index.

7. Dry-run on nums = [2,7,11,15], target = 9:

inums[i]need = 9 - nums[i]seen before stepAction
027{}7 not in map; put 2 → 0
172{2=0}2 is in map → return [0, 1]

8. Test. Running the code gives: [2,7,11,15], 9 → [0, 1]; [3,2,4], 6 → [1, 2]; [3,3], 6 → [0, 1] (the second 3 finds the first); [1,2], 7 → [-1, -1] (no answer).

Complexity: O(n) time — one pass, each map operation O(1) on average. O(n) space — the map may hold every element.

Follow-up the interviewer may ask: "What if the array is sorted?" Then use two pointers: left = 0, right = n - 1; if the sum is too small move left right, if too big move right left. That is O(n) time and O(1) space — better on memory than the map. Recognising when a different constraint unlocks a different pattern is exactly what they want to see.

2. Big-O & Complexity

In plain words

Big-O describes how the running time (or memory) of an algorithm grows as the input gets bigger. It ignores the exact seconds, the machine, and small details, and keeps only the shape of the growth: does doubling the input double the work, quadruple it, or barely change it?

Time complexity counts steps. Space complexity counts extra memory. Both are written as a function of n, the input size.

Handing out invitations. Walking to each of n guests is O(n). Having every guest shake hands with every other guest is O(n²). Shouting once in the hall is O(1) — the same effort whether 10 or 10,000 people are there.

What Big-O really means

Formally, f(n) = O(g(n)) means that for large enough n, f(n) is at most some constant times g(n). In practice this gives two rules:

  • Drop constants. 3n + 5 steps is O(n). Two separate passes over the array is still O(n).
  • Keep only the biggest term. n² + 100n + 7 is O(n²), because when n is a million, the n² part dwarfs everything else.

Related words you may hear: Big-Omega (Ω) is a lower bound ("at least this much"), Big-Theta (Θ) is a tight bound (both). In interviews, people say "Big-O" and mean the tight worst-case bound.

How to count — the patterns

// O(n): one loop over the input
static int sum(int[] a) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int x : a) total += x;          // runs n times
    return total;
}

// O(n^2): a loop inside a loop, both over n
static int countPairs(int[] a) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++)
        for (int j = i + 1; j < a.length; j++)   // n-1, n-2, ... 1 -> n(n-1)/2
            count++;
    return count;
}

// O(log n): the problem halves every step
static int halvings(int n) {
    int steps = 0;
    while (n > 1) {
        n /= 2;                           // n, n/2, n/4, ... 1
        steps++;
    }
    return steps;
}

// O(n log n): n outer iterations, each doing log n work
static int nLogN(int n) {
    int ops = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j < n; j *= 2)    // doubles -> log n times
            ops++;
    return ops;
}

// Two SEPARATE loops over different inputs: O(n + m), not O(n * m)
static int twoLoops(int[] a, int[] b) {
    int ops = 0;
    for (int x : a) ops++;
    for (int y : b) ops++;
    return ops;
}

Running these on an 8-element array: countPairs does 28 steps (= 8·7/2), nLogN(8) does 24 (= 8·3), and halvings(1_000_000) takes only 19 steps. That last number is why O(log n) feels almost free.

  • Sequential blocks add: loop A then loop B is O(A + B).
  • Nested blocks multiply: loop A containing loop B is O(A × B).
  • Halving or doubling the loop variable gives a log.
  • Different inputs get different letters: looping over a string of length n and a list of length m is O(n + m) or O(n·m) — never just "O(n)".
  • Hidden loops count: list.contains(x), s.substring(...), String + in a loop, Arrays.sort all cost more than O(1).

Why log n is so small — binary search

// Binary search: O(log n) time, O(1) space
static int binarySearch(int[] a, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;     // avoids (lo + hi) overflow
        if (a[mid] == target) return mid;
        if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
        else hi = mid - 1;
    }
    return -1;
}

Counting recursion

For recursive code, ask two questions: how many calls happen in total, and how much work does each call do outside its recursive calls? Multiply them.

  • One call per level, n levels, O(1) work each (factorial) → O(n).
  • One call on half the input, O(1) work each (binary search) → O(log n).
  • Two calls on n - 1 (naive Fibonacci) → the calls double each level → O(2ⁿ).
  • Two calls on half, O(n) merge work (merge sort) → log n levels, O(n) per level → O(n log n).

A useful rule: a recursion with b branches and depth d makes about bd calls.

Best, average and worst case

The same algorithm can take very different time depending on the input:

AlgorithmBestAverageWorstWorst happens when
Linear searchO(1)O(n)O(n)target is last or missing
QuicksortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n²)pivot is always the smallest/largest
HashMap getO(1)O(1)O(n), or O(log n) in Java 8+many keys collide into one bucket
Insertion sortO(n)O(n²)O(n²)array is reverse sorted

When nobody says which, give the worst case, and mention the average if it is different (for hashing and quicksort, always mention it).

Amortised complexity — ArrayList growth

Amortised means "averaged over a long sequence of operations." Some single operations are expensive, but they are rare enough that the average cost per operation stays small.

An ArrayList is backed by a fixed-size array. When it is full, add allocates a new array about 1.5 times bigger and copies everything over — an O(n) step. But after that, the next many adds are O(1) again. Because the capacity grows by a multiplied amount, the total copying over n adds is a geometric series that adds up to a constant times n.

Simulating 1,000,000 adds with Java's 1.5x growth rule (starting at capacity 10) gives 2,430,972 element copies in total — about 2.4 copies per add. A constant, so add is amortised O(1).

Why multiply, not add? If the list grew by a fixed +10 slots each time, you would copy 10 + 20 + 30 + … + n elements = O(n²) total, so each add would be O(n) on average. Growing by a factor is what makes it cheap. The same idea applies to StringBuilder and HashMap resizing.

Space complexity (and the hidden stack)

Space complexity counts the extra memory your algorithm uses, not the input itself (say so if you are unsure — some interviewers count the input). Watch for:

  • New arrays, lists, maps, sets: O(size).
  • Copies: substring, toCharArray, new ArrayList<>(other) are all O(length).
  • The recursion stack: every active call holds a stack frame. A recursion that goes n levels deep uses O(n) space even if it creates no objects.
// Recursion that sums an array: O(n) time AND O(n) space (n stack frames)
static int sumRec(int[] a, int i) {
    if (i == a.length) return 0;
    return a[i] + sumRec(a, i + 1);
}

The iterative sum above does the same job in O(1) space. Recursive DFS on a tree uses O(h) space where h is the height: O(log n) for a balanced tree but O(n) for a skewed one.

Common complexity classes

Big-ONamen = 1,000,000 means roughlyTypical examples
O(1)Constant1 stepArray index, HashMap get/put, stack push/pop
O(log n)Logarithmic20 stepsBinary search, balanced BST / TreeMap ops, heap push/pop
O(√n)Square root1,000 stepsChecking if n is prime by trial division
O(n)Linear10⁶ stepsOne pass, two pointers, sliding window, BFS/DFS over V + E
O(n log n)Linearithmic2 × 10⁷ stepsSorting (merge sort, Arrays.sort), n heap operations
O(n²)Quadratic10¹² — too slowAll pairs, simple DP on two strings, bubble/insertion sort
O(n³)Cubicway too slowFloyd–Warshall, naive matrix multiply, interval DP
O(2ⁿ)ExponentialimpossibleAll subsets, naive recursive Fibonacci
O(n!)FactorialimpossibleAll permutations, brute-force travelling salesman

Input size → which complexity fits

A typical judge (and a typical interviewer's mental model) allows about 10⁸ simple operations per second. So the constraint on n tells you the complexity they expect — a huge hint that is often ignored:

If n is up to…Target complexityUsually means
≤ 10O(n!)Try all permutations; backtracking
≤ 20O(2ⁿ) or O(2ⁿ · n)All subsets, bitmask DP
≤ 500O(n³)Triple loop, interval DP, Floyd–Warshall
≤ 5,000O(n²)Double loop, 2D DP
≤ 10⁶O(n log n)Sort, heap, binary search, divide and conquer
larger (10⁸, 10¹⁸)O(n) or O(log n)One pass, math, binary search on the answer

The Master Theorem in easy words

Many divide-and-conquer algorithms have a running time like

T(n) = a · T(n / b) + f(n)
  a    = how many sub-problems each call makes
  n/b  = how big each sub-problem is
  f(n) = the work done in this call itself (splitting + combining)

Picture the recursion as a tree. The top level does f(n) work. The bottom level has a huge number of tiny leaves. The Master Theorem simply asks: which part of the tree does most of the work? Compare f(n) with nlog_b(a) (the number of leaves):

Level 0: 1 call doing f(n) Level 1: a calls each on n/b Level 2: a² calls each on n/b² ... (log_b n levels) Leaves: n^(log_b a) tiny calls Case 1 leaves win (f(n) smaller) T = O(n^(log_b a)) Case 2 a tie (f(n) same size) T = O(f(n) · log n) Case 3 root wins (f(n) bigger) T = O(f(n))
AlgorithmRecurrenceLeaves nlog_b aWho winsResult
Binary searchT(n) = T(n/2) + O(1)n⁰ = 1TieO(log n)
Merge sortT(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n)n¹ = nTieO(n log n)
Tree traversalT(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(1)nLeavesO(n)
Karatsuba multiplyT(n) = 3T(n/2) + O(n)n^1.58LeavesO(n^1.58)
Quickselect (average)T(n) = T(n/2) + O(n)1RootO(n)
Interview tip: you rarely need to quote the theorem. Just say "there are log n levels, and each level does O(n) total work, so O(n log n)." Drawing the recursion tree and summing each level is the reasoning the theorem packages up.

3. Recursion & the Call Stack

In plain words

Recursion is when a method solves a problem by calling itself on a smaller version of the same problem, until the problem is so small the answer is obvious.

Every recursive method needs two parts: a base case (the tiny problem you answer directly, which stops the recursion) and a recursive case (break the problem down, call yourself on the smaller piece, and combine the result).

Russian nesting dolls. To find the smallest doll you open a doll, and if there is another inside, you do the same thing again. The base case is the solid doll that does not open. Then you put them back together in reverse order.

The two rules

// factorial: n! = n * (n-1) * ... * 1.  O(n) time, O(n) stack space
static long factorial(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;              // base case: answer directly
    return n * factorial(n - 1);       // recursive case: smaller input
}
// factorial(5)  = 120
// factorial(20) = 2432902008176640000  (factorial(21) overflows long)
  1. Every path must reach a base case. Forget it, or never shrink the input, and you recurse forever.
  2. Each call must move toward the base case (n - 1, n / 2, index + 1, a smaller subtree).

Trust the recursion (the "leap of faith"): when writing factorial(n), assume factorial(n - 1) already works and only ask "how do I use it to build my answer?" Do not try to trace every level in your head while writing — trace afterwards to check.

What the call stack actually does

The JVM keeps a call stack for each thread. Every time a method is called, a new stack frame is pushed on top. The frame holds that call's parameters, local variables, and where to return to. When the method returns, its frame is popped and the caller continues exactly where it paused.

So recursion is not magic: factorial(3) pauses in the middle of 3 * ?, waiting for factorial(2) to finish, which pauses waiting for factorial(1).

Recursion trees — naive Fibonacci

When a method calls itself more than once, draw a recursion tree: each node is one call, its children are the calls it makes.

// naive fib: O(2^n) time, O(n) stack space
static long fib(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;                // fib(0) = 0, fib(1) = 1
    return fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2);
}
fib(5) / \ fib(4) fib(3) / \ / \ fib(3) fib(2) fib(2) fib(1) / \ / \ / \ fib(2) fib(1) fib(1) fib(0) fib(1) fib(0) / \ fib(1) fib(0) fib(3) is computed 2 times, fib(2) 3 times -- the same work, over and over.

Each call branches into 2, and the tree is up to n levels deep, so there are up to about 2ⁿ calls: O(2ⁿ) time. Counting for real: fib(10) makes 177 calls, and fib(30) makes 2,692,537. But the space is only O(n): at any moment only one root-to-leaf path is on the stack, and the deepest path has n frames.

Time vs space for recursion: time is the total number of nodes in the recursion tree (times work per node). Space is the height of the tree (the deepest the stack gets). Mixing these up is a very common interview slip.

Memoisation preview

The tree above repeats the same subproblems. Memoisation means caching each answer the first time you compute it and returning the cached value next time. Each fib(k) is then computed once:

// memoised fib: O(n) time, O(n) space (memo array + stack)
static long fibMemo(int n, long[] memo) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    if (memo[n] != 0) return memo[n];            // already solved: reuse
    return memo[n] = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
}
// fibMemo(50, new long[51]) = 12586269025, instantly.
// The naive version would need billions of calls.

This is the doorway to dynamic programming: "recursion + a cache" (top-down) or "fill a table in a loop" (bottom-up). It gets its own part later.

Head vs tail recursion

Where you do the work relative to the recursive call changes the order of results:

// Head recursion: recurse FIRST, work on the way back up
static void printHead(int n) {
    if (n == 0) return;
    printHead(n - 1);
    System.out.print(n + " ");            // prints: 1 2 3
}

// Tail recursion: work FIRST, the recursive call is the very last thing
static void printTail(int n) {
    if (n == 0) return;
    System.out.print(n + " ");            // prints: 3 2 1
    printTail(n - 1);
}

A tail call means nothing is left to do after the call returns, so in theory the frame could be reused. Some languages (Scheme, Scala with @tailrec, Kotlin tailrec) do this tail-call optimisation. Java does not. A tail-recursive method in Java still uses O(n) stack.

StackOverflowError and converting to iteration

The stack has a fixed size (roughly 512 KB to 1 MB per thread by default, adjustable with -Xss). Recurse too deep and the JVM throws StackOverflowError. On a default JVM, an empty infinite recursion died after about 67,000 frames in a quick test; real methods with more locals die sooner. So deep recursion on 10⁵–10⁶ elements (a long linked list, a skewed tree, DFS on a big grid) can crash.

// Tail-recursive sum with an accumulator -- still overflows in Java for n = 1,000,000
static long sumTail(int n, long acc) {
    if (n == 0) return acc;
    return sumTail(n - 1, acc + n);
}

// Same logic as a loop: O(n) time, O(1) space, cannot overflow the stack
static long sumIter(int n) {
    long acc = 0;
    while (n > 0) { acc += n; n--; }
    return acc;
}
// sumTail(100, 0) = 5050, sumIter(100) = 5050
// sumTail(1_000_000, 0) -> StackOverflowError
// sumIter(1_000_000)    -> 500000500000

How to convert recursion to iteration:

  • Tail recursion → a plain loop. The parameters become loop variables (as above).
  • General recursion (tree DFS, backtracking) → use your own explicit stack: Deque<Node> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(). Push the starting state, then loop: pop, process, push the children. Your stack lives on the heap, which is much bigger.
  • Recursion with memo → a bottom-up DP table filled in a loop.

Classic examples

Fast power — compute xⁿ by squaring half the answer instead of multiplying n times:

// x^n in O(log n) time, O(log n) stack
static double myPow(double x, long n) {
    if (n < 0) return 1 / myPow(x, -n);   // long n: -Integer.MIN_VALUE would overflow an int
    if (n == 0) return 1;                  // base case
    double half = myPow(x, n / 2);         // solve half the problem ONCE
    return (n % 2 == 0) ? half * half : half * half * x;
}
// myPow(2, 10) = 1024.0, myPow(2, -2) = 0.25

The key is calling myPow(x, n / 2) once and storing it. Writing myPow(x, n/2) * myPow(x, n/2) makes two calls per level and falls back to O(n).

Reverse a string — swap the ends, then recurse on the middle:

// O(n) time, O(n) stack (n/2 frames)
static void reverse(char[] s, int left, int right) {
    if (left >= right) return;             // base case: 0 or 1 chars left
    char tmp = s[left]; s[left] = s[right]; s[right] = tmp;
    reverse(s, left + 1, right - 1);
}
// "hello" -> "olleh"

All subsets (preview of backtracking) — for each element, make two choices: take it or skip it:

// O(n * 2^n) time (2^n subsets, each copied in O(n)), O(n) stack
static void subsets(int[] nums, int i, List<Integer> current, List<List<Integer>> out) {
    if (i == nums.length) {                // decided for every element
        out.add(new ArrayList<>(current)); // copy! current keeps changing
        return;
    }
    current.add(nums[i]);                  // choice 1: take nums[i]
    subsets(nums, i + 1, current, out);
    current.remove(current.size() - 1);    // undo the choice (backtrack)
    subsets(nums, i + 1, current, out);    // choice 2: skip nums[i]
}
// [1,2,3] -> [[1, 2, 3], [1, 2], [1, 3], [1], [2, 3], [2], [3], []]
start [] take 1 / \ skip 1 [1] [] take 2 / \ skip take / \ skip [1,2] [1] [2] [] / \ / \ / \ / \ [1,2,3][1,2][1,3][1] [2,3][2] [3] [] <- 8 leaves = 2^3 subsets
Recursion checklist for interviews: (1) state the base case first, (2) make sure each call shrinks the input, (3) decide what the call returns and trust it, (4) give time as "number of calls × work per call" and space as "max depth", (5) if depth could reach ~10⁴ or more, mention the stack risk and the iterative alternative.

4. Java Toolkit for DSA

In plain words

In an interview you do not want to fight the language. This section is the small set of Java classes and methods that show up in almost every DSA solution — which one to pick, the exact method names, and the traps that cause wrong answers.

The big idea: pick the collection whose fast operations match what your algorithm does most. Need "have I seen this?" → HashSet. Need "smallest so far" → PriorityQueue. Need "closest key below x" → TreeMap.

A carpenter's toolbox. You can drive a screw with a hammer, but it is slow and ugly. Knowing exactly which tool is in which drawer is half the skill.

Arrays

int[] a = {5, 3, 9, 1, 7};
Arrays.sort(a);                                  // [1, 3, 5, 7, 9]  O(n log n)
int[] dp = new int[5];
Arrays.fill(dp, -1);                             // [-1, -1, -1, -1, -1]
int[] part = Arrays.copyOfRange(a, 1, 4);        // [3, 5, 7]  (end index is EXCLUSIVE)
List<String> names = Arrays.asList("bob", "amy", "cat");  // fixed-size list view
int[][] grid = new int[3][4];                    // 3 rows, 4 cols, all 0
System.out.println(Arrays.toString(a));          // printing needs toString / deepToString

// Descending order needs boxed Integer[], not int[]
Integer[] boxed = {5, 3, 9, 1};
Arrays.sort(boxed, Collections.reverseOrder());  // [9, 5, 3, 1]

// Sort 2D array (e.g. intervals) by first element
int[][] intervals = {{5, 6}, {1, 3}, {2, 4}};
Arrays.sort(intervals, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[0], y[0]));  // [[1, 3], [2, 4], [5, 6]]

int total = Arrays.stream(a).sum();              // 25
int max = Arrays.stream(a).max().getAsInt();     // 9
  • Arrays.sort(int[]) uses dual-pivot quicksort (O(n log n) average, not stable — fine for primitives). Arrays.sort(Object[]) uses TimSort, which is stable (equal elements keep their order) and guaranteed O(n log n).
  • Arrays.asList returns a fixed-size view: add/remove throw UnsupportedOperationException. Wrap it: new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(...)). And Arrays.asList(intArray) gives a list with one element (the array itself) — it does not box.
  • List.of(...) is fully immutable and rejects null.

String vs StringBuilder

A Java String is immutable: it can never change. s += "x" builds a brand-new string and copies all the old characters. In a loop that is O(n²) total. StringBuilder is a growable char buffer: append is amortised O(1).

String s = "hello";
s.charAt(1);          // 'e'   O(1)
s.length();           // 5     (method for String; field .length for arrays; .size() for collections)
s.substring(1, 3);    // "el"  O(k) -- makes a copy, end exclusive
char[] cs = s.toCharArray();
Arrays.sort(cs);
new String(cs);       // "ehllo" -- sorted letters (anagram key)
String.valueOf(42);   // "42"
s.equals("hello");    // compare CONTENT with equals, never ==

StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) sb.append(i).append(',');
sb.setLength(sb.length() - 1);   // drop last comma -> "0,1,2"
sb.reverse();                    // "2,1,0"  (reverses in place)
sb.insert(0, 'x'); sb.deleteCharAt(0); sb.setCharAt(0, 'y');
String result = sb.toString();

new String("hi") == new String("hi") is false (two different objects) while .equals is true. Always use equals for strings.

char arithmetic

A char is really a small number (its Unicode code). Subtracting 'a' turns a lowercase letter into an index 0–25 — the standard way to count letters with an int[26] instead of a map.

'd' - 'a'            // 3       letter -> index
(char) ('a' + 3)     // 'd'     index -> letter (cast back!)
'7' - '0'            // 7       digit char -> int
Character.isDigit('7'); Character.isLetter('x'); Character.isLetterOrDigit(c);
Character.toUpperCase('q');     // 'Q'

int[] freq = new int[26];
for (char ch : "banana".toCharArray()) freq[ch - 'a']++;
// freq[0] ('a') = 3, freq['n' - 'a'] = 2

ArrayList

List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30));
list.add(40);                      // append, amortised O(1)
list.add(0, 5);                    // insert at index: O(n) -- shifts everything
list.get(2); list.set(2, 99);      // O(1)
list.remove(Integer.valueOf(20));  // remove by VALUE, O(n)
list.remove(0);                    // remove by INDEX, O(n)
list.contains(30);                 // O(n) linear scan!

Stack and queue: use ArrayDeque

ArrayDeque (a resizable circular array) is the right tool for both a stack and a queue. It is faster than LinkedList (no node object per element, better cache use) and does not allow null.

Stack (LIFO) -- push/pop/peek work on the FRONT push(1) push(2) push(3) -> [3, 2, 1] peek() = 3, pop() = 3 -> [2, 1] ^ top Queue (FIFO) -- offer adds at the BACK, poll removes from the FRONT offer(1) offer(2) offer(3) -> [1, 2, 3] peek() = 1, poll() = 1 -> [2, 3] ^ front ^ back
Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
stack.push(1); stack.push(2); stack.push(3);
stack.peek();   // 3
stack.pop();    // 3  -> stack is [2, 1]

Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
queue.offer(1); queue.offer(2); queue.offer(3);
queue.peek();   // 1
queue.poll();   // 1  -> queue is [2, 3]

// Deque (both ends): offerFirst/offerLast, pollFirst/pollLast, peekFirst/peekLast
// Empty behaviour: pop()/remove() THROW; poll()/peek() return null

Why not java.util.Stack? It is an old class that extends Vector: every method is synchronized (slow, pointless for one thread), and because it is a Vector you can insert into the middle, which breaks the stack idea. Java's own docs recommend Deque instead. Mentioning this is a small but good signal.

When is LinkedList useful? Rarely in interviews. It allows null and implements both List and Deque, but get(i) is O(n). Use ArrayDeque for queues and ArrayList for lists.

HashMap and HashSet

// Counting: three equivalent styles
Map<String, Integer> count = new HashMap<>();
for (String w : "a b a c b a".split(" ")) count.merge(w, 1, Integer::sum);   // {a=3, b=2, c=1}
map.put(k, map.getOrDefault(k, 0) + 1);                   // classic

// Grouping: create the list the first time a key is seen
Map<String, List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
for (String w : List.of("eat", "tea", "tan", "ate")) {
    char[] k = w.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(k);
    groups.computeIfAbsent(new String(k), key -> new ArrayList<>()).add(w);
}
// {aet=[eat, tea, ate], ant=[tan]}

// Iterate keys and values together
for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : count.entrySet()) {
    System.out.println(e.getKey() + ":" + e.getValue());
}
// also: map.keySet(), map.values(), map.containsKey(k), map.remove(k), map.putIfAbsent(k, v)

Set<Integer> set = new HashSet<>();
set.add(5);        // true  (was new)
set.add(5);        // false (already there) -- handy for "seen before?" in one call
set.contains(5);   // true
  • HashMap iteration order is not defined. Use LinkedHashMap for insertion order (also the basis of an LRU cache), TreeMap for sorted order.
  • Keys must have correct equals and hashCode. int[] does not — two arrays with the same contents are different keys. Use a String key, a List<Integer>, a record, or encode a pair as (long) r * cols + c.

TreeMap and TreeSet — sorted, with "nearest" queries

A red-black tree (a self-balancing binary search tree) under the hood: every operation is O(log n) and keys stay sorted. Use them when you need "the closest key to x" or ordered iteration.

TreeMap<Integer, String> tm = new TreeMap<>(Map.of(10, "a", 20, "b", 30, "c"));
tm.floorKey(25);     // 20    largest key <= 25
tm.ceilingKey(25);   // 30    smallest key >= 25
tm.lowerKey(10);     // null  largest key strictly < 10
tm.higherKey(30);    // null  smallest key strictly > 30
tm.firstKey(); tm.lastKey();   // 10, 30
tm.headMap(20);      // {10=a}          keys < 20
tm.tailMap(20);      // {20=b, 30=c}    keys >= 20
// also: floorEntry, ceilingEntry, pollFirstEntry, descendingMap

TreeSet<Integer> ts = new TreeSet<>(List.of(4, 1, 9, 6));   // [1, 4, 6, 9]
ts.floor(5);         // 4
ts.ceiling(5);       // 6
ts.pollFirst();      // 1 (removes it)

Always check for null — the "nearest" methods return null when nothing qualifies, and unboxing that into an int throws NullPointerException.

PriorityQueue — a heap

A binary heap: peek gives the smallest element in O(1); offer and poll are O(log n). By default it is a min-heap.

PriorityQueue<Integer> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>();
PriorityQueue<Integer> maxHeap = new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.reverseOrder());
for (int v : new int[]{5, 1, 8, 3}) { minHeap.offer(v); maxHeap.offer(v); }
minHeap.poll();   // 1
maxHeap.poll();   // 8

// Heap of arrays: {node, distance}, ordered by distance (Dijkstra style)
PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((p, q) -> Integer.compare(p[1], q[1]));
pq.offer(new int[]{0, 7}); pq.offer(new int[]{1, 2}); pq.offer(new int[]{2, 5});
pq.poll();        // [1, 2] -- smallest distance first
  • Iterating or printing a PriorityQueue does not give sorted order — only poll does.
  • remove(Object) and contains are O(n).
  • Building from a collection, new PriorityQueue<>(list), is O(n) (heapify), faster than n offers.

Collections helpers and Comparator chains

List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(3, 1, 2));
Collections.sort(nums);        // [1, 2, 3]
Collections.reverse(nums);     // [3, 2, 1]
Collections.max(nums);         // 3
Collections.min(nums);         // 1
Collections.frequency(List.of(1, 1, 2), 1);   // 2
Collections.swap(nums, 0, 2);  // also: Collections.nCopies, Collections.emptyList

record Person(String name, int age) {}
List<Person> people = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
    new Person("Zoe", 30), new Person("Adam", 25), new Person("Bob", 30)));
// age descending, then name ascending
people.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(Person::age).reversed()
                      .thenComparing(Person::name));
// [Bob 30, Zoe 30, Adam 25]

List<String> words = new ArrayList<>(List.of("pear", "fig", "apple", "kiwi"));
// shortest first; ties broken by reverse alphabetical
words.sort(Comparator.comparing(String::length)
                     .thenComparing(Comparator.reverseOrder()));
// [fig, pear, kiwi, apple]
Never compare with subtraction: the comparator (a, b) -> a - b overflows for large values. For example -2_000_000_000 - 2_000_000_000 evaluates to 294967296 — a positive number, so it says the negative value is larger. Use Integer.compare(a, b) (which returned -1 here) or Comparator.comparingInt.

Integer overflow

An int is 32 bits: range about ±2.1 billion (Integer.MAX_VALUE = 2147483647). Going past it silently wraps around to negative — no error.

int big = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
big + 1;                  // -2147483648   wrapped around!
(long) big + 1;           // 2147483648    cast BEFORE the math
int m = 100_000;
m * m;                    // 1410065408    wrong (true answer is 10^10)
(long) m * m;             // 10000000000   right
Math.addExact(big, 1);    // throws ArithmeticException("integer overflow")
int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;   // safe midpoint; (lo + hi) / 2 can overflow

Use long for sums of many values, products, and anything counted "modulo 10⁹ + 7" (multiply as long, then take %). (long) (m * m) is still wrong — the overflow already happened inside the brackets.

The Integer caching trap

Integer p1 = 127, p2 = 127, q1 = 128, q2 = 128;
p1 == p2;          // true   -- same cached object
q1 == q2;          // false  -- two different objects!
q1.equals(q2);     // true

Map<Character, Integer> m1 = ..., m2 = ...;   // both map 'a' -> 1000
m1.get('a') == m2.get('a');       // false  -- compares object identity
m1.get('a').equals(m2.get('a'));  // true

Java caches Integer objects for values −128 to 127, so == happens to work for small numbers in your tests and then fails on large ones. Whenever values come out of a collection, compare with .equals or unbox first (int x = map.get(k);). This bug is famous in "Minimum Window Substring"-style problems that compare two count maps.

int vs Integer in collections

  • Collections hold objects only, so List<int> is illegal; use List<Integer>. Java autoboxes for you, but each Integer is an object (≈16 bytes vs 4) — an int[] is much faster and smaller when the size is known.
  • Unboxing a null throws NullPointerException: int c = map.get(missingKey); crashes. Use getOrDefault.
  • List<Integer>.remove(1) removes index 1; remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) removes the value 1. From [1, 2, 3]: remove(1) gives [1, 3], then remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) gives [3].

Fast input (for online judges)

Scanner is convenient but slow for 10⁵+ numbers. Use BufferedReader with StringTokenizer:

BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));
int n = Integer.parseInt(br.readLine().trim());
StringTokenizer st = new StringTokenizer(br.readLine());
long total = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) total += Integer.parseInt(st.nextToken());
// For lots of output, collect in a StringBuilder and print once
// (or use PrintWriter) instead of calling System.out.println in a loop.

Operation cost cheat sheet

StructureAccess / getSearch / containsInsertDeleteNotes
int[] arrayO(1)O(n)——Fixed size; fastest
ArrayListO(1)O(n)O(1)* at end, O(n) middleO(1) at end, O(n) middle*amortised
LinkedListO(n)O(n)O(1) at endsO(1) at endsRarely the best choice
ArrayDequepeek O(1)O(n)O(1)* either endO(1) either endStack and queue
HashMap / HashSetO(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgNo order; worst O(log n) per bucket in Java 8+
LinkedHashMapO(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgKeeps insertion (or access) order
TreeMap / TreeSetO(log n)O(log n)O(log n)O(log n)Sorted; floor/ceiling O(log n); first/last O(log n)
PriorityQueuepeek O(1)O(n)O(log n)poll O(log n), remove(x) O(n)Min-heap by default

StringBuildercharAt O(1)O(n)append O(1)*deleteCharAt O(n)insert(0, …) is O(n)

5. Bit Manipulation

In plain words

Computers store every integer as a row of bits — 0s and 1s. Bit manipulation means working on those bits directly with operators like AND, OR and XOR. It gives tiny, very fast solutions to certain problems, and lets you use a single int as a set of up to 32 yes/no flags.

A row of light switches on a wall. The number is the pattern of which switches are on. Bit operations are like saying "flip switch 3", "turn on every switch that is on in either of these two rows", or "which switches differ between these two rows?"

Binary basics

In binary each position is a power of 2, counted from the right starting at position 0. So 1101 = 1·8 + 1·4 + 0·2 + 1·1 = 13.

Integer.toBinaryString(13);      // "1101"
Integer.parseInt("1101", 2);     // 13
int n = 0b1010;                  // binary literal = 10
// bit i has value 1 << i:  1 << 0 = 1, 1 << 3 = 8, 1 << 10 = 1024

Two's complement — how negatives are stored

Java's int is 32 bits and signed. The leftmost bit is the sign bit: 0 for non-negative, 1 for negative. Negatives use two's complement: to get -x, flip every bit of x and add 1. So -x == ~x + 1.

5 = 00000000 00000000 00000000 00000101 ~5 = 11111111 11111111 11111111 11111010 (flip every bit) = -6 -5 = 11111111 11111111 11111111 11111011 (~5 + 1) -1 = 11111111 11111111 11111111 11111111 (all ones) Range: Integer.MIN_VALUE = -2^31 = 1000...0000 Integer.MAX_VALUE = 2^31 - 1 = 0111...1111 Note: -Integer.MIN_VALUE overflows back to Integer.MIN_VALUE

Why this design? Addition works the same for positive and negative numbers, and there is only one zero. It also explains ~5 == -6, which we confirmed by running it.

The operators

OperatorNameRuleExample (12 = 1100, 10 = 1010)
&AND1 only if both bits are 112 & 10 = 1000 = 8
|OR1 if either bit is 112 | 10 = 1110 = 14
^XOR1 if the bits are different12 ^ 10 = 0110 = 6
~NOTflip every bit~5 = -6
<<Left shiftmove bits left, fill with 0 (multiply by 2ᵏ)5 << 2 = 20
>>Signed right shiftmove right, copy the sign bit in (divide by 2ᵏ, rounding down)20 >> 2 = 5, -20 >> 2 = -5
>>>Unsigned right shiftmove right, always fill with 0-20 >>> 28 = 15
  • Shift amounts are taken mod 32 for int: 1 << 32 is 1, not 0. And 1 << 31 is -2147483648 (the sign bit). Use 1L << k for 64-bit masks (1L << 32 = 4294967296).
  • Precedence trap: &, ^, | bind weaker than ==. n & 1 == 0 does not compile in Java (it parses as n & (1 == 0)). Always write (n & 1) == 0.
  • Odd/even: (n & 1) is 1 for odd, 0 for even — it works for negatives too, while -7 % 2 is -1, not 1.

Single-bit tricks

// i = bit position, 0 = rightmost.  All O(1).
static boolean isSet(int n, int i) { return (n & (1 << i)) != 0; }  // check
static int setBit(int n, int i)    { return n | (1 << i); }          // turn on
static int clearBit(int n, int i)  { return n & ~(1 << i); }         // turn off
static int toggleBit(int n, int i) { return n ^ (1 << i); }          // flip

// n = 0b1010 (10)
isSet(10, 1);      // true
isSet(10, 2);      // false
setBit(10, 0);     // 11  (1011)
clearBit(10, 3);   // 2   (0010)
toggleBit(10, 1);  // 8   (1000)

Pattern: build a mask with a single 1 at position i (1 << i), then AND to test, OR to set, AND-with-NOT to clear, XOR to flip.

The two most useful tricks: n & (n - 1) and n & -n

Subtracting 1 from a number flips its lowest 1 bit to 0 and all the 0s below it to 1. So n & (n - 1) wipes out exactly the lowest set bit. And because -n = ~n + 1, n & -n keeps only the lowest set bit.

// Power of two: exactly one bit set. O(1)
static boolean isPowerOfTwo(int n) { return n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0; }
// 64 -> true, 96 -> false, 0 -> false, Integer.MIN_VALUE -> false (n > 0 matters!)

static int lowestSetBit(int n) { return n & -n; }    // 12 -> 4

// Brian Kernighan's bit count: loops once per SET bit. O(number of 1s), at most 32
static int countBits(int n) {
    int count = 0;
    while (n != 0) {
        n &= n - 1;        // drop the lowest set bit
        count++;
    }
    return count;
}
// countBits(13) = 3, countBits(-1) = 32  (while n != 0, so negatives work)
// Built-in: Integer.bitCount(13) = 3

Other handy built-ins: Integer.numberOfTrailingZeros(8) = 3, Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(8) = 28, Integer.highestOneBit(100) = 64, Integer.reverse(x), and Long versions of each. Say you would use the built-in in production, and write the loop if asked.

XOR tricks

XOR has three properties that power a surprising number of problems:

  • a ^ a = 0 — a number cancels itself.
  • a ^ 0 = a — zero changes nothing.
  • Order does not matter (commutative and associative) — so you can XOR a whole list in any order and every pair cancels.

Single Number — every value appears twice except one. Find it in O(1) space:

// O(n) time, O(1) space
static int singleNumber(int[] nums) {
    int x = 0;
    for (int v : nums) x ^= v;   // pairs cancel: a ^ a = 0
    return x;
}
// [4, 1, 2, 1, 2] -> 4 ^ (1 ^ 1) ^ (2 ^ 2) = 4 ^ 0 ^ 0 = 4

Missing Number — nums holds n distinct values from 0..n with one missing. XOR every index and every value; everything present appears twice and cancels, leaving the missing one:

// O(n) time, O(1) space. (Sum formula n(n+1)/2 also works but can overflow for huge n.)
static int missingNumber(int[] nums) {
    int x = nums.length;              // include n itself
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) x ^= i ^ nums[i];
    return x;
}
// [3, 0, 1] -> 2

Swap without a temp variable — a party trick; know it, but prefer a temp in real code (it breaks if both refer to the same array slot, because x ^ x = 0):

int a = 3, b = 7;
a ^= b; b ^= a; a ^= b;     // now a = 7, b = 3

Single Number III — two values appear once, everything else twice. XOR of all gives a ^ b. Any 1 bit in that result is a position where a and b differ; split the numbers into two groups by that bit and XOR each group:

// O(n) time, O(1) space
static int[] singleNumberIII(int[] nums) {
    int xor = 0;
    for (int v : nums) xor ^= v;       // = a ^ b, non-zero because a != b
    int diff = xor & -xor;             // lowest bit where a and b differ
    int a = 0, b = 0;
    for (int v : nums) {
        if ((v & diff) == 0) a ^= v;   // group 1
        else b ^= v;                   // group 2
    }
    return new int[]{a, b};
}
// [1, 2, 1, 3, 2, 5] -> [5, 3]

Subsets via bitmask

For n items, each of the numbers 0 to 2ⁿ - 1 is a different pattern of n bits. Read bit i as "is item i in the subset?" and you get every subset exactly once — no recursion needed.

nums = [1, 2, 3] bit 0 -> 1, bit 1 -> 2, bit 2 -> 3 mask binary subset 0 000 [] 1 001 [1] 2 010 [2] 3 011 [1, 2] 4 100 [3] 5 101 [1, 3] 6 110 [2, 3] 7 111 [1, 2, 3]
// O(n * 2^n) time, O(n * 2^n) for the output
static List<List<Integer>> subsets(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {          // 0 .. 2^n - 1
        List<Integer> subset = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) != 0) subset.add(nums[i]); // bit i on -> take nums[i]
        }
        out.add(subset);
    }
    return out;
}
// [1,2,3] -> [[], [1], [2], [1, 2], [3], [1, 3], [2, 3], [1, 2, 3]]

This is also why problems with n ≤ 20 hint at bitmasks: 2²⁰ is about a million states. The same idea powers bitmask DP, where the mask records "which items are already used" (for example, visiting-all-cities problems).

More classic problems

Counting Bits — return the number of 1s for every number 0..n. Reuse earlier answers: i >> 1 is i with its last bit dropped, so its count is already known; add back the last bit.

// O(n) time, O(n) output
static int[] countBitsUpTo(int n) {
    int[] ans = new int[n + 1];
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) ans[i] = ans[i >> 1] + (i & 1);
    return ans;
}
// n = 5 -> [0, 1, 1, 2, 1, 2]

Reverse Bits — mirror all 32 bits. Take the lowest bit of n, push it into the result from the right, repeat 32 times. Use >>> so negative inputs do not drag in sign bits:

// O(32) = O(1) time, O(1) space
static int reverseBits(int n) {
    int result = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 32; i++) {
        result = (result << 1) | (n & 1);  // append n's lowest bit to result
        n >>>= 1;                          // unsigned shift: no sign fill
    }
    return result;
}
// reverseBits(0b1011) = -805306368 = 11010000...0 (binary), same as Integer.reverse(0b1011)

Letter case — in ASCII, upper and lower case letters differ only in bit 5 (value 32, the space character): (char) ('A' | ' ') = 'a' and (char) ('a' & '_') = 'A'. Cute, but Character.toLowerCase is clearer in real code.

Summary table

GoalExpression
Is bit i set?(n & (1 << i)) != 0 or ((n >> i) & 1) == 1
Set / clear / toggle bit in | (1 << i) / n & ~(1 << i) / n ^ (1 << i)
Drop lowest set bitn & (n - 1)
Keep only lowest set bitn & -n
Power of two?n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0
Odd?(n & 1) == 1
Multiply / divide by 2ᵏn << k / n >> k (floor division, also for negatives)
All ones for k bits(1 << k) - 1
Count set bitsInteger.bitCount(n)
When to think "bits" in an interview: the words "appears twice except", "without extra space", "power of two", "subsets" with n ≤ 20, or "without using + or *". Explain the key property out loud (for XOR: "equal numbers cancel"), because bit solutions look like magic unless you say why they work.
Part 2 — Linear Data Structures

6. Arrays & Strings

In plain words

An array is a row of boxes, all the same size, sitting side by side in memory. Each box has a number called its index, starting at 0. Because the boxes are side by side, the computer can jump straight to box number i without looking at the others.

A string is basically an array of characters with some extra rules. In Java, a String can never be changed once it is made.

A row of numbered lockers in a hallway. If you know the locker number, you walk straight to it. But if you want to squeeze a new locker into the middle, everyone after it has to shift one place down.

How an array sits in memory

When you write new int[7], Java asks for one contiguous (unbroken) block of memory big enough for 7 ints. An int is 4 bytes, so that is 28 bytes in a row. To find element i, the machine does one small sum: start address + i × element size. That single sum is why reading a[i] is O(1) no matter how big the array is.

A side benefit: when the CPU reads one element, it pulls the neighbouring bytes into its fast cache too. Walking an array from left to right is therefore much faster in practice than hopping around a linked list, even when both are O(n) on paper.

Static vs dynamic arrays

A plain Java array (int[], String[]) is static: its length is fixed when you create it. An ArrayList is a dynamic array: a static array hidden inside an object that swaps it for a bigger one when it fills up.

ArrayList, capacity 4, size 4: [10][20][30][40] add(50) → no room! 1. allocate a bigger array [ ][ ][ ][ ][ ][ ] (Java: 1.5x, here 6) 2. copy the old elements over [10][20][30][40][ ][ ] O(n), but rare 3. write the new element [10][20][30][40][50][ ] 4. old array becomes garbage

Why is add still called O(1)? Because the expensive copy happens rarely. With doubling, filling an array to n elements copies 1 + 2 + 4 + … + n/2 elements in total, which is less than n. Spread over n adds, that is less than one extra copy per add. This is called amortised O(1): one call can be slow, but the average over many calls is constant. Java's ArrayList grows by 1.5x instead of 2x (saves memory, still amortised O(1)); the first add on a new list allocates capacity 10.

Building a dynamic array from scratch

Interviewers sometimes ask "implement an ArrayList". The whole idea fits in a few lines:

class IntList {
    private int[] data = new int[4];
    private int size = 0;

    void add(int x) {                       // amortised O(1)
        if (size == data.length) {
            data = Arrays.copyOf(data, data.length * 2);  // O(n) copy, rare
        }
        data[size++] = x;
    }
    int get(int i) {                        // O(1)
        if (i < 0 || i >= size) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException(i);
        return data[i];
    }
    void removeAt(int i) {                  // O(n): shift left
        if (i < 0 || i >= size) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException(i);
        System.arraycopy(data, i + 1, data, i, size - i - 1);
        size--;
    }
    int size() { return size; }
}
// Adding 10, 20, ..., 90 makes capacity go 4 4 4 4 8 8 8 8 16

Operations and their cost

Operationint[]ArrayListWhy
Read / write a[i]O(1)O(1)Address arithmetic
Add at endnot possible (fixed size)O(1) amortisedOccasional resize copy
Insert / remove at index iO(n)O(n)Everything after i must shift
Remove last—O(1)Nothing to shift
Search (unsorted)O(n)O(n)Must check each element
Search (sorted)O(log n)O(log n)Binary search
SpaceO(n)O(n)ArrayList may hold up to ~50% spare capacity

2D arrays

In Java, int[][] grid = new int[3][4] is really an array of 3 references, each pointing to its own int[4]. Rows are separate objects, so rows can even have different lengths (a jagged array). grid.length is the number of rows, grid[0].length the number of columns.

int[][] grid = new int[3][4];          // 3 rows, 4 columns, all 0
grid[1][2] = 7;                        // row 1, column 2
int[][] jagged = {{1}, {2, 3}, {4, 5, 6}};
System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(jagged));   // [[1], [2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]

// The four neighbours of (r, c) — you will write this in every grid problem
int[][] dirs = {{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}};
for (int[] d : dirs) {
    int nr = r + d[0], nc = c + d[1];
    if (nr >= 0 && nr < rows && nc >= 0 && nc < cols) { /* visit */ }
}

Loop row by row (for r … for c …), not column by column: each row is contiguous, so row order is cache-friendly.

Strings: immutable, so use StringBuilder

A Java String is immutable: no method ever changes it. Methods like concat, replace and toUpperCase return a new string. This makes strings safe to share and cache (and to use as HashMap keys), but it has a cost: building a string with += in a loop copies the whole string every time, which is O(n²) total.

String s = "hi";
s.concat(" there");          // result thrown away
System.out.println(s);       // hi
s = s.concat(" there");      // reassign to keep it
System.out.println(s);       // hi there

// Build strings in a loop with StringBuilder: append is amortised O(1)
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) sb.append(i).append(',');
sb.setLength(sb.length() - 1);    // drop the last comma
System.out.println(sb.reverse()); // 4,3,2,1,0

// Compare contents with equals, never ==
String x = "java", y = "java", z = new String("java");
System.out.println((x == y) + " " + (x == z) + " " + x.equals(z));  // true false true

x == y is true only because identical string literals share one object in the string pool. new String(...) makes a separate object, so == (which compares references, i.e. "same object?") fails. Always use equals.

String operationCostNote
charAt(i), length()O(1)Backed by an array
substring(a, b)O(b − a)Copies the characters (since Java 7)
equals, indexOf, containsO(n) / O(n·m)Compares character by character
s + tO(n + m)New string every time
toCharArray()O(n)Gives a mutable copy you can sort or swap in
StringBuilder.appendO(1) amortisedDynamic char array inside

Counting characters with int[26]

When the input is only lowercase letters, a 26-slot array beats a HashMap: c - 'a' turns 'a' into 0, 'b' into 1, and so on. It is faster, uses fixed O(1) space, and is less code.

int[] freq = new int[26];
for (char c : "banana".toCharArray()) freq[c - 'a']++;
// freq[0] ('a') = 3, freq[1] ('b') = 1, freq['n' - 'a'] = 2

Use int[128] for any ASCII character, and a HashMap<Character, Integer> for full Unicode.

Common techniques (the toolbox)

TechniqueIdea in one lineTypical signal
Two pointersOne index from each end, or a slow and a fast index, moving toward an answerSorted array, pairs, palindromes, in-place removal
Sliding windowKeep a range [left, right] and grow/shrink it while tracking a running value"Longest / shortest subarray or substring with …"
Prefix sumsprefix[i] = sum of the first i elements, so any range sum is one subtractionMany range-sum queries, "subarray sum equals k"
Frequency countingint[26] or HashMap of countsAnagrams, "most common", duplicates
Reverse tricksReversing parts of an array rearranges it in placeRotation, reverse words
Sort firstO(n log n) sort often turns a hard problem into a two-pointer one3Sum, merge intervals, meeting rooms

Each of these gets its own section in the patterns part of these notes.

When to use what

  • int[] — size known up front, primitives, maximum speed (no boxing: int stays a raw number instead of an Integer object).
  • ArrayList — size grows, you mostly add at the end and read by index. The default list in Java.
  • Not an array — if you insert/remove at the front a lot, use ArrayDeque; if you look things up by value, use a HashSet/HashMap.

Common pitfalls

  • Off-by-one: the last index is length - 1. Loops are i < n, not i <= n.
  • length vs length() vs size(): arrays use the field a.length, strings the method s.length(), collections list.size().
  • Arrays.asList is fixed-size: calling add on it throws UnsupportedOperationException. Wrap it: new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(...)).
  • list.remove(1) on a List<Integer> removes index 1, not the value 1. Use list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) for the value.
  • Printing an array with System.out.println(a) prints something like [I@1b6d3586. Use Arrays.toString(a).
  • Integer overflow when summing or multiplying: use long when values can exceed about 2.1 billion.

Problem: Rotate Array (right by k)

Given [1,2,3,4,5,6,7] and k = 3, produce [5,6,7,1,2,3,4] in place.

void rotate(int[] nums, int k) {
    int n = nums.length;
    k %= n;                         // k can be larger than n
    reverse(nums, 0, n - 1);        // 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 -> 7 6 5 4 3 2 1
    reverse(nums, 0, k - 1);        // -> 5 6 7 | 4 3 2 1
    reverse(nums, k, n - 1);        // -> 5 6 7 | 1 2 3 4
}
void reverse(int[] a, int i, int j) {
    while (i < j) {
        int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[j]; a[j] = t;
        i++; j--;
    }
}

Time O(n) (every element is swapped at most twice). Space O(1). Why it works: reversing the whole array puts the last k elements at the front but backwards; reversing each part separately fixes the order inside each part.

Problem: Product of Array Except Self

Return ans[i] = product of every element except nums[i], without division. [1,2,3,4] gives [24,12,8,6].

int[] productExceptSelf(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] ans = new int[n];
    ans[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {           // ans[i] = product of everything left of i
        ans[i] = ans[i - 1] * nums[i - 1];
    }
    int right = 1;                          // running product of everything right of i
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        ans[i] *= right;
        right *= nums[i];
    }
    return ans;
}
// [1, 2, 3, 4]      -> [24, 12, 8, 6]
// [-1, 1, 0, -3, 3] -> [0, 0, 9, 0, 0]

Time O(n). Space O(1) extra (the output array does not count). Why it works: "everything except me" is exactly "product on my left" times "product on my right", and both can be built in one pass each.

Problem: Longest Common Prefix

String longestCommonPrefix(String[] strs) {
    if (strs.length == 0) return "";
    String first = strs[0];
    for (int i = 0; i < first.length(); i++) {       // column by column
        char c = first.charAt(i);
        for (int j = 1; j < strs.length; j++) {
            if (i == strs[j].length() || strs[j].charAt(i) != c) {
                return first.substring(0, i);
            }
        }
    }
    return first;
}
// ["flower","flow","flight"] -> "fl"
// ["dog","racecar","car"]    -> ""

Time O(S), where S is the total number of characters (in the worst case every character is checked once). Space O(1) apart from the answer. Why it works: scanning column by column stops at the very first position where any string disagrees or runs out, which is exactly where the shared prefix ends.

Problem: Valid Anagram

boolean isAnagram(String s, String t) {
    if (s.length() != t.length()) return false;
    int[] count = new int[26];
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
        count[s.charAt(i) - 'a']++;
        count[t.charAt(i) - 'a']--;
    }
    for (int c : count) if (c != 0) return false;
    return true;
}
// ("anagram", "nagaram") -> true,  ("rat", "car") -> false

Time O(n). Space O(1) (26 counters, whatever n is). Why it works: two strings are anagrams exactly when every letter appears the same number of times, so adding for one string and subtracting for the other must leave all zeros. (Sorting both and comparing also works, in O(n log n).)

Say this in the interview: "Arrays give O(1) access by index because memory is contiguous, but inserting in the middle is O(n) because of shifting. For strings I'll use a StringBuilder in loops since String is immutable, and an int[26] for letter counts."

7. Hashing

In plain words

Hashing turns a key (a word, a number, an object) into a number, and uses that number to pick a slot in an array. A hash table is that array of slots. Because the key tells you directly which slot to look in, finding, adding and removing are O(1) on average — no searching through everything.

In Java you use it through HashMap (key to value) and HashSet (just keys, no duplicates).

A coat check. You hand over your coat and get ticket number 42. When you come back, the attendant does not search every hook; they walk straight to hook 42. The ticket number is the hash.

The three pieces

  1. Hash function — turns a key into an integer. Same key must always give the same number. A good one spreads different keys evenly. In Java this is the key's hashCode() method.
  2. Buckets — the array of slots. The slot for a key is hash % capacity (Java uses hash & (capacity - 1), which is the same thing when capacity is a power of 2, and faster).
  3. Collision handling — two different keys can land in the same bucket. This is not a bug; it is guaranteed to happen (infinitely many possible keys, finitely many buckets). The table needs a plan for it.

Collisions: chaining vs open addressing

Separate chainingOpen addressing
IdeaEach bucket holds a list of entries; colliding keys join the listOne entry per slot; on collision, probe (try) the next slot, then the next, until a free one
Probing styles—Linear (i+1, i+2 …), quadratic (i+1, i+4, i+9 …), double hashing (step size from a 2nd hash)
DeleteEasy: unlink from the listTricky: must leave a "deleted" marker (tombstone) or later probes break
Load factor limitCan go above 1 (just longer chains)Must stay well below 1 (clusters form fast)
Cache friendlinessWorse (pointer chasing)Better (all in one array)
Used byJava HashMapPython dict, Java IdentityHashMap, ThreadLocal
Linear probing, capacity 8. Insert "owl" (hashes to 5) when slot 5 already holds "dog": slot: 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 [ ] [ ] [ ] [bee] [emu] [dog] [ ] [cat] ^ taken → try 6 [ ] [ ] [ ] [bee] [emu] [dog] [owl] [cat] ^ free, store here get("owl"): start at 5, not equal → 6, equal → found. A search stops only at an EMPTY slot, which is why delete needs a tombstone.

Load factor and resizing

The load factor is entries / buckets. As it rises, chains get longer and lookups slow down. So when it passes a threshold (Java: 0.75), the table resizes: it allocates twice as many buckets and re-inserts every entry, because each entry's bucket index depends on the capacity. A resize is O(n), but it happens rarely (each time the size doubles), so put is still amortised O(1) — the same argument as ArrayList growth.

If you know you will store about a million entries, create the map with enough room to skip all the intermediate resizes: new HashMap<>(1_400_000) (expected size / 0.75), or HashMap.newHashMap(1_000_000) on Java 19+.

How Java's HashMap actually works

  • Internally it is an array Node<K,V>[] table, lazily created with 16 buckets on the first put. Capacity is always a power of 2.
  • Hash spreading: it computes h ^ (h >>> 16) so the high bits of hashCode() also influence the bucket (otherwise with 16 buckets only the lowest 4 bits would matter).
  • Bucket index = (capacity - 1) & hash.
  • get: go to the bucket, walk it, and for each node check hash == hash && key.equals(k).
  • Treeification: if one bucket's chain grows past 8 nodes, i.e. when the 9th is added, and the table has at least 64 buckets, that chain is converted into a red-black tree, so a bad bucket costs O(log n) instead of O(n). It turns back into a list if it shrinks to 6. (If the table is smaller than 64, Java resizes instead.) This protects against many keys with colliding hashes, including deliberate attacks.
  • Resize at size > capacity × 0.75, doubling the capacity. Each entry either stays at index i or moves to i + oldCapacity — Java checks one bit to decide.
  • One null key is allowed (it goes to bucket 0). Iteration order is not defined and can change after a resize.
  • Not thread-safe. Use ConcurrentHashMap across threads.

Build a hash map from scratch (separate chaining)

class MyHashMap<K, V> {
    private static class Node<K, V> {
        final K key; V value; Node<K, V> next;
        Node(K key, V value, Node<K, V> next) { this.key = key; this.value = value; this.next = next; }
    }
    private Node<K, V>[] buckets;
    private int size = 0;
    private static final double LOAD_FACTOR = 0.75;

    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    MyHashMap() { buckets = (Node<K, V>[]) new Node[16]; }

    private int indexFor(Object key, int length) {
        int h = (key == null) ? 0 : key.hashCode();
        h ^= (h >>> 16);                  // mix high bits into low bits (like Java)
        return h & (length - 1);          // same as h % length when length is a power of 2
    }

    V get(K key) {                        // O(1) average
        for (Node<K, V> n = buckets[indexFor(key, buckets.length)]; n != null; n = n.next) {
            if (Objects.equals(n.key, key)) return n.value;
        }
        return null;
    }

    void put(K key, V value) {            // O(1) average, amortised
        int i = indexFor(key, buckets.length);
        for (Node<K, V> n = buckets[i]; n != null; n = n.next) {
            if (Objects.equals(n.key, key)) { n.value = value; return; }  // update
        }
        buckets[i] = new Node<>(key, value, buckets[i]);                   // insert at head
        if (++size > buckets.length * LOAD_FACTOR) resize();
    }

    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    private void resize() {               // O(n), but rare -> amortised O(1)
        Node<K, V>[] old = buckets;
        buckets = (Node<K, V>[]) new Node[old.length * 2];
        for (Node<K, V> head : old) {
            for (Node<K, V> n = head; n != null; n = n.next) {
                int i = indexFor(n.key, buckets.length);
                buckets[i] = new Node<>(n.key, n.value, buckets[i]);
            }
        }
    }
}
// After 13 puts, the 13th pushes size past 16 * 0.75 = 12, so capacity becomes 32.

Operations and their cost

OperationAverageWorst (Java 8+)Note
put / get / remove / containsKeyO(1)O(log n)Worst case = everything in one treeified bucket (O(n) before Java 8 or for keys that are not Comparable)
containsValueO(n)O(n)Values are not indexed — scans everything
Iterate all entriesO(n + capacity)O(n + capacity)Visits every bucket, even empty ones
ResizeO(n), amortised O(1) per putAt load factor 0.75
SpaceO(n)Each entry is a node object: much heavier than an array slot

The equals / hashCode contract

HashMap finds a key in two steps: hashCode() picks the bucket, then equals() finds the exact key inside it. So the rule is: if a.equals(b) is true, then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode() must also be true. (The reverse is not required: different keys may share a hash — that is just a collision. For example "Aa".hashCode() and "BB".hashCode() are both 2112.)

Override equals but forget hashCode, and two "equal" objects get different identity-based hash codes, land in different buckets, and never find each other:

class BadPoint {
    final int x, y;
    BadPoint(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof BadPoint p && p.x == x && p.y == y;
    }
    // hashCode NOT overridden -> uses identity hash
}

Set<BadPoint> bad = new HashSet<>();
bad.add(new BadPoint(1, 2));
System.out.println(bad.contains(new BadPoint(1, 2)));   // false  (bug!)

// Fix: a record generates correct equals AND hashCode for you
record Point(int x, int y) {}
Set<Point> good = new HashSet<>();
good.add(new Point(1, 2));
System.out.println(good.contains(new Point(1, 2)));     // true

Without records, write @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); } using the same fields that equals uses.

HashSet, LinkedHashMap, TreeMap

HashSet is literally a HashMap where the set elements are the keys and every value is the same dummy object. Same costs, same rules. set.add(x) returns false if x was already there — handy for duplicate detection.

ClassOrder when iteratingget / putUse when
HashMapNone (looks random)O(1) avgDefault choice
LinkedHashMapInsertion order (or access order)O(1) avgNeed predictable order; LRU cache (access order + removeEldestEntry)
TreeMapSorted by keyO(log n)Need sorted keys, or floor/ceiling/range queries

Handy Map methods that make interview code short: getOrDefault(k, 0), merge(k, 1, Integer::sum) (counting), computeIfAbsent(k, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(v) (grouping), putIfAbsent.

When to use hashing

  • "Have I seen this before?" — HashSet. Turns an O(n) inner search into O(1), so many O(n²) brute forces become O(n).
  • "How many times?" — HashMap<T, Integer> of counts.
  • "Group things by some property" — HashMap<Key, List<T>>.
  • Don't use it when you need order (use TreeMap), or when keys are small integers in a known range (a plain array is faster and simpler).

Common pitfalls

  • Mutable keys: if you change a key object after putting it in, its hash changes and the map can no longer find it. A List key that you add to afterwards makes contains return false, even though the set's size is still 1. Use immutable keys (String, Integer, records).
  • Comparing Integer values with ==: map.get("x") == map.get("y") compares object references. It happens to work for -128 to 127 (Java caches those), and silently fails for 1000. Use equals or unbox to int first.
  • get returns null for a missing key; unboxing that into int throws NullPointerException. Use getOrDefault.
  • Using arrays as keys: int[] uses identity equality. Convert to a String or List<Integer> first.
  • Assuming iteration order of a HashMap. It is not insertion order.

Problem: Two Sum

Return indices of the two numbers that add up to target.

int[] twoSum(int[] nums, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();   // value -> index
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        int need = target - nums[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(need)) return new int[]{seen.get(need), i};
        seen.put(nums[i], i);
    }
    return new int[0];
}
// [2, 7, 11, 15], 9 -> [0, 1]
// [3, 2, 4], 6      -> [1, 2]

Time O(n). Space O(n). Why it works: for each number, its partner (target - x) is fully determined, so instead of searching for it we ask the map "have I already seen it?" in O(1). Checking before inserting stops a number pairing with itself.

Problem: Group Anagrams

List<List<String>> groupAnagrams(String[] strs) {
    Map<String, List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
    for (String s : strs) {
        int[] count = new int[26];
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) count[c - 'a']++;
        String key = Arrays.toString(count);          // e.g. "[1, 0, 0, ...]"
        groups.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(s);
    }
    return new ArrayList<>(groups.values());
}
// ["eat","tea","tan","ate","nat","bat"]
//   -> [bat], [eat, tea, ate], [tan, nat]   (group order may vary)

Time O(n · k), where k is the longest word length (the key has fixed length 26). Space O(n · k). Why it works: all anagrams have identical letter counts, so the count array is a perfect "signature" to group by. (Sorting each word as the key also works: O(n · k log k).)

Problem: Longest Consecutive Sequence

In an unsorted array, find the length of the longest run of consecutive integers, in O(n).

int longestConsecutive(int[] nums) {
    Set<Integer> set = new HashSet<>();
    for (int x : nums) set.add(x);
    int best = 0;
    for (int x : set) {
        if (set.contains(x - 1)) continue;       // not the start of a run
        int len = 1;
        while (set.contains(x + len)) len++;     // walk the run
        best = Math.max(best, len);
    }
    return best;
}
// [100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2]          -> 4   (1, 2, 3, 4)
// [0, 3, 7, 2, 5, 8, 4, 6, 0, 1]  -> 9

Time O(n): the inner while only runs from the start of each run, so every number is walked over at most once in total. Space O(n). Why it works: only counting from numbers that have no left neighbour means each run is measured exactly once, from its first element.

Problem: Subarray Sum Equals K (prefix sums preview)

Count the contiguous subarrays whose sum is exactly k. Numbers can be negative, so a sliding window does not work.

int subarraySum(int[] nums, int k) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> prefixCount = new HashMap<>();
    prefixCount.put(0, 1);                       // empty prefix
    int sum = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : nums) {
        sum += x;
        count += prefixCount.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0);
        prefixCount.merge(sum, 1, Integer::sum);
    }
    return count;
}
// [1, 1, 1], k = 2                  -> 2
// [1, 2, 3], k = 3                  -> 2   ([1,2] and [3])
// [3, 4, 7, 2, -3, 1, 4, 2], k = 7  -> 4

Time O(n). Space O(n). Why it works: the sum of the subarray from j+1 to i is prefix[i] - prefix[j]. That equals k exactly when an earlier prefix equals prefix[i] - k, so we just count how many earlier prefixes had that value. The put(0, 1) covers subarrays that start at index 0.

Say this in the interview: "A HashMap gives O(1) average lookup, O(log n) worst case in Java 8+ because long buckets become red-black trees. I'll use it to trade O(n) extra space for dropping an O(n) inner loop. If I use custom objects as keys, I'll make sure equals and hashCode agree — a record does that for me."

8. Linked Lists

In plain words

A linked list is a chain of small objects called nodes. Each node holds a value and a pointer (in Java: a reference) to the next node. The list only remembers where the first node is — the head. The last node points to null.

Unlike an array, the nodes can be anywhere in memory. That makes adding or removing a node cheap (just redirect a pointer), but finding the 50th node means walking through the first 49.

A treasure hunt. Each clue tells you where the next clue is hidden. Adding a new clue in the middle is easy — just change one clue to point at it. But to reach clue 50 you must follow all the clues before it.

How it sits in memory

Singly, doubly, circular

Singly: head → [1|•] → [2|•] → [3|•] → null one link per node, can only move forward Doubly: null ← [•|1|•] ⇄ [•|2|•] ⇄ [•|3|•] → null prev + next; can walk both ways, remove a known node in O(1) Circular: head → [1|•] → [2|•] → [3|•] ─┐ ↑ │ └────────────────────────────┘ last node points back to the first (round-robin scheduling, ring buffers)

In interviews, "linked list" almost always means singly linked with this node class (the LeetCode one):

class ListNode {
    int val;
    ListNode next;
    ListNode(int val) { this.val = val; }
    ListNode(int val, ListNode next) { this.val = val; this.next = next; }
}

Insert and delete = rewiring pointers

To insert or delete, you need a reference to the node before the spot. Then it is two pointer changes, in the right order:

Insert X after A: A → B Delete B (have A = node before): 1. X.next = A.next A → B, X → B A.next = A.next.next 2. A.next = X A → X → B A → B → C becomes A → C (swap the steps and you lose B forever) B is now unreachable; Java's GC frees it

The dummy head trick: inserting or deleting at the head is a special case, because there is no "node before". Put a fake node (a dummy or sentinel) in front of the head. Now every real node has a node before it, the special case disappears, and at the end you return dummy.next. Use it any time the head might change.

Implement a linked list from scratch

class MyLinkedList {
    private final ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0);   // sentinel before the real head
    private ListNode tail = dummy;
    private int size = 0;

    void addFirst(int val) {                 // O(1)
        dummy.next = new ListNode(val, dummy.next);
        if (tail == dummy) tail = dummy.next;
        size++;
    }
    void addLast(int val) {                  // O(1) thanks to tail pointer
        tail.next = new ListNode(val);
        tail = tail.next;
        size++;
    }
    void addAt(int index, int val) {         // O(n): walk to index-1
        if (index < 0 || index > size) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException(index);
        if (index == size) { addLast(val); return; }
        ListNode prev = dummy;
        for (int i = 0; i < index; i++) prev = prev.next;
        prev.next = new ListNode(val, prev.next);
        size++;
    }
    boolean remove(int val) {                // O(n): find the node BEFORE the target
        for (ListNode prev = dummy; prev.next != null; prev = prev.next) {
            if (prev.next.val == val) {
                if (prev.next == tail) tail = prev;
                prev.next = prev.next.next;  // unlink
                size--;
                return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }
    int indexOf(int val) {                   // O(n)
        int i = 0;
        for (ListNode cur = dummy.next; cur != null; cur = cur.next, i++) {
            if (cur.val == val) return i;
        }
        return -1;
    }
}
// addLast(2), addLast(3), addFirst(1), addAt(3, 4), addAt(1, 9)
//   -> 1 -> 9 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4   (size 5)

With a doubly linked list, if you already hold a node, removing it needs no search at all — this is how an LRU cache removes entries in O(1):

void unlink(DNode node) {          // assumes dummy head and tail, so prev/next are never null
    node.prev.next = node.next;
    node.next.prev = node.prev;
}

Operations and their cost

OperationSinglyDoublyArray (compare)
Access i-th elementO(n)O(n)O(1)
Search by valueO(n)O(n)O(n)
Insert / delete at headO(1)O(1)O(n)
Insert at tailO(1) with tail pointerO(1)O(1) amortised
Delete at tailO(n) (need the node before)O(1)O(1)
Insert / delete after a node you holdO(1)O(1)O(n)
Delete a node you holdO(n) (must find prev)O(1)O(n)
Extra space1 reference per node2 references per nodenone (maybe spare capacity)

Java built-in: LinkedList (and why you rarely want it)

java.util.LinkedList is a doubly linked list that implements both List and Deque: addFirst, addLast, removeFirst, getFirst are all O(1). But its get(i) is O(n), and every element is a separate object, so it is slow and memory-hungry in practice. For a stack or queue, ArrayDeque is faster; for a list, ArrayList. In interviews, linked-list questions give you ListNode and expect you to move pointers yourself.

When to use a linked list

  • You hold references to nodes and must remove/insert them in O(1): LRU cache (HashMap + doubly linked list), adjacency lists, free lists in allocators.
  • You need to splice lists together or split them without copying.
  • Otherwise, prefer an array-based structure — random access and cache locality usually win.

Common pitfalls

  • NullPointerException: check cur != null before cur.next, and fast != null && fast.next != null before fast.next.next.
  • Losing the rest of the list: save next before you overwrite cur.next.
  • Moving head itself while walking, then returning it — use a separate cur variable.
  • Forgetting the head can change — use a dummy node.
  • Creating cycles by accident (for example forgetting to set the new tail's next to null), which makes printing loop forever.
  • Draw it. Three boxes and arrows on paper catch most bugs before you write code.

Problem: Reverse Linked List (iterative and recursive)
