Iterative: walk once, flipping each arrow to point backwards. start: null 1 → 2 → 3 → null prev cur step 1: null ← 1 2 → 3 → null next = 2; 1.next = null prev cur step 2: null ← 1 ← 2 3 → null next = 3; 2.next = 1 prev cur step 3: null ← 1 ← 2 ← 3 null next = null; 3.next = 2 prev cur cur is null → return prev (node 3), the new head
// Iterative: O(n) time, O(1) space
ListNode reverse(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null, cur = head;
    while (cur != null) {
        ListNode next = cur.next;   // 1. save the rest
        cur.next = prev;            // 2. flip the pointer
        prev = cur;                 // 3. step prev forward
        cur = next;                 // 4. step cur forward
    }
    return prev;                    // prev is the new head
}

// Recursive: O(n) time, O(n) space (call stack)
ListNode reverseRec(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    ListNode newHead = reverseRec(head.next);  // reverse the rest
    head.next.next = head;                      // node after me points back to me
    head.next = null;                           // I become the tail
    return newHead;
}
// 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 5  becomes  5 -> 4 -> 3 -> 2 -> 1  (both versions)

Why it works: iteratively, everything left of cur is already reversed and prev is its head, so each step grows the reversed part by one. Recursively, trust the call to reverse the rest; head.next is now the tail of that reversed part, so hang head after it. The recursive version can overflow the stack on very long lists (tens of thousands of nodes).

Problem: Merge Two Sorted Lists

ListNode mergeTwoLists(ListNode a, ListNode b) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    while (a != null && b != null) {
        if (a.val <= b.val) { tail.next = a; a = a.next; }
        else                { tail.next = b; b = b.next; }
        tail = tail.next;
    }
    tail.next = (a != null) ? a : b;   // attach whatever is left
    return dummy.next;
}
// 1->2->4  +  1->3->4  ->  1 -> 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 4

Time O(n + m). Space O(1) — it reuses the existing nodes. Why it works: the smallest remaining element is always at the front of one of the two lists, so taking the smaller front each time builds the merged list in order (the same step as merge sort).

Problem: Remove Nth Node From End

ListNode removeNthFromEnd(ListNode head, int n) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode fast = dummy, slow = dummy;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) fast = fast.next;   // gap of n+1 nodes
    while (fast != null) { fast = fast.next; slow = slow.next; }
    slow.next = slow.next.next;                       // slow is just before the target
    return dummy.next;
}
// 1->2->3->4->5, n = 2  ->  1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 5
// 1, n = 1              ->  null   (the dummy handles removing the head)

Time O(n), one pass. Space O(1). Why it works: if fast starts n+1 steps ahead and both move together, then when fast falls off the end, slow is exactly n+1 from the end — the node just before the one to delete.

Problem: Linked List Cycle (preview of fast and slow pointers)

boolean hasCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) return true;
    }
    return false;
}
// 3 -> 2 -> 0 -> -4 -> (back to 2)  -> true
// 1 -> 2                           -> false

Time O(n). Space O(1) (a HashSet of visited nodes also works but costs O(n)). Why it works: inside a loop, the fast pointer gains one step on the slow one each move, so the gap shrinks by 1 every time and they must meet; with no loop, fast simply reaches null. The fast and slow pointers section covers finding where the cycle starts.

Problem: Add Two Numbers

Digits are stored in reverse order (ones digit first). Return their sum as a list: 342 + 465 = 807 is 2->4->3 + 5->6->4 = 7->0->8.

ListNode addTwoNumbers(ListNode l1, ListNode l2) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    int carry = 0;
    while (l1 != null || l2 != null || carry != 0) {
        int sum = carry;
        if (l1 != null) { sum += l1.val; l1 = l1.next; }
        if (l2 != null) { sum += l2.val; l2 = l2.next; }
        carry = sum / 10;
        tail.next = new ListNode(sum % 10);
        tail = tail.next;
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// 2->4->3 + 5->6->4  ->  7 -> 0 -> 8
// 9->9->9 + 1        ->  0 -> 0 -> 0 -> 1

Time O(max(n, m)). Space O(max(n, m)) for the result list. Why it works: it is school addition, right to left with a carry — and the reversed storage means the list already starts at the rightmost digit. Keeping carry != 0 in the loop condition adds the final extra digit.

Say this in the interview: "I'll use a dummy node so I don't need a special case for the head, and I'll save next before rewiring. Linked lists give O(1) insert/delete once I have the node, but O(n) access by index — so I only reach for one when I need that O(1) splice, like in an LRU cache."

9. Stacks

In plain words

A stack is a collection where you can only add or remove at one end, called the top. The last thing you put in is the first thing you take out: LIFO (Last In, First Out).

Three operations: push (put on top), pop (take off the top), peek (look at the top without removing). All O(1).

A stack of plates. You put clean plates on top and take plates from the top. The plate at the bottom was put there first and will be used last.

Two ways to build one

Array-backed: keep an array and an index top. Push writes at top and moves it up; pop moves it down. Grow the array when full (amortised O(1), like ArrayList). Fast and cache-friendly.

class ArrayStack {
    private int[] data = new int[8];
    private int top = 0;                      // index of the next free slot

    void push(int x) {                        // amortised O(1)
        if (top == data.length) data = Arrays.copyOf(data, top * 2);
        data[top++] = x;
    }
    int pop() {                               // O(1)
        if (top == 0) throw new RuntimeException("stack underflow");
        return data[--top];
    }
    int peek() {
        if (top == 0) throw new RuntimeException("stack is empty");
        return data[top - 1];
    }
    boolean isEmpty() { return top == 0; }
}

Linked: the head of a singly linked list is the top. Push = add a new head, pop = move head forward. Always true O(1) (no resizing), but one object per element.

class LinkedStack {
    private static class Node { int val; Node next; Node(int v, Node n) { val = v; next = n; } }
    private Node head;                        // head IS the top

    void push(int x) { head = new Node(x, head); }        // O(1)
    int pop() {                                            // O(1)
        if (head == null) throw new RuntimeException("stack underflow");
        int v = head.val;
        head = head.next;
        return v;
    }
    int peek() {
        if (head == null) throw new RuntimeException("stack is empty");
        return head.val;
    }
    boolean isEmpty() { return head == null; }
}

Operations and their cost

OperationArray-backedLinked
pushO(1) amortisedO(1)
pop / peekO(1)O(1)
isEmpty / sizeO(1)O(1)
Search for a valueO(n)O(n)
SpaceO(n), compactO(n), plus a node object each

Java built-in: use ArrayDeque, not Stack

The old java.util.Stack class extends Vector: every method is synchronized (slow) and it lets you insert in the middle, which breaks the idea of a stack. The Java docs themselves recommend Deque:

Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
stack.push(1); stack.push(2); stack.push(3);   // push adds at the FRONT of the deque
System.out.println(stack);                     // [3, 2, 1]  (top first)
stack.peek();      // 3  (null if empty)
stack.pop();       // 3  (throws NoSuchElementException if empty)
stack.isEmpty();   // false

Note: ArrayDeque does not allow null elements. peek() returns null on an empty deque, while pop() throws.

The call stack: where you already use a stack

Every time a method is called, the JVM pushes a stack frame (its local variables and where to return to). When the method returns, its frame is popped. That is why recursion works — and why very deep recursion throws StackOverflowError.

fact(3) calls fact(2) calls fact(1): push fact(3) | fact(3) | push fact(2) | fact(2) | ← waiting for fact(1) push fact(1) | fact(1) | ← base case, returns 1 | fact(2) | → 2 * 1 = 2, pop | fact(3) | → 3 * 2 = 6, pop empty result 6

This link matters: any recursive algorithm can be rewritten with your own explicit stack (for example iterative DFS), which avoids stack overflow on deep inputs.

When to use a stack

  • Matching pairs / nesting: brackets, HTML tags, nested k[...] encodings.
  • Undo and back-button history.
  • Expression evaluation: postfix (RPN), infix with operator precedence.
  • DFS without recursion.
  • "Next greater / smaller element" problems — the monotonic stack (a stack kept sorted), covered in its own section.

Common pitfalls

  • Popping an empty stack: always check isEmpty() first — it is often part of the answer (a ) with nothing to match means invalid).
  • Forgetting leftovers: after the loop, a non-empty stack usually means unmatched items.
  • Operand order for - and /: the first pop is the right operand.
  • Mixing deque ends: with ArrayDeque, use push/pop/peek consistently. Mixing in offer/poll (other end) turns it into something else.
  • Storing indices instead of values is often what the problem needs (for distances, like daily temperatures).

Problem: Valid Parentheses

boolean isValid(String s) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(') stack.push(')');           // push the EXPECTED closer
        else if (c == '[') stack.push(']');
        else if (c == '{') stack.push('}');
        else if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != c) return false;
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();                      // leftovers = unclosed openers
}
// "()[]{}" -> true   "([)]" -> false   "{[]}" -> true   "((" -> false

Time O(n). Space O(n). Why it works: the most recently opened bracket must be the first one closed — exactly LIFO. Pushing the expected closer makes the check a single comparison.

Problem: Min Stack

Support push, pop, top and getMin, all in O(1).

class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // {value, minSoFar}

    void push(int val) {
        int min = stack.isEmpty() ? val : Math.min(val, stack.peek()[1]);
        stack.push(new int[]{val, min});
    }
    void pop()   { stack.pop(); }
    int top()    { return stack.peek()[0]; }
    int getMin() { return stack.peek()[1]; }
}
// push(-2), push(0), push(-3): getMin() = -3
// pop(): top() = 0, getMin() = -2

Time O(1) for every operation. Space O(n). Why it works: a stack only ever removes from the top, so the minimum "below and including" each element never changes while that element is there — store it alongside.

Problem: Evaluate Reverse Polish Notation

In RPN (postfix), operators come after their operands: ["2","1","+","3","*"] means (2 + 1) × 3 = 9.

int evalRPN(String[] tokens) {
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (String t : tokens) {
        switch (t) {
            case "+" -> stack.push(stack.pop() + stack.pop());
            case "*" -> stack.push(stack.pop() * stack.pop());
            case "-" -> { int b = stack.pop(), a = stack.pop(); stack.push(a - b); }
            case "/" -> { int b = stack.pop(), a = stack.pop(); stack.push(a / b); }
            default  -> stack.push(Integer.parseInt(t));
        }
    }
    return stack.pop();
}
// ["2","1","+","3","*"]   -> 9
// ["4","13","5","/","+"]  -> 6    (13 / 5 = 2, integer division toward zero)
// ["10","6","9","3","+","-11","*","/","*","17","+","5","+"] -> 22

Time O(n). Space O(n). Why it works: in postfix, an operator always applies to the two most recent unused values, which are exactly the top two of the stack. For - and / the first pop is the right-hand operand.

Problem: Daily Temperatures (monotonic stack preview)

For each day, how many days until a warmer one? 0 if never.

int[] dailyTemperatures(int[] temps) {
    int n = temps.length;
    int[] ans = new int[n];
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // indices, temps strictly decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!stack.isEmpty() && temps[i] > temps[stack.peek()]) {
            int j = stack.pop();                 // day j finally found a warmer day: i
            ans[j] = i - j;
        }
        stack.push(i);
    }
    return ans;                                  // days left on stack keep 0
}
// [73, 74, 75, 71, 69, 72, 76, 73] -> [1, 1, 4, 2, 1, 1, 0, 0]

Time O(n): each index is pushed once and popped at most once. Space O(n). Why it works: the stack holds days still waiting for a warmer day, coldest on top. A new warmer day answers every waiting day colder than it, and those are exactly the ones on top.

Problem: Decode String

"3[a2[c]]" decodes to "accaccacc".

String decodeString(String s) {
    Deque<Integer> counts = new ArrayDeque<>();
    Deque<StringBuilder> outer = new ArrayDeque<>();
    StringBuilder cur = new StringBuilder();
    int k = 0;
    for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
        if (Character.isDigit(c)) {
            k = k * 10 + (c - '0');              // numbers can have many digits
        } else if (c == '[') {
            counts.push(k);                      // save state, start fresh inside
            outer.push(cur);
            cur = new StringBuilder();
            k = 0;
        } else if (c == ']') {
            int times = counts.pop();
            StringBuilder prev = outer.pop();
            prev.append(cur.toString().repeat(times));
            cur = prev;                          // back to the outer level
        } else {
            cur.append(c);
        }
    }
    return cur.toString();
}
// "3[a]2[bc]"     -> "aaabcbc"
// "3[a2[c]]"      -> "accaccacc"
// "2[abc]3[cd]ef" -> "abcabccdcdcdef"
// "10[x]"         -> "xxxxxxxxxx"

Time O(output length), since every output character is built once per nesting level it sits in. Space O(output length). Why it works: [ means "pause what I was building and start a new inner piece", ] means "finish the inner piece and resume the outer one" — pausing and resuming in nested order is exactly what a stack does.

Say this in the interview: "This has nesting / most-recent-first behaviour, so I'll use a stack — ArrayDeque, not the legacy Stack class. Push, pop and peek are all O(1)." If the problem is "next greater element", say "monotonic stack, each element pushed and popped once, so O(n) total".

10. Queues & Deques

In plain words

A queue adds items at the back and removes them from the front. The first item in is the first item out: FIFO (First In, First Out). The two main operations are enqueue (add at the back; Java calls it offer) and dequeue (remove from the front; Java calls it poll).

A deque (say "deck", short for double-ended queue) lets you add and remove at both ends. It can act as a stack, a queue, or both at once.

A queue is the line at a coffee shop: new people join at the back, the person at the front is served first. A deque is a line where staff may also serve or let people in at the back.

offer(A) offer(B) offer(C) poll() → A offer(D) front → [A] front → [A][B][C] ← back [B][C] [B][C][D] ↑ front ↑ front ↑ back

Implementing a queue: the circular array

With a plain array, removing from the front would mean shifting everything left (O(n)). A linked list with head and tail pointers gives O(1) at both ends, but the classic array trick is the circular buffer (ring buffer): keep a head index that moves forward on every dequeue, and let both ends wrap around to index 0 when they pass the end, using % capacity. Nothing ever shifts.

Tracking size separately avoids the classic ambiguity: if you only keep head and tail, then head == tail could mean empty or full.

This is also the answer to Design Circular Queue (LeetCode 622) — the method names below are the ones that problem uses:

class MyCircularQueue {
    private final int[] data;
    private int head = 0;     // index of the front element
    private int size = 0;     // number of elements stored

    MyCircularQueue(int k) { data = new int[k]; }

    boolean enQueue(int value) {                         // O(1)
        if (isFull()) return false;
        int tail = (head + size) % data.length;          // first free slot, wrapping around
        data[tail] = value;
        size++;
        return true;
    }
    boolean deQueue() {                                  // O(1)
        if (isEmpty()) return false;
        head = (head + 1) % data.length;                 // front moves forward, wraps
        size--;
        return true;
    }
    int Front() { return isEmpty() ? -1 : data[head]; }
    int Rear()  { return isEmpty() ? -1 : data[(head + size - 1) % data.length]; }
    boolean isEmpty() { return size == 0; }
    boolean isFull()  { return size == data.length; }
}
// capacity 3: enQueue 1, 2, 3 -> true true true; enQueue(4) -> false (full)
// Rear() = 3; deQueue() -> true; enQueue(4) -> true (wrapped into slot 0)
// Rear() = 4, Front() = 2

Time O(1) for every operation. Space O(k). Why it works: % capacity turns the straight array into a loop, so freed slots at the start are reused instead of shifting elements.

Operations and their cost

OperationCircular array / ArrayDequeLinked listNaive array (shift on dequeue)
Enqueue at backO(1) amortisedO(1)O(1) amortised
Dequeue from frontO(1)O(1)O(n)
Peek frontO(1)O(1)O(1)
Add / remove at front (deque)O(1)O(1) if doubly linkedO(n)
SearchO(n)O(n)O(n)
SpaceO(n)O(n) + node objectsO(n)

Java built-ins: Queue, Deque, ArrayDeque

Queue and Deque are interfaces. ArrayDeque (a growable circular array) is the default implementation for both — faster than LinkedList. Each operation comes in two flavours: one that throws on failure and one that returns a special value (null or false). Interview code normally uses the second.

IntentThrows if empty/fullReturns null/false
Add at backadd(x) / addLastoffer(x) / offerLast
Remove frontremove() / removeFirstpoll() / pollFirst
Look at frontelement() / getFirstpeek() / peekFirst
Add at front (deque)addFirst / pushofferFirst
Remove / look at back (deque)removeLast / getLastpollLast / peekLast
Queue<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer(1); q.offer(2); q.offer(3);
q.peek();   // 1
q.poll();   // 1, queue is now [2, 3]
new ArrayDeque<Integer>().poll();   // null (remove() would throw)

Deque<Integer> d = new ArrayDeque<>();
d.offerFirst(2); d.offerLast(3); d.offerFirst(1);   // [1, 2, 3]
d.pollLast();    // 3
d.peekFirst();   // 1

Other queues you should know exist: PriorityQueue (next section — not FIFO, smallest first), LinkedList (allows null, slower), and BlockingQueue implementations such as ArrayBlockingQueue for producer-consumer threads.

When to use a queue or deque

  • Queue: process things in arrival order — BFS, task scheduling, buffering between a producer and a consumer, rate limiting (a queue of timestamps).
  • Deque: when you need both ends — sliding window max/min (monotonic deque), palindrome checks, 0-1 BFS, "undo with a size limit" (drop from the old end).
  • If you only need one end, it is really a stack — still use ArrayDeque.

Common pitfalls

  • Mixing ends: push adds at the front, offer at the back. Using push + poll gives a stack, not a queue.
  • ArrayDeque rejects null — offer(null) throws NullPointerException.
  • Unchecked poll() returns null; unboxing it to int throws.
  • Using q.size() as a loop bound while adding: in level-order BFS, save the size in a variable before the inner loop.
  • In your own ring buffer, forgetting the % on one of the index updates.

Problem: Implement Queue using Stacks

class MyQueue {
    private final Deque<Integer> in = new ArrayDeque<>();   // new items land here
    private final Deque<Integer> out = new ArrayDeque<>();  // items leave from here

    void push(int x) { in.push(x); }                        // O(1)
    int pop()  { shift(); return out.pop(); }               // amortised O(1)
    int peek() { shift(); return out.peek(); }
    boolean empty() { return in.isEmpty() && out.isEmpty(); }

    private void shift() {
        if (out.isEmpty()) {                                // only refill when empty
            while (!in.isEmpty()) out.push(in.pop());       // reverses the order
        }
    }
}
// push 1, push 2: peek() = 1, pop() = 1; push 3: pop() = 2, pop() = 3, empty() = true
push 1, 2, 3 in: [3 2 1] (3 on top) out: [] pop() → out empty, pour in → out: in: [] out: [1 2 3] (1 on top) → pop 1 push 4 in: [4] out: [2 3] pop() → out not empty → pop 2 (4 waits in "in" until out runs dry)

Time push O(1); pop/peek amortised O(1) — each element moves from in to out exactly once. Space O(n). Why it works: pouring one stack into another reverses the order, turning last-in-first-out into first-in-first-out; only pouring when out is empty keeps older items ahead of newer ones.

Problem: Sliding Window Maximum (monotonic deque)

For every window of size k, report the maximum. [1,3,-1,-3,5,3,6,7], k = 3 gives [3,3,5,5,6,7].

int[] maxSlidingWindow(int[] nums, int k) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] ans = new int[n - k + 1];
    Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();     // indices; their values are decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (!dq.isEmpty() && dq.peekFirst() <= i - k) dq.pollFirst();       // fell out of window
        while (!dq.isEmpty() && nums[dq.peekLast()] <= nums[i]) dq.pollLast(); // useless now
        dq.offerLast(i);
        if (i >= k - 1) ans[i - k + 1] = nums[dq.peekFirst()];              // front = max
    }
    return ans;
}
// [1, 3, -1, -3, 5, 3, 6, 7], k = 3 -> [3, 3, 5, 5, 6, 7]
// [9, 8, 7, 6], k = 2               -> [9, 8, 7]
i num deque (values, front → back) window max 0 1 [1] 1 3 [3] 1 removed: 3 is bigger and newer 2 -1 [3, -1] 3 3 -3 [3, -1, -3] 3 4 5 [5] 3 left the window, -1 and -3 beaten by 5 5 5 3 [5, 3] 5 6 6 [6] 6 7 7 [7] 7

Time O(n): each index enters and leaves the deque at most once. Space O(k). Why it works: a number that is smaller than a newer number can never be a window maximum again (the newer one outlives it), so it is thrown away. What remains is decreasing, so the front is always the max.

Problem: BFS preview — shortest path in a grid

Minimum steps from the top-left to the bottom-right of a grid (0 = open, 1 = wall), moving up/down/left/right.

int shortestPath(int[][] grid) {
    int rows = grid.length, cols = grid[0].length;
    if (grid[0][0] == 1) return -1;
    boolean[][] seen = new boolean[rows][cols];
    Queue<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    q.offer(new int[]{0, 0});
    seen[0][0] = true;
    int[][] dirs = {{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}};
    for (int steps = 0; !q.isEmpty(); steps++) {
        for (int size = q.size(); size > 0; size--) {       // one "ring" (level) at a time
            int[] cell = q.poll();
            if (cell[0] == rows - 1 && cell[1] == cols - 1) return steps;
            for (int[] d : dirs) {
                int r = cell[0] + d[0], c = cell[1] + d[1];
                if (r >= 0 && r < rows && c >= 0 && c < cols && grid[r][c] == 0 && !seen[r][c]) {
                    seen[r][c] = true;                       // mark when ENQUEUED
                    q.offer(new int[]{r, c});
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
// {{0,0,0},{1,1,0},{0,0,0}} -> 4
// {{0,1},{1,0}}             -> -1

Time O(rows × cols). Space O(rows × cols). Why it works: the FIFO queue processes all cells 1 step away, then all cells 2 steps away, and so on, like ripples in water — so the first time we reach the target is by a shortest path. The graph sections cover BFS fully.

Say this in the interview: "I'll use ArrayDeque as the queue — offer to add, poll to remove, both O(1). For a fixed-capacity queue I'd use a circular array with a head index and a size, wrapping with modulo." For sliding-window max/min: "a monotonic deque, O(n) because each index is added and removed once."

11. Heaps & Priority Queues

In plain words

A priority queue is a queue where the item that comes out next is not the oldest, but the one with the highest priority — usually the smallest (or largest) value. A heap is the data structure that makes this fast: getting the smallest item is O(1), and adding or removing one is O(log n).

A min-heap is a tree where every parent is smaller than or equal to its children, so the smallest value is always at the top (the root). A max-heap is the same with "larger". The heap is only partly sorted: it does not care how siblings compare, which is why it can be updated so cheaply.

A hospital emergency room. Patients are not seen in arrival order; the most urgent one goes next. The triage nurse doesn't fully sort the waiting room — they only need to always know who is most urgent right now.

A complete binary tree stored in an array

A heap is a complete binary tree: every level is full except possibly the last, which fills from left to right. Because there are no gaps, the tree fits perfectly in an array, level by level, with no pointers at all. Parent and children are found with index maths:

A complete tree with n nodes has height about log₂ n. Every heap operation walks at most one path from root to leaf, which is where O(log n) comes from.

Sift up (insert) and sift down (remove the top)

  • Insert (offer): put the new value at the end of the array (the next free spot in the bottom level), then sift up: while it is smaller than its parent, swap with the parent.
  • Remove the min (poll): the answer is heap[0]. Move the last element into the root to keep the tree complete, then sift down: while it is bigger than its smaller child, swap with that child.
offer(1) into [3, 5, 8]: append [3, 5, 8, 1] 1 at i=3, parent i=1 holds 5 → swap sift up [3, 1, 8, 5] 1 at i=1, parent i=0 holds 3 → swap sift up [1, 3, 8, 5] 1 at root → stop poll() on [1, 3, 2, 5, 9, 8]: return 1, move last (8) to root [8, 3, 2, 5, 9] sift down children 3 and 2, smaller is 2 → swap [2, 3, 8, 5, 9] 8 at i=2 has no children (2*2+1 = 5 ≥ size 5) → stop

Implement a min-heap from scratch

class MinHeap {
    private int[] heap;
    private int size = 0;

    MinHeap(int capacity) { heap = new int[capacity]; }

    // Build from an existing array in O(n) (heapify)
    MinHeap(int[] values) {
        heap = Arrays.copyOf(values, Math.max(1, values.length));
        size = values.length;
        for (int i = size / 2 - 1; i >= 0; i--) siftDown(i);   // last parent down to root
    }

    void offer(int x) {                                  // O(log n)
        if (size == heap.length) heap = Arrays.copyOf(heap, size * 2);
        heap[size] = x;                                  // put at the end ...
        siftUp(size++);                                  // ... and bubble up
    }
    int peek() {                                         // O(1)
        if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException();
        return heap[0];
    }
    int poll() {                                         // O(log n)
        if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException();
        int min = heap[0];
        heap[0] = heap[--size];                          // move last element to root ...
        siftDown(0);                                     // ... and sink it
        return min;
    }

    private void siftUp(int i) {
        while (i > 0) {
            int parent = (i - 1) / 2;
            if (heap[parent] <= heap[i]) break;          // order is fine
            swap(i, parent);
            i = parent;
        }
    }
    private void siftDown(int i) {
        while (true) {
            int left = 2 * i + 1, right = 2 * i + 2, smallest = i;
            if (left < size && heap[left] < heap[smallest]) smallest = left;
            if (right < size && heap[right] < heap[smallest]) smallest = right;
            if (smallest == i) break;                    // both children bigger: done
            swap(i, smallest);
            i = smallest;
        }
    }
    private void swap(int a, int b) { int t = heap[a]; heap[a] = heap[b]; heap[b] = t; }
}
// offer 5, 3, 8, 1, 9, 2 -> array [1, 3, 2, 5, 9, 8]; polling gives 1 2 3 5 8 9
// new MinHeap(new int[]{9, 4, 7, 1, 8, 2, 3}) -> array [1, 4, 2, 9, 8, 7, 3]

Heapify in O(n), not O(n log n)

Building a heap by calling offer n times costs O(n log n). Heapify is faster: treat the array as a tree already, and call siftDown on every parent from the last one (n/2 - 1) back to the root. Leaves (half of all nodes) need no work.

Why O(n)? A node can only sink as far as the levels below it. Half the nodes are leaves (sink 0), a quarter sit one level up (sink at most 1), an eighth at most 2, and so on: n/4·1 + n/8·2 + n/16·3 + … which adds up to less than n. Most nodes are near the bottom, where sifting down is cheap.

Operations and their cost

OperationBinary heapWhy
peek (min/max)O(1)It is heap[0]
offer (insert)O(log n)Sift up one path
poll (remove top)O(log n)Sift down one path
Build from n items (heapify)O(n)Sum argument above
remove(x) / contains(x) (arbitrary)O(n)Must search; heap is not sorted
Heap sortO(n log n) time, O(1) spacen polls
SpaceO(n)Just the array

Java built-in: PriorityQueue

PriorityQueue is a binary min-heap by default. For a max-heap or custom ordering, pass a Comparator (a rule that says which of two items comes first).

PriorityQueue<Integer> minPq = new PriorityQueue<>(List.of(5, 1, 9));
PriorityQueue<Integer> maxPq = new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.reverseOrder());
maxPq.addAll(List.of(5, 1, 9));
System.out.println(minPq.peek() + " " + maxPq.peek());      // 1 9

// Objects: order by priority, then by name to break ties
record Task(String name, int priority) {}
PriorityQueue<Task> tasks = new PriorityQueue<>(
        Comparator.comparingInt(Task::priority).thenComparing(Task::name));
tasks.offer(new Task("write", 2));
tasks.offer(new Task("deploy", 1));
tasks.offer(new Task("alert", 1));
// polling gives: alert, deploy, write

// int[] pairs: sort by the second element
PriorityQueue<int[]> pairs = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
MethodCostNote
offer / addO(log n)
pollO(log n)Returns null if empty
peekO(1)Returns null if empty
remove(Object), containsO(n)Linear search
new PriorityQueue<>(collection)O(n)Uses heapify
sizeO(1)

Heap sort

Build a max-heap in place (O(n)), then repeatedly swap the root (the largest) to the end of the array and sift the new root down within the shrinking heap. The sorted part grows from the back.

void heapSort(int[] a) {
    int n = a.length;
    for (int i = n / 2 - 1; i >= 0; i--) sink(a, i, n);   // build max-heap: O(n)
    for (int end = n - 1; end > 0; end--) {
        int t = a[0]; a[0] = a[end]; a[end] = t;           // largest goes to the back
        sink(a, 0, end);                                   // fix the shrunk heap
    }
}
void sink(int[] a, int i, int n) {
    while (2 * i + 1 < n) {
        int child = 2 * i + 1;
        if (child + 1 < n && a[child + 1] > a[child]) child++;  // bigger child
        if (a[i] >= a[child]) break;
        int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[child]; a[child] = t;
        i = child;
    }
}
// [5, 1, 9, 3, 7, 2] -> [1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 9]

Time O(n log n) always (no bad worst case like quicksort). Space O(1). Not stable (equal items may swap order), and slower than quicksort in practice because it jumps around the array.

When to use a heap

  • You repeatedly need the current smallest/largest while items keep arriving: scheduling, Dijkstra's shortest path, event simulation.
  • Top K / Kth largest: keep a heap of size k — O(n log k) instead of sorting everything in O(n log n).
  • Merging K sorted things: a heap of the K current fronts.
  • Running median: two heaps.
  • Not for searching a specific value (O(n)), and not when you need everything in sorted order at once (just sort) or need ordered iteration (use TreeMap/TreeSet).

Common pitfalls

  • Iterating or printing a PriorityQueue is not sorted. Offering 5, 1, 4, 2, 3 and printing gives [1, 2, 4, 5, 3] — the raw heap array. Only repeated poll() gives sorted order (1 2 3 4 5).
  • Overflow in comparators: (a, b) -> a - b breaks for large values — Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1 overflows to 2147483647, a positive number, so the order is wrong. Use Integer.compare(a, b) or Comparator.comparingInt.
  • Changing an object's priority while it is inside the heap does not re-sort it. Remove it, change it, re-insert — or insert a new entry and skip stale ones when polled ("lazy deletion", common in Dijkstra).
  • Min vs max confusion for "k largest": you keep a min-heap of size k, so the smallest of the top k sits on top and is easy to evict.
  • PriorityQueue is not thread-safe; use PriorityBlockingQueue across threads.

Problem: Kth Largest Element in an Array

int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
    PriorityQueue<Integer> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>();   // holds the k largest seen
    for (int x : nums) {
        minHeap.offer(x);
        if (minHeap.size() > k) minHeap.poll();               // drop the smallest
    }
    return minHeap.peek();                                    // smallest of the top k
}
// [3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4], k = 2          -> 5
// [3, 2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 5, 5, 6], k = 4 -> 4

Time O(n log k). Space O(k). Why it works: the heap always holds the k largest values seen so far; the smallest of those is, by definition, the kth largest. (Quickselect gets O(n) on average — mention it as a follow-up.)

Problem: Merge K Sorted Lists

ListNode mergeKLists(ListNode[] lists) {
    PriorityQueue<ListNode> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a.val, b.val));
    for (ListNode head : lists) if (head != null) pq.offer(head);
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        ListNode node = pq.poll();            // smallest head among all lists
        tail.next = node;
        tail = node;
        if (node.next != null) pq.offer(node.next);
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1->4->5, 1->3->4, 2->6] -> 1 1 2 3 4 4 5 6

Time O(N log k), where N is the total number of nodes and k the number of lists. Space O(k) for the heap. Why it works: the next node of the merged list must be the smallest of the k current fronts; the heap gives that in O(log k), and when a front is used its successor takes its place.

Problem: Top K Frequent Elements

int[] topKFrequent(int[] nums, int k) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
    for (int x : nums) freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);
    // min-heap by frequency, size k
    PriorityQueue<Map.Entry<Integer, Integer>> pq =
            new PriorityQueue<>(Map.Entry.comparingByValue());
    for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : freq.entrySet()) {
        pq.offer(e);
        if (pq.size() > k) pq.poll();
    }
    int[] ans = new int[k];
    for (int i = k - 1; i >= 0; i--) ans[i] = pq.poll().getKey();   // most frequent first
    return ans;
}
// [1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3], k = 2 -> [1, 2]

Time O(n log k): counting is O(n), then each of up to n distinct values costs O(log k). Space O(n) for the counts. Why it works: the same "keep the k best in a min-heap" idea as kth largest, just ranked by count instead of value. (Bucket sort by frequency gets O(n).)

Problem: Find Median from Data Stream (two heaps preview)

class MedianFinder {
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> low = new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.reverseOrder()); // max-heap
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> high = new PriorityQueue<>();                          // min-heap

    void addNum(int num) {                         // O(log n)
        low.offer(num);
        high.offer(low.poll());                    // largest of low moves to high
        if (high.size() > low.size()) low.offer(high.poll());  // keep low >= high in size
    }
    double findMedian() {                          // O(1)
        if (low.size() > high.size()) return low.peek();
        return (low.peek() + high.peek()) / 2.0;
    }
}
// addNum(1), addNum(2): findMedian() = 1.5
// addNum(3):            findMedian() = 2.0
low (max-heap) high (min-heap) smaller half larger half [ ... 1 2 ] top = 2 | top = 3 [ 3 ... ] ↑ the median lives at one or both of these tops

Time addNum O(log n), findMedian O(1). Space O(n). Why it works: every number in low is ≤ every number in high, and the sizes differ by at most one, so the middle value(s) are exactly the two tops. The two-heaps section goes further.

Say this in the interview: "A heap is a complete binary tree stored in an array: peek is O(1), offer and poll are O(log n), and building one from n items is O(n). In Java I'll use PriorityQueue, which is a min-heap; for a max-heap I pass Collections.reverseOrder(). For top-k I keep a min-heap of size k, which gives O(n log k)."
Part 3 — Trees & Graphs

12. Binary Trees

In plain words

A tree is a set of nodes joined by links, where every node except the top one has exactly one parent, and there are no loops. A binary tree is a tree where each node has at most two children, called left and right.

Almost every tree problem is solved the same way: do something at this node, and ask the left and right subtrees to solve the same smaller problem (recursion).

A family tree drawn upside down. The great-grandparent sits at the top, each person points to their (at most two) children, and nobody is their own ancestor.

Terminology

TermMeaning
RootThe top node; it has no parent.
LeafA node with no children.
Internal nodeAny node that is not a leaf.
SubtreeA node plus everything below it. Every node is the root of its own subtree.
Depth of a nodeNumber of edges from the root down to it. Root has depth 0.
Height of a nodeNumber of edges on the longest path from it down to a leaf. A leaf has height 0. The tree's height is the root's height. (LeetCode's "max depth" counts nodes instead, so it is height + 1.)
FullEvery node has 0 or 2 children — never exactly 1.
CompleteEvery level is full except maybe the last, and the last is filled from the left. Heaps are complete trees, which is why they fit in an array.
PerfectAll internal nodes have 2 children and all leaves are on the same level. Has exactly 2h+1 − 1 nodes.
BalancedFor every node, the heights of the left and right subtrees differ by at most 1. Height stays O(log n).
Degenerate (skewed)Every node has one child — really a linked list. Height is n − 1.

The node class

class TreeNode {
    int val;
    TreeNode left, right;
    TreeNode(int val) { this.val = val; }
    TreeNode(int val, TreeNode left, TreeNode right) {
        this.val = val; this.left = left; this.right = right;
    }
}

In memory each node is a separate object on the heap holding a value and two references. There is no array and no index — you can only reach a node by walking down from the root. That is why "find a value" in a plain binary tree is O(n): you may have to visit every node.

Traversals — the four ways to visit every node

The name tells you when the root is visited compared to its children:

  • Preorder (root, left, right) — visit the node before its children. Use it to copy or serialize a tree: you meet the parent before you need to build its children.
  • Inorder (left, root, right) — on a Binary Search Tree this gives values in sorted order.
  • Postorder (left, right, root) — children finish before the parent. Use it when a node needs answers from its children (height, diameter, deleting a tree).
  • Level order (BFS) — row by row, top to bottom. Use it for "per level" questions and shortest distance from the root.
void preorder(TreeNode node, List<Integer> out) {
    if (node == null) return;
    out.add(node.val);            // root
    preorder(node.left, out);     // left
    preorder(node.right, out);    // right
}
void inorder(TreeNode node, List<Integer> out) {
    if (node == null) return;
    inorder(node.left, out);      // left
    out.add(node.val);            // root
    inorder(node.right, out);     // right
}
void postorder(TreeNode node, List<Integer> out) {
    if (node == null) return;
    postorder(node.left, out);    // left
    postorder(node.right, out);   // right
    out.add(node.val);            // root
}

All three are O(n) time (each node visited once) and O(h) extra space for the call stack, where h is the height: O(log n) for a balanced tree, O(n) for a skewed one. A skewed tree with 100,000 nodes can throw StackOverflowError in Java, which is the main reason interviewers ask for iterative versions.

Iterative traversals with an explicit stack

Recursion uses the call stack for you. Iterative code just makes that stack visible, using ArrayDeque (never the old Stack class, which is synchronized and slow).

// Preorder: pop, visit, push right then left (so left is processed first)
List<Integer> preorderIter(TreeNode root) {
    List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return out;
    Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    stack.push(root);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        TreeNode node = stack.pop();
        out.add(node.val);
        if (node.right != null) stack.push(node.right); // push right first
        if (node.left != null) stack.push(node.left);   // so left pops first
    }
    return out;
}

// Inorder: slide left pushing nodes, pop one, visit, then go right
List<Integer> inorderIter(TreeNode root) {
    List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
    Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null || !stack.isEmpty()) {
        while (cur != null) {          // go as far left as possible
            stack.push(cur);
            cur = cur.left;
        }
        cur = stack.pop();             // leftmost unvisited node
        out.add(cur.val);
        cur = cur.right;               // then its right subtree
    }
    return out;
}

// Postorder: do "root, right, left" and reverse it -> "left, right, root"
List<Integer> postorderIter(TreeNode root) {
    LinkedList<Integer> out = new LinkedList<>();
    if (root == null) return out;
    Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    stack.push(root);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        TreeNode node = stack.pop();
        out.addFirst(node.val);        // prepend = reverse for free
        if (node.left != null) stack.push(node.left);
        if (node.right != null) stack.push(node.right);
    }
    return out;
}

Level order with a queue

List<List<Integer>> levelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> levels = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return levels;
    Queue<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();             // nodes on this level
        List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>(size);
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            level.add(node.val);
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        levels.add(level);
    }
    return levels;   // sample tree: [[1], [2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]
}

The trick is int size = queue.size() before the inner loop: it freezes how many nodes belong to the current level, even while you add the next level's nodes to the back. Time O(n), space O(w) where w is the widest level (up to about n/2 for the last level of a perfect tree).

Morris traversal (O(1) extra space)

Morris inorder avoids both recursion and a stack. For each node with a left subtree, it finds the inorder predecessor (the rightmost node of the left subtree) and temporarily points that node's right back up to the current node — a "thread". Following the thread later is how it climbs back up without a stack. On the second visit it removes the thread, so the tree is restored.

List<Integer> morrisInorder(TreeNode root) {
    List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null) {
        if (cur.left == null) {
            out.add(cur.val);
            cur = cur.right;
        } else {
            TreeNode pred = cur.left;            // find inorder predecessor
            while (pred.right != null && pred.right != cur) pred = pred.right;
            if (pred.right == null) {            // first visit: build thread
                pred.right = cur;
                cur = cur.left;
            } else {                             // second visit: remove thread
                pred.right = null;
                out.add(cur.val);
                cur = cur.right;
            }
        }
    }
    return out;
}

Time O(n) (each edge is walked at most a few times), space O(1). It is rarely required, but mentioning it when asked "can you do it without extra space?" scores points. Downside: it modifies the tree while running, so it is not safe with concurrent readers.

Operations complexity (plain binary tree, n nodes, height h)

OperationTimeExtra spaceWhy
Search for a valueO(n)O(h)No ordering, so you may check every node
Insert (at a known spot)O(1) once thereO(1)Just set a left/right reference
Pre / in / post-orderO(n)O(h)Stack holds one root-to-node path
Level orderO(n)O(w)Queue holds up to one full level
Morris inorderO(n)O(1)Uses temporary threads instead of a stack
Height / size / count leavesO(n)O(h)Postorder: combine children's answers

Java built-ins: Java has no general binary tree class. TreeMap/TreeSet are balanced binary search trees (section 14) and PriorityQueue is a binary heap in an array. In interviews you write TreeNode yourself.

Problem 1 — Maximum depth

// Number of nodes on the longest root-to-leaf path
int maxDepth(TreeNode root) {
    if (root == null) return 0;
    return 1 + Math.max(maxDepth(root.left), maxDepth(root.right));
}   // sample tree: 3

Why it works: the depth of a tree is one (for this node) plus the deeper of its two subtrees. The empty tree has depth 0, which stops the recursion. O(n) time, O(h) space.

Problem 2 — Invert a binary tree

TreeNode invertTree(TreeNode root) {
    if (root == null) return null;
    TreeNode tmp = root.left;
    root.left = invertTree(root.right);
    root.right = invertTree(tmp);
    return root;
}   // sample tree level order becomes [[1], [3, 2], [6, 5, 4]]

Why it works: a mirror image is "swap my two children, and mirror each of them". Save left in tmp first, or you would overwrite it before using it. O(n) time, O(h) space.

Problem 3 — Diameter of a binary tree

The diameter is the number of edges on the longest path between any two nodes. That path may not go through the root.

class Solution {
    private int best = 0;
    public int diameterOfBinaryTree(TreeNode root) {
        height(root);
        return best;
    }
    // returns height in nodes; updates best with the longest path through node
    private int height(TreeNode node) {
        if (node == null) return 0;
        int l = height(node.left);
        int r = height(node.right);
        best = Math.max(best, l + r);   // path bends at this node
        return 1 + Math.max(l, r);
    }
}   // sample tree: 4 (path 4-2-1-3-6)

Why it works: every path has one highest node where it "bends". The longest path bending at a node is (height of left) + (height of right) edges. Postorder computes both heights once, so one pass checks every bend point. O(n) time instead of the naive O(n2) that recomputes heights.

Pattern — "return one thing, track another": the helper returns what the parent needs (height), while a field tracks the global answer (best path). The same shape solves Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum and Longest Univalue Path.

Problem 4 — Lowest common ancestor (LCA)

The LCA of p and q is the deepest node that has both p and q in its subtree (a node counts as its own descendant).

TreeNode lowestCommonAncestor(TreeNode root, TreeNode p, TreeNode q) {
    if (root == null || root == p || root == q) return root;
    TreeNode left = lowestCommonAncestor(root.left, p, q);
    TreeNode right = lowestCommonAncestor(root.right, p, q);
    if (left != null && right != null) return root; // p and q split here
    return left != null ? left : right;             // both on one side
}   // sample: lca(4,5)=2, lca(4,6)=1, lca(2,4)=2

Why it works: each call reports "I found p or q (or their LCA) in my subtree" by returning a non-null node. The first node where both sides report something is where the paths to p and q split — the LCA. If a node is p itself, returning it right away is correct even when q is below it, because then p is the LCA. O(n) time, O(h) space.

Problem 5 — Serialize and deserialize

public class Codec {
    public String serialize(TreeNode root) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        write(root, sb);
        return sb.toString();
    }
    private void write(TreeNode node, StringBuilder sb) {
        if (node == null) { sb.append("#,"); return; }   // mark nulls
        sb.append(node.val).append(',');
        write(node.left, sb);
        write(node.right, sb);
    }
    public TreeNode deserialize(String data) {
        Deque<String> tokens = new ArrayDeque<>(Arrays.asList(data.split(",")));
        return read(tokens);
    }
    private TreeNode read(Deque<String> tokens) {
        String t = tokens.poll();
        if (t.equals("#")) return null;
        TreeNode node = new TreeNode(Integer.parseInt(t));
        node.left = read(tokens);      // same order as write: root, left, right
        node.right = read(tokens);
        return node;
    }
}   // sample tree -> "1,2,4,#,#,5,#,#,3,#,6,#,#,"

Why it works: preorder alone cannot rebuild a tree (many shapes share the same order), but preorder with null markers can: the markers tell the reader exactly where each subtree ends. Reading consumes tokens in the same root-left-right order they were written. O(n) time and space for both directions.

Pitfalls

  • Forgetting the null base case — nearly every tree bug is a NullPointerException.
  • Mixing up depth (counted from the top) and height (counted from the bottom), or edges vs nodes. Say which one you mean.
  • Recomputing heights inside a recursion (O(n2)). Return the height from the same pass instead.
  • Deep recursion on skewed input — offer the iterative version.
  • Using a static field for the answer and forgetting to reset it between test cases. Prefer an instance field or an int[] holder.

13. Binary Search Trees

In plain words

A Binary Search Tree (BST) is a binary tree with one rule at every node: everything in the left subtree is smaller, everything in the right subtree is larger. Because of that rule, you can find a value by going left or right at each step and throwing away half of what remains — like binary search, but on linked nodes.

The rule is about whole subtrees, not just direct children. That detail is the source of the most common BST interview bug.

Playing "guess my number, higher or lower". Each answer rules out a whole range, so you never check the numbers on the wrong side.

Search and insert

TreeNode search(TreeNode root, int key) {
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null && cur.val != key) {
        cur = key < cur.val ? cur.left : cur.right;   // discard half
    }
    return cur;   // null if not found
}

TreeNode insert(TreeNode root, int key) {
    if (root == null) return new TreeNode(key);       // empty spot found
    if (key < root.val) root.left = insert(root.left, key);
    else if (key > root.val) root.right = insert(root.right, key);
    return root;                                      // duplicates ignored
}

Insert walks down exactly like search. When it falls off the tree (reaches null), that empty spot is where the new key belongs. The pattern root.left = insert(root.left, key) — "rebuild the link on the way back up" — is worth memorising: delete uses it too. Both are O(h) time.

Delete — the three cases

  1. Leaf (no children): just remove it — return null to the parent.
  2. One child: the child takes the node's place — return the child to the parent.
  3. Two children: find the inorder successor (the smallest value in the right subtree — go right once, then left as far as possible). Copy its value into this node, then delete the successor from the right subtree. The successor has no left child, so that second delete is always case 1 or 2.
TreeNode delete(TreeNode root, int key) {
    if (root == null) return null;
    if (key < root.val) {
        root.left = delete(root.left, key);
    } else if (key > root.val) {
        root.right = delete(root.right, key);
    } else {
        // Case 1 and 2: zero or one child -> replace node by the other side
        if (root.left == null) return root.right;
        if (root.right == null) return root.left;
        // Case 3: two children -> copy inorder successor, delete it from right
        TreeNode succ = root.right;
        while (succ.left != null) succ = succ.left;   // smallest in right subtree
        root.val = succ.val;
        root.right = delete(root.right, succ.val);
    }
    return root;
}

Notice the leaf case needs no special code: if left is null we return right, which is also null for a leaf. Time O(h). (Using the inorder predecessor — largest value on the left — works equally well.)

Inorder = sorted

Inorder visits left subtree, node, right subtree. In a BST that is "everything smaller, me, everything larger" at every level, so the output is sorted. This one fact solves a family of problems: validate a BST (the inorder must be strictly increasing), kth smallest (stop at the kth visit), two-sum in a BST, recover a BST with two swapped nodes (find the two places where the order breaks).

Operations complexity

OperationBalanced (h = log n)Degenerate (h = n)Space
SearchO(log n)O(n)O(1) iterative
InsertO(log n)O(n)O(h) recursive
DeleteO(log n)O(n)O(h) recursive
Min / max (leftmost / rightmost)O(log n)O(n)O(1)
Successor / predecessorO(log n)O(n)O(1)
Sorted listing (inorder)O(n)O(n)O(h)

Degenerate BST — why balancing matters

A plain BST has no control over its shape: the shape depends on insert order. Insert 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 in order and every node goes to the right of the last one:

sorted inserts 1..7 same keys, balanced 1 4 \ / \ 2 2 6 \ / \ / \ 3 height 7 1 3 5 7 height 3 \ (a linked list) ...7

Now search is O(n), no better than a list. Real data is often sorted or nearly sorted (timestamps, auto-increment IDs), so this is not a rare edge case. Self-balancing trees (AVL, red-black — next section) fix the shape after every insert and delete, guaranteeing O(log n). That is what Java's TreeMap uses.

Problem 1 — Validate a BST (min/max bounds)

boolean isValidBST(TreeNode root) {
    return valid(root, Long.MIN_VALUE, Long.MAX_VALUE);
}
// every value in this subtree must be strictly between low and high
boolean valid(TreeNode node, long low, long high) {
    if (node == null) return true;
    if (node.val <= low || node.val >= high) return false;
    return valid(node.left, low, node.val)       // left: tighten upper bound
        && valid(node.right, node.val, high);    // right: tighten lower bound
}

Why it works: checking only left.val < node.val < right.val is the classic wrong answer. The tree 5 → right 6 → left 4 passes that local check, but 4 sits in the right subtree of 5, so it is invalid (our code returns false). Passing down the allowed range catches it: when you go left, the current value becomes the new upper limit; going right, it becomes the new lower limit. long bounds make a node holding Integer.MAX_VALUE work. O(n) time, O(h) space.

Problem 2 — Kth smallest element

int kthSmallest(TreeNode root, int k) {
    Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null || !stack.isEmpty()) {
        while (cur != null) { stack.push(cur); cur = cur.left; }
        cur = stack.pop();
        if (--k == 0) return cur.val;     // k-th node in sorted order
        cur = cur.right;
    }
    throw new IllegalArgumentException("k is larger than the tree");
}   // sample BST, k = 3 -> 4

Why it works: iterative inorder produces values in sorted order, so the kth pop is the kth smallest. It stops early: O(h + k) time, O(h) space. Follow-up "the tree changes often and kth is asked often": store a subtree size in each node, then pick left or right by comparing k to the left size — O(h) per query.

Problem 3 — Lowest common ancestor in a BST

TreeNode lcaBST(TreeNode root, TreeNode p, TreeNode q) {
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null) {
        if (p.val < cur.val && q.val < cur.val) cur = cur.left;        // both left
        else if (p.val > cur.val && q.val > cur.val) cur = cur.right;  // both right
        else return cur;                                               // they split here
    }
    return null;
}   // sample BST: lca(4,7)=6, lca(1,7)=3, lca(13,10)=10

Why it works: while both values are on the same side, the LCA must be on that side too. The first node where they split (or one of them equals the node) is the LCA. Uses the ordering, so it is O(h) time and O(1) space — better than the O(n) general-tree version in section 12.

Problem 4 — Convert a sorted array to a balanced BST

TreeNode sortedArrayToBST(int[] nums) {
    return build(nums, 0, nums.length - 1);
}
TreeNode build(int[] nums, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo > hi) return null;
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;          // middle element becomes the root
    TreeNode node = new TreeNode(nums[mid]);
    node.left = build(nums, lo, mid - 1);
    node.right = build(nums, mid + 1, hi);
    return node;
}   // [1..7] -> root 4, height 3 (in nodes)

Why it works: picking the middle puts half the values on each side, so the two subtree sizes differ by at most one at every level — the tree is height-balanced. Everything left of mid is smaller, everything right is larger, so the BST rule holds. O(n) time, O(log n) recursion space.

When to use a BST, and pitfalls

  • Use an (always balanced) BST when you need order: sorted iteration, min/max, "next larger than x", range queries. If you only need lookup by exact key, a HashMap is faster (O(1)).
  • Pitfall: validating with parent-child comparisons only (see Problem 1).
  • Pitfall: forgetting to assign the return value — insert(root.left, key) without root.left = loses the new node.
  • Pitfall: duplicates. Decide a rule up front (ignore, count field, or always go right) and say it out loud.
  • Pitfall: assuming O(log n) for a plain BST. It is O(h), and h can be n.

14. Balanced Trees & TreeMap

In plain words

A self-balancing BST is a binary search tree that repairs its own shape after every insert or delete, so its height always stays around log n. That turns the BST's "O(h), could be O(n)" promise into a guaranteed O(log n).

You will almost never code one in an interview. What you will use is Java's TreeMap and TreeSet, which are red-black trees under the hood, and their "find the nearest key" methods.

A bookshelf that re-spaces itself. Whenever one end gets crowded, it shifts a few books over so you can always find any title in a handful of glances.

Why balance matters

Every BST operation costs O(height). With n = 1,000,000 keys a balanced tree has height about 20; a degenerate one has height 1,000,000. Balanced trees keep a small invariant (a rule that is always true) that forces the height to stay O(log n), and they restore the rule after each change using rotations.

Rotations — the one move every balanced tree uses

A rotation changes which node is the parent in a small group of nodes, without breaking the BST order. In a right rotation, the left child x moves up and its old parent y moves down to the right. The middle subtree B (values between x and y) switches from x's right to y's left. Only three links change, so a rotation is O(1).

AVL trees in easy words

An AVL tree (named after its inventors Adelson-Velsky and Landis) stores a height in each node and keeps this rule: at every node, the left and right subtree heights differ by at most 1. The difference is called the balance factor. After an insert, walk back up; the first node whose balance factor becomes +2 or −2 is fixed with one of four cases:

CaseShapeFix
LLToo tall on the left, new key went into the left child's leftOne right rotation
RRMirror of LLOne left rotation
LRToo tall on the left, new key went into the left child's right (a zig-zag)Left-rotate the child (turns it into LL), then right-rotate the node
RLMirror of LRRight-rotate the child, then left-rotate the node
class Node {
    int key, height = 1;
    Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; }
}
int h(Node n) { return n == null ? 0 : n.height; }
void update(Node n) { n.height = 1 + Math.max(h(n.left), h(n.right)); }
int balance(Node n) { return h(n.left) - h(n.right); }

Node rotateRight(Node y) {
    Node x = y.left;
    Node b = x.right;
    x.right = y;          // y comes down to the right
    y.left = b;           // B changes parent (still between x and y)
    update(y);            // y is now lower: update it first
    update(x);
    return x;             // new root of this subtree
}
Node rotateLeft(Node x) {
    Node y = x.right;
    Node b = y.left;
    y.left = x;
    x.right = b;
    update(x);
    update(y);
    return y;
}

Node insert(Node node, int key) {
    if (node == null) return new Node(key);
    if (key < node.key) node.left = insert(node.left, key);
    else if (key > node.key) node.right = insert(node.right, key);
    else return node;
    update(node);
    int bal = balance(node);
    if (bal > 1 && key < node.left.key) return rotateRight(node);             // LL
    if (bal < -1 && key > node.right.key) return rotateLeft(node);            // RR
    if (bal > 1) { node.left = rotateLeft(node.left); return rotateRight(node); }   // LR
    if (bal < -1) { node.right = rotateRight(node.right); return rotateLeft(node); } // RL
    return node;
}   // inserting 1..7 in order gives root 4, height 3 (not 7)

Insert is O(log n): one walk down, one walk up, at most two rotations. You are unlikely to be asked for this code, but "explain how AVL fixes itself" is a fair question.

Red-black trees in easy words

A red-black tree paints each node red or black and keeps these rules:

  1. Every node is red or black; the root is black.
  2. Null leaves count as black.
  3. No red node has a red child (no two reds in a row).
  4. Every path from a node down to a null leaf passes the same number of black nodes (the "black height").

Why that keeps it balanced: the shortest possible path is all black; the longest alternates red and black. Rule 4 says both have the same number of blacks, and rule 3 says reds cannot pile up, so the longest path is at most twice the shortest. Height is at most 2 log2(n + 1). On insert the new node is red; if that breaks rule 3, the tree fixes it by recolouring and at most two rotations.

AVLRed-black
BalanceStrict (heights differ by at most 1)Looser (longest path at most 2x shortest)
LookupsSlightly faster (shorter tree)Slightly slower
Inserts / deletesMore rotationsFewer rotations (at most 2 per insert, 3 per delete)
Used byRead-heavy in-memory indexesJava TreeMap/TreeSet, HashMap buckets with 8+ entries, C++ std::map, Linux scheduler

B-trees — why databases use them

A B-tree is a balanced search tree where each node holds many sorted keys (often hundreds) and has one more child than keys. All leaves sit at the same depth. A B+ tree, used by MySQL InnoDB and PostgreSQL indexes, keeps all real data in the leaves and links the leaves together for fast range scans.

The reason is the disk. Reading from disk or SSD happens in pages (for example 16 KB in InnoDB), and each page read is thousands of times slower than a memory access. A binary tree with a billion keys is about 30 levels deep — 30 page reads. A B-tree with ~500 keys per node reaches a billion keys in about 4 levels, and the top levels stay cached in memory, so a lookup often needs just 1–2 real disk reads. Same O(log n) in theory; a much smaller log base in practice.

Java TreeMap and TreeSet API

TreeMap<K,V> keeps keys sorted (natural order or a Comparator). TreeSet<E> is the same thing with only keys. Both implement NavigableMap/NavigableSet, which adds the "nearest key" methods that make them useful.

TreeMap<Integer, String> map = new TreeMap<>();
map.put(10, "a"); map.put(20, "b"); map.put(30, "c"); map.put(40, "d");

map.floorKey(25);      // 20   largest key <= 25
map.ceilingKey(25);    // 30   smallest key >= 25
map.floorKey(20);      // 20   "or equal" methods include the key itself
map.lowerKey(20);      // 10   strictly less
map.higherKey(20);     // 30   strictly greater
map.floorKey(5);       // null nothing <= 5, always null-check
map.firstKey();        // 10
map.lastKey();         // 40
map.headMap(30);       // {10=a, 20=b}         keys < 30
map.tailMap(30);       // {30=c, 40=d}         keys >= 30
map.subMap(15, 35);    // {20=b, 30=c}         15 <= key < 35
map.subMap(20, true, 40, true);  // {20=b, 30=c, 40=d}  both ends inclusive
map.descendingMap();   // {40=d, 30=c, 20=b, 10=a}
map.pollFirstEntry();  // removes and returns 10=a

TreeSet<Integer> set = new TreeSet<>(List.of(5, 1, 9, 3));   // [1, 3, 5, 9]
set.floor(4);          // 3
set.higher(5);         // 9
set.pollFirst();       // 1 (removed)
set.pollLast();        // 9 (removed) -> set is now [3, 5]

Memory aid: floor/ceiling include equal, lower/higher do not. Methods ending in Key return just the key; the Entry versions (floorEntry, ceilingEntry) return key and value. headMap/subMap return live views, not copies, and cost O(log n) to create.

Operations complexity

OperationTreeMap / TreeSetHashMap / HashSet
get / put / remove / containsO(log n)O(1) average
first / last / pollFirst / pollLastO(log n)O(n) — no order
floor / ceiling / lower / higherO(log n)not supported
Iterate all in sorted orderO(n)O(n log n) — must sort
Range view (subMap) then iterate k itemsO(log n + k)O(n)
SpaceO(n), ~40 bytes per entry overheadO(n)

When to use: reach for TreeMap when a problem says "closest", "next bigger", "previous smaller", "in a range", "sorted while changing", or needs both min and max with deletes of arbitrary elements (a heap cannot remove an arbitrary element fast; a TreeMap of counts can).

Problem 1 — My Calendar I (booking overlap)

Book an event [start, end) only if it does not overlap an existing booking.

class MyCalendar {
    private final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> bookings = new TreeMap<>(); // start -> end

    public boolean book(int start, int end) {           // half-open [start, end)
        Integer prevStart = bookings.floorKey(start);    // latest booking starting at or before start
        if (prevStart != null && bookings.get(prevStart) > start) return false;
        Integer nextStart = bookings.ceilingKey(start);  // earliest booking starting at or after start
        if (nextStart != null && nextStart < end) return false;
        bookings.put(start, end);
        return true;
    }
}
// book(10,20)=true, book(15,25)=false, book(20,30)=true, book(5,10)=true, book(5,11)=false

Why it works: the bookings never overlap, so sorted by start they are also sorted by end. Only two bookings can possibly clash with the new one: the one just before it (does it end after we start?) and the one just after it (does it start before we end?). floorKey and ceilingKey find both in O(log n), so each book is O(log n) and space is O(n). A list scan would be O(n) per call.

Problem 2 — Contains Duplicate III

Are there indices i != j with |i − j| <= indexDiff and |nums[i] − nums[j]| <= valueDiff?

boolean containsNearbyAlmostDuplicate(int[] nums, int indexDiff, int valueDiff) {
    TreeSet<Long> window = new TreeSet<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        long x = nums[i];
        Long ceil = window.ceiling(x - valueDiff);     // smallest value >= x - valueDiff
        if (ceil != null && ceil <= x + valueDiff) return true;
        window.add(x);
        if (i >= indexDiff) window.remove((long) nums[i - indexDiff]); // keep last indexDiff values
    }
    return false;
}
// [1,2,3,1], 3, 0 -> true     [1,5,9,1,5,9], 2, 3 -> false

Why it works: a sliding window keeps only the last indexDiff values, which handles the index condition. For the value condition we need "is any value in [x − valueDiff, x + valueDiff]?" — ceiling(x − valueDiff) returns the smallest candidate in that range, and if even that one is above x + valueDiff, none fits. Using long avoids overflow when values are near Integer.MAX_VALUE. Time O(n log k), space O(k) where k = indexDiff. (A bucket approach reaches O(n), but the TreeSet version is what most interviewers expect first.)

Problem 3 — Stock Price Fluctuation

Updates arrive as (timestamp, price) and may correct an earlier timestamp. Support current(), maximum(), minimum().

class StockPrice {
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> priceAt = new HashMap<>();      // timestamp -> price
    private final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> count = new TreeMap<>();    // price -> how many timestamps
    private int latest = 0;

    public void update(int timestamp, int price) {
        Integer old = priceAt.put(timestamp, price);
        if (old != null) {                                  // correction: remove old price once
            if (count.merge(old, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) count.remove(old);
        }
        count.merge(price, 1, Integer::sum);
        latest = Math.max(latest, timestamp);
    }
    public int current() { return priceAt.get(latest); }
    public int maximum() { return count.lastKey(); }
    public int minimum() { return count.firstKey(); }
}
// update(1,10), update(2,5): current 5, max 10
// update(1,3) corrects t=1: max 5.  update(4,2): min 2, current 2

Why it works: the hard part is corrections: the old price must disappear from the max/min structure. A TreeMap used as a multiset (value to count) removes one copy of any price in O(log n) and gives min and max as firstKey/lastKey. Update is O(log n), current is O(1), max/min are O(log n). The alternative — two heaps with lazy deletion (pop stale tops whose price no longer matches) — also works but is harder to get right.

Pitfalls

  • floorKey and friends return null, not an exception, when nothing qualifies. Store the result in an Integer, not an int, or auto-unboxing throws NullPointerException.
  • TreeSet is a set: duplicates are silently dropped. Use a TreeMap<value, count> as a multiset.
  • A Comparator that returns 0 for different objects makes the tree treat them as equal — one of them vanishes. Break ties (for example by id).
  • Comparing boxed Integer keys with == only works for small values (-128 to 127 are cached). Use equals or unbox.
  • Modifying the map while iterating over it (other than through the iterator) throws ConcurrentModificationException.

15. Tries

In plain words

A trie (say "try", from retrieval), also called a prefix tree, stores strings letter by letter along paths from the root. Words that start the same way share the same first nodes. Each node has a flag that says "a word ends here".

Its superpower is prefixes: "is there any word starting with ca?" is answered by walking two steps, no matter how many words you stored.

The index tabs of a paper dictionary: first you jump to the C section, then CA, then CAR. You never look at the D pages, and all the CAR- words sit together.

Node design: array vs map

Node[] children = new Node[26]Map<Character, Node> children
SpeedFastest: direct index c - 'a'Slower: hashing and boxing
Memory26 references per node even if 1 is usedOnly stores children that exist
AlphabetFixed and small (lowercase letters, digits, bits)Any characters (Unicode, URLs)
Children in sorted orderFree: just loop 0..25Need a TreeMap

In interviews, use the array unless the problem says the characters can be anything.

Implementation — insert, search, startsWith

class Trie {
    private static class Node {
        Node[] children = new Node[26];   // one slot per letter 'a'..'z'
        boolean isEnd;                    // a word ends here
    }
    private final Node root = new Node();

    public void insert(String word) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (char c : word.toCharArray()) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (cur.children[i] == null) cur.children[i] = new Node();
            cur = cur.children[i];
        }
        cur.isEnd = true;
    }
    public boolean search(String word) {
        Node n = walk(word);
        return n != null && n.isEnd;       // path exists AND a word ends here
    }
    public boolean startsWith(String prefix) {
        return walk(prefix) != null;       // path exists is enough
    }
    private Node walk(String s) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
            cur = cur.children[c - 'a'];
            if (cur == null) return null;
        }
        return cur;
    }
}
// insert("apple"): search("apple")=true, search("app")=false, startsWith("app")=true
// insert("app"):   search("app")=true

The map version only changes two lines — cur = cur.children.computeIfAbsent(c, k -> new Node()) on insert and cur = cur.children.get(c) on lookup.

Operations complexity (L = length of the word or prefix)

OperationTrieHashSet<String>TreeSet<String>
InsertO(L)O(L) to hashO(L log n)
Exact searchO(L)O(L) averageO(L log n)
Any word with prefix p?O(|p|)O(n · L) — check every wordO(|p| log n) via ceiling(p)
List all k words with prefix pO(|p| + size of that subtree)O(n · L)O(|p| log n + k · L)
SpaceO(total characters × alphabet) worst caseO(total characters)O(total characters)

A trie does not beat a HashSet for exact lookups, and it uses more memory. Pick it when the problem is about prefixes, or when you search for many words at once while walking a structure (a grid, a stream of characters) and want to stop as soon as no word can match.

Problem 1 — Implement Trie

That is the class above. Every operation is O(L) time; insert uses O(L) new space in the worst case. Why it works: a path from the root spells a prefix, and the isEnd flag separates "a stored word" from "only a prefix of a stored word".

Problem 2 — Word Search II (the idea, plus code)

Given a letter grid and a list of words, find all words that can be traced through adjacent cells. Running a separate grid search for each word is too slow. Instead, put all words in a trie and run one DFS from each cell, walking the grid and the trie together. The moment the current path is not a prefix of any word, stop.

class Solution {
    private static class Node {
        Node[] next = new Node[26];
        String word;                      // set on the node where a word ends
    }
    public List<String> findWords(char[][] board, String[] words) {
        Node root = new Node();
        for (String w : words) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (char c : w.toCharArray()) {
                if (cur.next[c - 'a'] == null) cur.next[c - 'a'] = new Node();
                cur = cur.next[c - 'a'];
            }
            cur.word = w;
        }
        List<String> found = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int r = 0; r < board.length; r++)
            for (int c = 0; c < board[0].length; c++)
                dfs(board, r, c, root, found);
        return found;
    }
    private void dfs(char[][] b, int r, int c, Node parent, List<String> found) {
        if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= b.length || c >= b[0].length) return;
        char ch = b[r][c];
        if (ch == '#' || parent.next[ch - 'a'] == null) return;  // visited, or no word has this prefix
        Node node = parent.next[ch - 'a'];
        if (node.word != null) {
            found.add(node.word);
            node.word = null;                 // report each word once
        }
        b[r][c] = '#';                        // mark visited
        dfs(b, r + 1, c, node, found);
        dfs(b, r - 1, c, node, found);
        dfs(b, r, c + 1, node, found);
        dfs(b, r, c - 1, node, found);
        b[r][c] = ch;                         // restore (backtrack)
    }
}
// board oaan/etae/ihkr/iflv, words [oath, pea, eat, rain] -> [oath, eat]

Why it works: the trie shares work between words with the same prefix and prunes dead paths immediately. Time is O(R · C · 4 · 3L−1) in the worst case (L = longest word; after the first step each cell has at most 3 unvisited neighbours), but pruning makes it far faster in practice. Space O(total letters in words) for the trie plus O(L) recursion. Clearing node.word avoids duplicates without a Set.

Problem 3 — Autocomplete / search suggestions

After each typed character of searchWord, return up to three product names with that prefix, in lexicographic order.

List<List<String>> suggestedProducts(String[] products, String searchWord) {
    Arrays.sort(products);                    // insert in sorted order
    Node root = new Node();                   // Node { Node[] next = new Node[26]; List<String> top = new ArrayList<>(); }
    for (String p : products) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (char c : p.toCharArray()) {
            if (cur.next[c - 'a'] == null) cur.next[c - 'a'] = new Node();
            cur = cur.next[c - 'a'];
            if (cur.top.size() < 3) cur.top.add(p);  // first 3 seen are the 3 smallest
        }
    }
    List<List<String>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    Node cur = root;
    for (char c : searchWord.toCharArray()) {
        cur = (cur == null) ? null : cur.next[c - 'a'];
        result.add(cur == null ? List.of() : cur.top);
    }
    return result;
}
// products [mobile, mouse, moneypot, monitor, mousepad], "mouse" ->
// [[mobile, moneypot, monitor], [mobile, moneypot, monitor],
//  [mouse, mousepad], [mouse, mousepad], [mouse, mousepad]]

Why it works: because products are inserted in sorted order, the first three that pass through a node are the three smallest with that prefix, so each node can cache its answer. Build is O(n log n · L) for the sort plus O(total characters) for the trie; each keystroke is then O(1). Space O(total characters) nodes plus up to 3 references per node. Once a prefix has no match, every longer prefix returns an empty list. (A sorted array plus binary search for the prefix also works and uses less memory.)

Problem 4 — Longest word in dictionary

Find the longest word that can be built one character at a time by other words in the list (for "world", the words w, wo, wor, worl must all exist). Ties go to the lexicographically smallest.

class Solution {
    private static class Node {
        Node[] next = new Node[26];
        String word;
    }
    private String best = "";
    public String longestWord(String[] words) {
        Node root = new Node();
        for (String w : words) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (char c : w.toCharArray()) {
                if (cur.next[c - 'a'] == null) cur.next[c - 'a'] = new Node();
                cur = cur.next[c - 'a'];
            }
            cur.word = w;
        }
        dfs(root);
        return best;
    }
    // only step into children that are complete words themselves
    private void dfs(Node node) {
        for (Node child : node.next) {          // 'a'..'z' order = lexicographic
            if (child != null && child.word != null) {
                if (child.word.length() > best.length()) best = child.word;
                dfs(child);
            }
        }
    }
}
// [a, banana, app, appl, ap, apply, apple] -> "apple"  (apply is equally long but larger)
