  • Greedy choice property — there is always an optimal solution that starts with the greedy choice. In other words, taking the "locally best" option never rules out reaching the best overall answer.
  • Optimal substructure — after making that choice, what remains is a smaller version of the same problem, and solving it optimally gives an optimal overall answer.

The exchange argument (how you prove it, in easy words): take any optimal solution. If it doesn't use the greedy choice, swap one of its choices for the greedy one and show the result is still valid and no worse. Then an optimal solution with the greedy choice exists, so greedy is safe. Example for activity selection: an optimal schedule's first meeting ends at time E; the greedy first meeting ends at time e <= E (it's the earliest-ending one). Swap them — everything after still fits, because we finish even earlier. Same count, still optimal.

When greedy fails — the coin change counterexample

Coins {1, 3, 4}, amount 6 Greedy (biggest coin first): 4 + 1 + 1 = 3 coins Optimal: 3 + 3 = 2 coins With US coins {25, 10, 5, 1}, amount 63: greedy gives 25+25+10+1+1+1 = 6 coins = optimal. The greedy choice property holds for "canonical" coin systems, not for all of them.
// Greedy coin change: always take the biggest coin that fits. Time O(number of coins)
static int greedyCoins(int[] coinsDesc, int amount) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int c : coinsDesc) {
        count += amount / c;
        amount %= c;
    }
    return amount == 0 ? count : -1;
}
// greedyCoins({4, 3, 1}, 6) = 3, but the true minimum (coin-change DP) is 2

Lesson: before trusting greedy, try to break it with a tiny counterexample. If you find one, the problem probably needs DP (see the Dynamic Programming section). Same story for the 0/1 knapsack (greedy by value/weight fails) versus the fractional knapsack (greedy by value/weight is optimal, because you can take part of an item).

The usual recipe

  1. Decide what to sort by (end time? start time? size? ratio?). This is usually the whole insight.
  2. Sweep once, making the locally best decision and keeping a small amount of state.
  3. Sanity check with a counterexample hunt, then argue with an exchange argument.

Problem: Activity Selection / Non-overlapping Intervals (LeetCode 435)

Remove the fewest intervals so the rest don't overlap. Equivalent: keep the most non-overlapping intervals. Greedy rule: sort by end time and always keep the interval that finishes first — it leaves the most room for everything after.

Intervals [1,2] [2,3] [3,4] [1,3], sorted by end: [1,2] [2,3] [1,3] [3,4] [1,2] start 1 >= lastEnd -inf keep lastEnd = 2 [2,3] start 2 >= lastEnd 2 keep lastEnd = 3 [1,3] start 1 < lastEnd 3 drop [3,4] start 3 >= lastEnd 3 keep lastEnd = 4 kept 3 of 4 -> remove 1
// Time O(n log n), Space O(1) beyond the sort
static int eraseOverlapIntervals(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));   // by END time
    int kept = 0;
    int lastEnd = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int[] in : intervals) {
        if (in[0] >= lastEnd) {          // starts after the last kept one ends
            kept++;
            lastEnd = in[1];
        }
    }
    return intervals.length - kept;
}
// {{1,2},{2,3},{3,4},{1,3}} -> 1;   {{1,2},{1,2},{1,2}} -> 2

Why this works: the exchange argument above. Sorting by start time is the classic wrong answer: one long interval starting first would block many short ones.

Problem: Jump Game (LeetCode 55)

nums[i] is the maximum jump length from index i. Can you reach the last index? Track the farthest index reachable so far. If you ever stand on an index beyond it, you're stuck.

// Time O(n), Space O(1)
static boolean canJump(int[] nums) {
    int farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (i > farthest) return false;           // we can't even stand here
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + nums[i]);
    }
    return true;
}
// {2, 3, 1, 1, 4} -> true;   {3, 2, 1, 0, 4} -> false (everything stops at index 3)

Why this works: every index up to farthest is reachable (you can always jump shorter than the max). So we don't need to know which jumps to take — only how far the reachable region extends.

Problem: Jump Game II (LeetCode 45) — minimum jumps

Think of it as BFS in levels without a queue: all indexes reachable with 1 jump form level 1, those with 2 jumps form level 2, and so on. curEnd is the end of the current level; farthest is how far the next level reaches.

nums = [2, 3, 1, 1, 4] i=0 farthest = 2 i == curEnd(0) -> jumps = 1, curEnd = 2 (level 1 = indexes 1..2) i=1 farthest = 4 i=2 farthest = 4 i == curEnd(2) -> jumps = 2, curEnd = 4 (level 2 reaches the end) i=3 farthest = 4 (loop stops before the last index) answer 2: 0 -> 1 -> 4
// Time O(n), Space O(1). Assumes the end is reachable.
static int jump(int[] nums) {
    int jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length - 1; i++) {   // stop before last: no jump needed from it
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + nums[i]);
        if (i == curEnd) {                        // used up this jump's range
            jumps++;
            curEnd = farthest;                    // next "level" of reach
        }
    }
    return jumps;
}
// {2, 3, 1, 1, 4} -> 2;   {2, 3, 0, 1, 4} -> 2;   {0} -> 0

Pitfall: looping to nums.length instead of nums.length - 1 counts one extra jump when a level ends exactly on the last index.

Problem: Gas Station (LeetCode 134)

Circular route; gas[i] fuel at station i, cost[i] to drive to the next. Find the start index that completes the loop (unique if it exists). Two facts: (1) if total gas >= total cost, a solution exists; (2) if you start at s and run dry before reaching i + 1, then no station between s and i can be the start either — so jump the start straight to i + 1.

// Time O(n), Space O(1)
static int canCompleteCircuit(int[] gas, int[] cost) {
    int total = 0, tank = 0, start = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < gas.length; i++) {
        int diff = gas[i] - cost[i];
        total += diff;
        tank += diff;
        if (tank < 0) {          // can't reach i+1 from start: no station in [start..i] works
            start = i + 1;
            tank = 0;
        }
    }
    return total >= 0 ? start : -1;
}
// gas {1,2,3,4,5}, cost {3,4,5,1,2} -> 3;   gas {2,3,4}, cost {3,4,3} -> -1

Why this works: any station k inside [start, i] was reached with a tank >= 0, so starting at k with an empty tank can only be worse — it would also fail at i. That's why one pass is enough.

Problem: Assign Cookies (LeetCode 455)

// Time O(n log n + m log m), Space O(1) beyond sorting
static int findContentChildren(int[] greed, int[] cookies) {
    Arrays.sort(greed);
    Arrays.sort(cookies);
    int child = 0;
    for (int c = 0; c < cookies.length && child < greed.length; c++) {
        if (cookies[c] >= greed[child]) child++;   // smallest cookie that satisfies this child
    }
    return child;
}
// greed {1,2,3}, cookies {1,1} -> 1;   greed {1,2}, cookies {1,2,3} -> 2

Why this works: give the least greedy child the smallest cookie that satisfies them. Using a bigger cookie there would never help — by exchange, it can only take a cookie some greedier child could have used.

Problem: Meeting Rooms II / Minimum Platforms

Given meetings (or train arrival/departure times), find the minimum rooms (platforms) so nothing clashes. The answer is the maximum number of meetings happening at the same moment. Sweep start times in order and free up rooms whose meetings have ended.

// Two sorted arrays + two pointers. Time O(n log n), Space O(n)
static int minMeetingRooms(int[][] meetings) {
    int n = meetings.length;
    int[] starts = new int[n], ends = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { starts[i] = meetings[i][0]; ends[i] = meetings[i][1]; }
    Arrays.sort(starts);
    Arrays.sort(ends);
    int rooms = 0, maxRooms = 0, e = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        while (e < n && ends[e] <= starts[s]) { e++; rooms--; }   // free rooms that ended
        rooms++;
        maxRooms = Math.max(maxRooms, rooms);
    }
    return maxRooms;
}

// Same answer with a min-heap of end times (one entry per busy room). Time O(n log n), Space O(n)
static int minMeetingRoomsHeap(int[][] meetings) {
    Arrays.sort(meetings, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    PriorityQueue<Integer> endTimes = new PriorityQueue<>();
    for (int[] m : meetings) {
        if (!endTimes.isEmpty() && endTimes.peek() <= m[0]) endTimes.poll();  // reuse a room
        endTimes.offer(m[1]);
    }
    return endTimes.size();
}
// {{0,30},{5,10},{15,20}} -> 2
// trains {{900,910},{940,1200},{950,1120},{1100,1130},{1500,1900},{1800,2000}} -> 3

Why this works: a room can only be reused once some meeting has ended, and we always reuse the one that ended earliest. The count only grows when every room is busy, so the peak is exactly the minimum needed. Edge case to ask about: if one meeting ends at 10 and another starts at 10, can they share a room? The code says yes (<=). For platforms problems that count "same minute" as a clash, use <.

Huffman coding — the greedy idea

Goal: give each character a binary code so frequent characters get short codes and the total encoded length is minimal (no code is a prefix of another). Greedy rule: repeatedly merge the two rarest nodes into a new node whose frequency is their sum. Each merge pushes those characters one level deeper — one more bit each — so the cost of a merge is a + b, and the total cost is the sum of all merges.

// Total encoded bits. Time O(n log n), Space O(n)
static long huffmanCost(int[] freq) {
    PriorityQueue<Long> pq = new PriorityQueue<>();
    for (int f : freq) pq.offer((long) f);
    long cost = 0;
    while (pq.size() > 1) {
        long a = pq.poll(), b = pq.poll();   // two rarest
        cost += a + b;                       // each merge adds one bit to all symbols below
        pq.offer(a + b);
    }
    return cost;
}
// freq {5, 9, 12, 13, 16, 45} -> 224 bits

Why this works: the two rarest characters should be the deepest leaves (exchange argument: swapping a rarer character deeper never increases cost), and they can be siblings. Merge them and the rest is the same problem, one character smaller. The same "merge two smallest" pattern solves Minimum Cost to Connect Sticks and optimal file merge.

Greedy vs DP in one line: greedy commits to one choice per step and never looks back; DP tries every choice per step and remembers sub-answers. If you can build a small counterexample to your greedy rule, switch to DP. If an exchange argument works, greedy is usually the O(n log n) answer the interviewer wants.

23. Divide & Conquer

In plain words

Divide and conquer solves a big problem in three moves: split it into smaller pieces of the same kind, solve each piece (usually by calling yourself recursively), and combine the piece answers into the full answer. The pieces keep shrinking until they are so small the answer is obvious (the base case).

It differs from DP in one key way: the pieces don't overlap. The left half and right half of an array are independent, so nothing is solved twice and nothing needs caching.

Counting the votes of a whole country: every district counts its own ballots at the same time, then regions add up their districts, then the nation adds up the regions. Nobody counts the whole pile alone.

Split, solve, combine

AlgorithmSplitSolveCombineWhere the real work is
Merge sortcut at the middle, O(1)sort both halvesmerge, O(n)combine
Quicksortpartition around a pivot, O(n)sort both sidesnothingsplit
Quickselectpartition, O(n)recurse into one sidenothingsplit
Binary searchcompare with middle, O(1)recurse into one halfnothing—
Max subarray (D&C)cut at the middlebest in each halfbest crossing the middle, O(n)combine
Fast powerx^n = (x^(n/2))^2one half-size powersquare (times x if odd), O(1)—

The recursion tree and the master theorem

Write the running time as a recurrence: T(n) = a T(n / b) + f(n), meaning "a subproblems, each 1/b the size, plus f(n) work to split and combine". Draw it as a tree and add up the work level by level. For merge sort, a = 2, b = 2, f(n) = n:

The master theorem is a shortcut for T(n) = a T(n / b) + O(n^d). Compare d with logb a (how fast the number of pieces grows):

CaseResultIn words
d > logb aO(n^d)The top-level combine dominates
d = logb aO(n^d log n)Every level costs the same; multiply by the height
d < logb aO(n^(logb a))The leaves dominate — there are so many of them
AlgorithmRecurrenceResult
Binary searchT(n) = T(n/2) + O(1)O(log n)
Fast powerT(n) = T(n/2) + O(1)O(log n)
Quickselect (average)T(n) = T(n/2) + O(n)O(n)
Tree traversal (balanced)T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(1)O(n)
Merge sort, count inversions, max subarray D&CT(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n)O(n log n)
Karatsuba multiplicationT(n) = 3T(n/2) + O(n)O(n^1.585)
Strassen matrix multiplyT(n) = 7T(n/2) + O(n²)O(n^2.81)

The theorem needs equal-sized splits. Quicksort's worst case, T(n) = T(n - 1) + O(n), isn't covered — sum it directly: n + (n-1) + ... = O(n²).

Recap: merge sort and quickselect

Both were written in full in the Sorting section. Merge sort is the textbook D&C: trivial split, O(n) combine, O(n log n) always. Quickselect shows the power of recursing into only one piece: O(n) + O(n/2) + O(n/4) + ... = O(2n) = O(n) on average, versus O(n log n) to sort first. Count inversions is merge sort with a counter in the combine step — same O(n log n).

Problem: Maximum Subarray (LeetCode 53) — D&C vs Kadane

Find the contiguous subarray with the largest sum. D&C view: the best subarray is either entirely in the left half, entirely in the right half, or crosses the middle. The crossing one is the best suffix of the left half glued to the best prefix of the right half — found in O(n) by walking outward from the middle.

// Divide & conquer. Time O(n log n), Space O(log n) recursion
static int maxSubArrayDC(int[] a) { return best(a, 0, a.length - 1); }

static int best(int[] a, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo == hi) return a[lo];                    // one element
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    int left = best(a, lo, mid);                   // answer fully in left half
    int right = best(a, mid + 1, hi);              // answer fully in right half
    // answer crossing the middle: best suffix of left + best prefix of right
    int sum = 0, bestLeft = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int i = mid; i >= lo; i--) { sum += a[i]; bestLeft = Math.max(bestLeft, sum); }
    sum = 0;
    int bestRight = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int j = mid + 1; j <= hi; j++) { sum += a[j]; bestRight = Math.max(bestRight, sum); }
    return Math.max(Math.max(left, right), bestLeft + bestRight);
}

// Kadane. Time O(n), Space O(1)
static int maxSubArrayKadane(int[] a) {
    int cur = a[0], best = a[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
        cur = Math.max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend the run, or start fresh here
        best = Math.max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}
// {-2, 1, -3, 4, -1, 2, 1, -5, 4}  ->  6 (subarray [4, -1, 2, 1]), both methods
// {-3, -1, -2}  ->  -1 (all negative: best is the single largest element)

Why both work: D&C covers all three places the answer can live. Kadane asks at each index "is the best run ending here the previous run extended, or a fresh start?" — a one-dimensional DP. Kadane wins in an interview (O(n)), but the D&C version is a favourite follow-up ("solve it with divide and conquer") and it generalises to a segment tree that answers max-subarray queries on ranges with updates, where Kadane cannot.

Problem: Pow(x, n) — fast exponentiation (LeetCode 50)

Multiplying x by itself n times is O(n). Instead: x^n = (x^(n/2))² if n is even, and x · (x^(n/2))² if odd. One half-size subproblem per level, so O(log n). The key is computing half once — writing pow(x, n/2) * pow(x, n/2) makes two calls and falls back to O(n).

// Recursive. Time O(log n), Space O(log n)
static double myPow(double x, int n) {
    long N = n;                          // -Integer.MIN_VALUE overflows int: use long
    if (N < 0) { x = 1 / x; N = -N; }
    return pow(x, N);
}

static double pow(double x, long n) {
    if (n == 0) return 1.0;
    double half = pow(x, n / 2);         // solve ONE half-size problem
    return (n % 2 == 0) ? half * half : half * half * x;
}

// Iterative (binary exponentiation). Time O(log n), Space O(1)
static double myPowIter(double x, int n) {
    long N = n;
    if (N < 0) { x = 1 / x; N = -N; }
    double result = 1.0;
    while (N > 0) {
        if ((N & 1) == 1) result *= x;   // this bit of n is set: include x^(2^k)
        x *= x;                          // x, x^2, x^4, x^8, ...
        N >>= 1;
    }
    return result;
}

// Modular version, used everywhere in counting problems ("answer mod 1e9+7")
static long modPow(long base, long exp, long mod) {
    long result = 1;
    base %= mod;
    while (exp > 0) {
        if ((exp & 1) == 1) result = result * base % mod;
        base = base * base % mod;
        exp >>= 1;
    }
    return result;
}
// myPow(2.0, 10) = 1024.0,  myPow(2.0, -2) = 0.25,  myPow(1.0, Integer.MIN_VALUE) = 1.0
// modPow(2, 10, 1_000_000_007) = 1024

Why this works: write n in binary, for example 13 = 1101₂, so x^13 = x^8 · x^4 · x^1. The loop walks the bits and multiplies in the matching powers of x. Pitfall: n = Integer.MIN_VALUE has no positive int counterpart, so -n overflows — convert to long first. In modPow, keep mod below about 3 · 10^9 so base * base fits in a long.

Closest pair of points — the idea

Given n points in a plane, find the two closest. Brute force checks all pairs: O(n²). D&C:

  1. Split: sort by x, cut into left and right halves at the middle x.
  2. Solve: find the closest distance d in each half recursively; let d = the smaller one.
  3. Combine: the only pairs not yet checked cross the dividing line. Such a pair can only beat d if both points are within d of the line — a thin strip. Sort the strip by y; each point needs checking only against the next few points (at most 7) whose y is within d, because points on the same side are at least d apart and only a handful can fit in a d x 2d box.
// Time O(n log² n) as written (strip re-sorted at each level); O(n log n) if you merge by y
// like merge sort instead of re-sorting. Space O(n)
static double closestPair(int[][] pts) {
    int[][] byX = pts.clone();
    Arrays.sort(byX, (p, q) -> Integer.compare(p[0], q[0]));
    return closest(byX, 0, byX.length - 1);
}

static double closest(int[][] p, int lo, int hi) {
    if (hi - lo < 3) {                                   // tiny: brute force
        double best = Double.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int i = lo; i <= hi; i++)
            for (int j = i + 1; j <= hi; j++) best = Math.min(best, dist(p[i], p[j]));
        return best;
    }
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    int midX = p[mid][0];
    double d = Math.min(closest(p, lo, mid), closest(p, mid + 1, hi));
    List<int[]> strip = new ArrayList<>();               // points within d of the dividing line
    for (int i = lo; i <= hi; i++) if (Math.abs(p[i][0] - midX) < d) strip.add(p[i]);
    strip.sort((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));   // sort strip by y
    for (int i = 0; i < strip.size(); i++)
        for (int j = i + 1; j < strip.size() && strip.get(j)[1] - strip.get(i)[1] < d; j++)
            d = Math.min(d, dist(strip.get(i), strip.get(j)));   // only a few j per i
    return d;
}

static double dist(int[] a, int[] b) {
    long dx = a[0] - b[0], dy = a[1] - b[1];
    return Math.sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
}
// {{2,3},{12,30},{40,50},{5,1},{12,10},{3,4}}  ->  1.4142135623730951  ((2,3) and (3,4))

Rarely asked to code in full, but "how would you beat O(n²)?" is a good D&C discussion question. The inner loop looks nested, but the geometry caps it at a constant number of steps per point.

Problem: Count Inversions (recap)

The full code is in the Sorting section. As D&C: split at the middle; solve = count inversions inside each half (and sort them); combine = during the merge, each time an element is taken from the right half, add mid - i + 1 (the number of left elements still waiting, all bigger). Recurrence 2T(n/2) + O(n), so O(n log n) instead of O(n²). The same "count across the split while merging" trick solves Count of Smaller Numbers After Self (LeetCode 315) and Reverse Pairs (LeetCode 493).

When D&C beats brute force

  • The combine step is cheaper than brute-forcing across the split. Inversions: checking all left-right pairs is O(n²), but sorted halves let us count them in O(n).
  • You can throw pieces away. Binary search and quickselect recurse into only one piece — that's where O(log n) and O(n) come from.
  • Reusing one sub-result. Fast power computes x^(n/2) once and squares it.
  • Pieces are independent, so they can run in parallel (Java's ForkJoinPool, Arrays.parallelSort, MapReduce).

When it doesn't help: if the subproblems overlap (Fibonacci: fib(n-1) and fib(n-2) both recompute fib(n-3)), plain D&C is exponential — that is exactly when you switch to memoization / DP. And if the combine step is as expensive as the whole brute force, splitting buys nothing.

Pitfalls: forgetting the base case (or making it unreachable) gives a StackOverflowError. Splitting as [lo, mid] and [mid, hi] overlaps and can loop forever — use [lo, mid] and [mid + 1, hi]. Allocating new arrays at every level (e.g. Arrays.copyOfRange in merge sort) works but adds O(n log n) garbage; pass indexes and one shared buffer instead. Recursion depth is O(log n) for balanced splits but O(n) for lopsided ones (bad quicksort pivots).

24. Dynamic Programming — the Fundamentals

In plain words

Dynamic programming (DP) is recursion with a memory. You break a problem into smaller versions of itself, solve each small version once, write the answer down, and reuse it whenever it comes up again.

It works when two things are true: the same small problems come up again and again (overlapping subproblems), and the best answer to the big problem can be built from the best answers to the small ones (optimal substructure).

If someone asks you "what is 1+1+1+1+1+1+1?" you count: 7. If they then add "+1" at the end, you don't recount — you say "8", because you remembered 7. DP is simply refusing to count the same thing twice.

The two properties, explained

  • Overlapping subproblems — a plain recursive solution calls itself with the same arguments many times. If every call is different (like merge sort's halves), there is nothing to reuse: that is divide and conquer, not DP.
  • Optimal substructure — the best answer for size n is built from best answers for smaller sizes. Shortest path has it (a shortest path's middle part is also shortest). "Longest simple path" does not, which is why it has no DP.

Seeing the repeated work

Climbing stairs: you can climb 1 or 2 steps at a time; how many ways to reach step n? To land on step n, your last move was either from n-1 (a 1-step) or from n-2 (a 2-step). So ways(n) = ways(n-1) + ways(n-2) — the Fibonacci recurrence.

// Plain recursion: correct but O(2^n) time
static int climbNaive(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;                 // 1 way to stand on step 0 or reach step 1
    return climbNaive(n - 1) + climbNaive(n - 2);
}

The call counts are real: counting calls in the code above gives 15 for n = 5 and 2,692,537 for n = 30. The tree roughly doubles with each extra step, so it is O(2^n). Yet there are only n + 1 different questions (f0 to fn). DP answers each one once.

The 5-step recipe

Use these steps for every DP problem. Say them out loud in an interview — it shows structure even before the code is right.

StepQuestion to askClimbing stairs
1. StateWhat does dp[i] mean, in one sentence?dp[i] = number of ways to reach step i
2. RecurrenceHow is a state built from smaller states? (Think: "what was the last choice?")dp[i] = dp[i-1] + dp[i-2]
3. Base casesWhich states can be answered without the recurrence?dp[0] = 1, dp[1] = 1
4. OrderIn what order must states be filled so dependencies are ready?i from 2 up to n
5. AnswerWhich state (or max/min of states) is the final answer?dp[n]
The state is the hard part. A good state holds exactly the information needed to decide the future, and nothing more. If your recurrence needs to know "something about the past" that the state does not hold, add it as another dimension (for example dp[i][holding] in the stock problems).

Top-down (memoisation) vs bottom-up (tabulation)

Top-down: write the natural recursion, then add a cache (the memo). Before computing, check the memo; after computing, store it. Bottom-up: create a table and fill it from the smallest states upward with a loop, so every value you need is already there.

// Top-down: recursion + memo. O(n) time, O(n) space (memo + call stack)
static int climbMemo(int n) {
    int[] memo = new int[n + 1];
    Arrays.fill(memo, -1);                       // -1 = "not solved yet"
    return go(n, memo);
}
private static int go(int i, int[] memo) {
    if (i <= 1) return 1;                        // base cases
    if (memo[i] != -1) return memo[i];           // already solved? reuse
    return memo[i] = go(i - 1, memo) + go(i - 2, memo);
}

// Bottom-up: fill a table from small to big. O(n) time, O(n) space
static int climbTab(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;
    int[] dp = new int[n + 1];
    dp[0] = 1; dp[1] = 1;                        // base cases
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++)
        dp[i] = dp[i - 1] + dp[i - 2];           // recurrence
    return dp[n];                                // answer
}

// Space-optimised: only the last two values are ever read. O(n) time, O(1) space
static int climbO1(int n) {
    int prev2 = 1, prev1 = 1;                    // dp[i-2], dp[i-1]
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) {
        int cur = prev1 + prev2;
        prev2 = prev1;
        prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}
i0123456
dp[i]11235813

All three versions return 13 for n = 6 and 1,836,311,903 for n = 45 (the largest n that still fits in an int).

Top-down (memo)Bottom-up (table)
How you write itNatural recursion + cacheLoops in dependency order
Which states are computedOnly the ones actually reachedAll of them
Stack overflow riskYes — recursion depth can be n (Java's default stack breaks around 10,000 deep)None
Space optimisationHardEasy — keep only the rows/values you still read
When to pick itHard-to-order states, sparse state space, getting a first correct answer fastLarge n, need for speed, need to cut memory

Second example: House Robber (LC 198)

Houses in a row hold money nums[i]. You cannot rob two neighbours. Maximise the total. At each house the choice is rob it (then skip the next) or skip it.

  • State: dp[i] = most money from the first i houses.
  • Recurrence: dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], dp[i-2] + nums[i-1]) — skip house i, or rob it and add the best from two houses back.
  • Base: dp[0] = 0, dp[1] = nums[0]. Answer: dp[n].
// Top-down: best(i) = most money from houses i..n-1
static int robMemo(int[] nums) {
    return best(nums, 0, new Integer[nums.length]);  // null = not solved yet
}
private static int best(int[] nums, int i, Integer[] memo) {
    if (i >= nums.length) return 0;
    if (memo[i] != null) return memo[i];
    int rob  = nums[i] + best(nums, i + 2, memo);    // take i, skip neighbour
    int skip = best(nums, i + 1, memo);              // leave i
    return memo[i] = Math.max(rob, skip);
}

// Bottom-up table
static int robTab(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] dp = new int[n + 1];                       // dp[i] = best using first i houses
    dp[1] = nums[0];
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++)
        dp[i] = Math.max(dp[i - 1], dp[i - 2] + nums[i - 1]);
    return dp[n];
}

// Rolling variables: O(n) time, O(1) space
static int rob(int[] nums) {
    int prev2 = 0, prev1 = 0;                        // best up to i-2, best up to i-1
    for (int x : nums) {
        int cur = Math.max(prev1, prev2 + x);
        prev2 = prev1;
        prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}

Traced on nums = [2, 7, 9, 3, 1]:

ihouse valueskip: dp[i-1]rob: dp[i-2] + valuedp[i]
12——2
2720 + 7 = 77
3972 + 9 = 1111
43117 + 3 = 1011
511111 + 1 = 1212

All three versions return 12 (rob houses 2, 9 and 1). Time O(n); space O(n) for memo/table, O(1) for the rolling version.

Space optimisation — the general rule

  • If dp[i] only reads dp[i-1] and dp[i-2], keep two variables.
  • If a 2D dp[i][j] only reads row i-1, keep one or two 1D rows. Direction matters: loop so you never read a cell you already overwrote in this row (unless you want to — see knapsack in section 25).
  • You lose the ability to reconstruct the actual choices (which houses, which items). If the question asks for the path, not just the number, keep the full table.

How to recognise a DP problem

Signal in the questionExample
"Count the number of ways…"Climbing stairs, unique paths, coin change II, decode ways
"Minimum / maximum cost, profit, length…"Coin change, house robber, LIS, edit distance
"Is it possible to…?" (yes/no)Partition equal subset sum, word break, jump game
At each step you make a choice (take/skip, left/right, buy/sell) and choices affect the futureKnapsack, stock problems
Input is a sequence, string, grid or tree, and n is up to ~10^3–10^4O(n^2) DP fits; n around 20 hints at bitmask DP
DP or greedy? Greedy makes one locally best choice and never looks back. Try a small counter-example first: coins {1, 3, 4} for amount 6 — greedy takes 4+1+1 (3 coins) but the best is 3+3 (2 coins). When greedy can be beaten like this, you need DP. If you cannot break greedy and can prove it (exchange argument), greedy is simpler and faster.
Interview flow: (1) write the brute-force recursion and say its complexity; (2) point out the repeated subproblems; (3) add a memo; (4) if asked, convert to bottom-up and shrink the space. Each step is a correct solution, so you always have something working.

25. DP Patterns

In plain words

Most interview DP problems are one of about a dozen patterns wearing a costume. Once you know the pattern, the state and recurrence are almost given to you — you only adapt the details.

For each pattern below: what dp means, how it is filled, the Java code, and its cost.

Like chess openings. A strong player does not think from scratch on move one; they recognise "this is the Sicilian" and already know the plan. Recognising "this is knapsack" gets you 80% of the way.

PatternState shapeTypical problems
0/1 knapsackdp[i][capacity]Partition equal subset, target sum, last stone weight II
Unbounded knapsackdp[amount]Coin change I/II, perfect squares, rod cutting
Single sequencedp[i] = answer ending at / using first iLIS, house robber, word break, decode ways
Two sequencesdp[i][j] = answer for prefixesLCS, edit distance, distinct subsequences
Griddp[r][c]Unique paths, minimum path sum, maximal square
Intervaldp[l][r]Burst balloons, matrix chain, palindrome partitioning
Treevalue returned from DFS per nodeHouse robber III, binary tree cameras, max path sum
Bitmaskdp[mask][i]TSP, shortest path visiting all nodes, assignment
State machinedp[i][state]Stock buy/sell family

0/1 knapsack

n items each with a weight and a value; a bag of capacity W. Each item is taken at most once. Maximise value.

  • State: dp[i][c] = best value using the first i items with capacity c.
  • Recurrence: dp[i][c] = max(dp[i-1][c], dp[i-1][c - w] + v) — skip item i, or take it (only if w <= c).
  • Base: row 0 is all zeros (no items, no value). Answer: dp[n][W].
static int knapsack2D(int[] wt, int[] val, int W) {
    int n = wt.length;
    int[][] dp = new int[n + 1][W + 1];   // dp[i][c] = best value, first i items, capacity c
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (int c = 0; c <= W; c++) {
            dp[i][c] = dp[i - 1][c];                          // skip item i
            if (wt[i - 1] <= c)                               // take item i (once)
                dp[i][c] = Math.max(dp[i][c], dp[i - 1][c - wt[i - 1]] + val[i - 1]);
        }
    }
    return dp[n][W];
}

Traced with weights {1, 3, 4}, values {15, 20, 30}, W = 4:

items \ capacity01234
none00000
+ item (w1, v15)015151515
+ item (w3, v20)015152035
+ item (w4, v30)015152035

Answer 35 (items of weight 1 and 3). Time O(n·W), space O(n·W).

1D version. Row i only reads row i-1, so one array is enough — if you loop capacity backwards.

static int knapsack1D(int[] wt, int[] val, int W) {
    int[] dp = new int[W + 1];                 // dp[c] = best value with capacity c
    for (int i = 0; i < wt.length; i++)
        for (int c = W; c >= wt[i]; c--)       // BACKWARDS: each item used at most once
            dp[c] = Math.max(dp[c], dp[c - wt[i]] + val[i]);
    return dp[W];
}
Why backwards? dp[c] reads dp[c - w], a smaller index. Going from high c to low, the smaller cell has not been touched yet in this round, so it still holds the "previous item" value — exactly dp[i-1][c-w]. Going forwards, dp[c-w] may already include the current item, so the item gets added again and again. With the example above, the forward loop returns 60 (the weight-1 item taken four times) instead of 35. Forwards = unbounded knapsack; backwards = 0/1 knapsack.

Time O(n·W), space O(W). Note W is a value, not a size — this is "pseudo-polynomial", so it is fast only when W is small (say up to 10^4–10^5).

Unbounded knapsack: Coin Change (LC 322) and Coin Change II (LC 518)

Each coin can be used any number of times. Two different questions: fewest coins (min), and number of combinations (count).

  • Coin Change — state: dp[a] = fewest coins to make amount a. Recurrence: dp[a] = min over coins (dp[a - coin] + 1). Base: dp[0] = 0.
  • Coin Change II — state: dp[a] = number of combinations making a. Recurrence: dp[a] += dp[a - coin]. Base: dp[0] = 1 (one way to make 0: take nothing).
static int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount) {
    int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];            // dp[a] = fewest coins to make a
    Arrays.fill(dp, amount + 1);               // "infinity" that cannot overflow on +1
    dp[0] = 0;
    for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
        for (int coin : coins)
            if (coin <= a)
                dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - coin] + 1);
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}

static int change(int amount, int[] coins) {
    int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];            // dp[a] = number of combinations making a
    dp[0] = 1;                                 // one way to make 0: take nothing
    for (int coin : coins)                     // coins OUTER -> combinations, not orders
        for (int a = coin; a <= amount; a++)   // forwards: a coin may repeat
            dp[a] += dp[a - coin];
    return dp[amount];
}

coinChange({1, 2, 5}, 11) = 3 (5+5+1); coinChange({2}, 3) = -1; change(5, {1, 2, 5}) = 4. Both run in O(amount · coins) time, O(amount) space.

Loop order decides combinations vs permutations. With coins in the outer loop, each coin is "introduced" once, so 1+2+2 and 2+1+2 are the same combination: 4 ways for amount 5. Swap the loops (amount outer, coins inner) and you count ordered sequences instead — the same input gives 9. That swapped version is Combination Sum IV (LC 377).

Longest Increasing Subsequence (LC 300)

A subsequence keeps order but may skip elements. Find the longest strictly increasing one.

O(n^2): dp[i] = length of the LIS that ends exactly at index i. For every earlier j with nums[j] < nums[i], you can extend it: dp[i] = max(dp[j] + 1). The answer is the max over all i (not dp[n-1]).

static int lengthOfLIS_n2(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length, best = 0;
    int[] dp = new int[n];                     // dp[i] = LIS ending exactly at i
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        dp[i] = 1;                             // just nums[i] alone
        for (int j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (nums[j] < nums[i])
                dp[i] = Math.max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1);
        best = Math.max(best, dp[i]);
    }
    return best;
}

O(n log n) — patience sorting. Keep tails[k] = the smallest possible last value of an increasing subsequence of length k+1. A smaller tail is always better, because it is easier to extend. For each number, binary-search the first tail >= x and replace it; if none, append.

static int lengthOfLIS(int[] nums) {
    int[] tails = new int[nums.length];
    int size = 0;
    for (int x : nums) {
        int lo = 0, hi = size;                 // find first tail >= x
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            if (tails[mid] < x) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
        }
        tails[lo] = x;                         // replace, or append if lo == size
        if (lo == size) size++;
    }
    return size;
}
nums = [10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7, 101, 18] x=10 tails = [10] x=9 tails = [9] 9 replaces 10 (smaller tail, same length) x=2 tails = [2] x=5 tails = [2, 5] append x=3 tails = [2, 3] 3 replaces 5 x=7 tails = [2, 3, 7] append x=101 tails = [2, 3, 7, 101] append x=18 tails = [2, 3, 7, 18] 18 replaces 101 answer = size = 4

Both return 4. tails is not itself a valid subsequence — only its length is meaningful. For "non-decreasing" use "first tail > x" instead. Time O(n log n), space O(n). Variants: Russian doll envelopes (sort by width ascending, height descending, then LIS on heights), number of LIS, longest chain.

Longest Common Subsequence (LC 1143)

  • State: dp[i][j] = LCS length of a[0..i) and b[0..j) (the first i and j characters).
  • Recurrence: if a[i-1] == b[j-1], both last chars join the LCS: dp[i-1][j-1] + 1. Otherwise drop one of them: max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]).
  • Base: row 0 and column 0 are 0 (empty string). Answer: dp[m][n].
static int lcs(String a, String b) {
    int m = a.length(), n = b.length();
    int[][] dp = new int[m + 1][n + 1];        // dp[i][j] = LCS of a[0..i) and b[0..j)
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            dp[i][j] = a.charAt(i - 1) == b.charAt(j - 1)
                    ? dp[i - 1][j - 1] + 1                    // diagonal + 1
                    : Math.max(dp[i - 1][j], dp[i][j - 1]);   // up or left
    return dp[m][n];
}

Table for a = "abcde", b = "ace" (bold = a character match, taken from the diagonal):

""ace
""0000
a0111
b0111
c0122
d0122
e0123

Answer 3 ("ace"). To rebuild the string, start at dp[m][n]: on a match step diagonally and record the char; else move toward the larger of up/left. Time O(m·n), space O(m·n), or O(min(m, n)) with two rows.

Edit Distance (LC 72)

Fewest single-character inserts, deletes or replaces to turn a into b.

  • State: dp[i][j] = edits to turn a[0..i) into b[0..j).
  • Recurrence: chars equal: dp[i-1][j-1] (free). Otherwise 1 + min(replace dp[i-1][j-1], delete dp[i-1][j], insert dp[i][j-1]).
  • Base: dp[i][0] = i (delete all), dp[0][j] = j (insert all).
static int minDistance(String a, String b) {
    int m = a.length(), n = b.length();
    int[][] dp = new int[m + 1][n + 1];
    for (int i = 0; i <= m; i++) dp[i][0] = i; // delete everything
    for (int j = 0; j <= n; j++) dp[0][j] = j; // insert everything
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++) {
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++) {
            if (a.charAt(i - 1) == b.charAt(j - 1)) {
                dp[i][j] = dp[i - 1][j - 1];                 // free: chars match
            } else {
                dp[i][j] = 1 + Math.min(dp[i - 1][j - 1],    // replace
                               Math.min(dp[i - 1][j],        // delete from a
                                        dp[i][j - 1]));      // insert into a
            }
        }
    }
    return dp[m][n];
}

minDistance("horse", "ros") = 3 (horse → rorse → rose → ros); ("intention", "execution") = 5. Time and space O(m·n).

Grid paths: Unique Paths (LC 62) and Minimum Path Sum (LC 64)

Moving only right or down, a cell can be reached only from above or from the left. So dp[r][c] combines dp[r-1][c] and dp[r][c-1] — sum them to count paths, min them for cheapest path.

static int uniquePaths(int m, int n) {
    int[] dp = new int[n];                     // one row, reused
    Arrays.fill(dp, 1);                        // first row: one way to each cell
    for (int r = 1; r < m; r++)
        for (int c = 1; c < n; c++)
            dp[c] += dp[c - 1];                // from above (old dp[c]) + from left
    return dp[n - 1];
}

static int minPathSum(int[][] grid) {
    int m = grid.length, n = grid[0].length;
    int[][] dp = new int[m][n];
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < n; c++) {
            if (r == 0 && c == 0) dp[r][c] = grid[0][0];
            else if (r == 0)      dp[r][c] = dp[r][c - 1] + grid[r][c];   // top row: only from left
            else if (c == 0)      dp[r][c] = dp[r - 1][c] + grid[r][c];   // left column: only from above
            else dp[r][c] = Math.min(dp[r - 1][c], dp[r][c - 1]) + grid[r][c];
        }
    }
    return dp[m - 1][n - 1];
}

uniquePaths(3, 7) = 28; minPathSum([[1,3,1],[1,5,1],[4,2,1]]) = 7 (path 1→3→1→1→1). Both O(m·n) time; space O(n) and O(m·n) (can also be O(n) or done in place). With obstacles (LC 63), set dp to 0 on blocked cells.

Partition Equal Subset Sum (LC 416)

Can the array split into two parts with equal sums? That means: is there a subset summing to total / 2? This is 0/1 knapsack with booleans.

static boolean canPartition(int[] nums) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int x : nums) total += x;
    if (total % 2 != 0) return false;          // odd total can never split evenly
    int target = total / 2;
    boolean[] dp = new boolean[target + 1];    // dp[s] = can some subset sum to s?
    dp[0] = true;                              // the empty subset
    for (int x : nums)
        for (int s = target; s >= x; s--)      // backwards: each number used once
            dp[s] = dp[s] || dp[s - x];
    return dp[target];
}

[1, 5, 11, 5] → true (11 = 1+5+5); [1, 2, 3, 5] → false. Time O(n · sum), space O(sum). Same shape: Target Sum (LC 494) counts subsets with sum (total + target) / 2.

Word Break (LC 139)

  • State: dp[i] = the first i characters can be split into dictionary words.
  • Recurrence: dp[i] is true if some j < i has dp[j] true and s[j..i) is a word.
static boolean wordBreak(String s, List<String> wordDict) {
    Set<String> dict = new HashSet<>(wordDict);
    int n = s.length();
    boolean[] dp = new boolean[n + 1];         // dp[i] = s[0..i) can be segmented
    dp[0] = true;                              // empty prefix
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < i; j++) {
            if (dp[j] && dict.contains(s.substring(j, i))) {
                dp[i] = true;
                break;                         // one valid split is enough
            }
        }
    }
    return dp[n];
}

"leetcode" with ["leet", "code"] → true; "catsandog" with ["cats", "dog", "sand", "and", "cat"] → false. Time O(n^3) in the worst case (n^2 pairs, each substring costs O(n)); limit j to the longest word length to speed it up. Space O(n).

Interval DP: Burst Balloons (LC 312)

Interval DP solves every sub-range [l, r], shortest ranges first, by trying each split point k inside. The trick in Burst Balloons is to let k be the last balloon burst in the range: then its neighbours at that moment are the fixed walls l and r, and the left and right parts become independent.

  • State: dp[l][r] = most coins from bursting every balloon strictly between l and r (padded with 1s at both ends).
  • Recurrence: dp[l][r] = max over k (dp[l][k] + v[l]·v[k]·v[r] + dp[k][r]).
  • Order: by increasing gap r - l.
static int maxCoins(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] v = new int[n + 2];                  // pad with 1 on both ends
    v[0] = v[n + 1] = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) v[i + 1] = nums[i];
    int[][] dp = new int[n + 2][n + 2];
    for (int len = 2; len <= n + 1; len++) {   // gap between the two walls
        for (int l = 0; l + len <= n + 1; l++) {
            int r = l + len;
            for (int k = l + 1; k < r; k++)    // k = LAST balloon burst in (l, r)
                dp[l][r] = Math.max(dp[l][r],
                        dp[l][k] + v[l] * v[k] * v[r] + dp[k][r]);
        }
    }
    return dp[0][n + 1];
}

[3, 1, 5, 8] → 167. Time O(n^3), space O(n^2). Matrix chain multiplication is the same shape: dp[i][j] = min over k (dp[i][k] + dp[k+1][j] + rows[i]·cols[k]·cols[j]) — the cost of splitting the product at k.

DP on trees: House Robber III (LC 337)

Houses form a binary tree; you cannot rob a parent and its child. Each node returns two answers to its parent — best if this node is skipped, best if it is robbed — so the parent can choose. This "return a small array from DFS" is the general tree-DP pattern.

static int robTree(TreeNode root) {
    int[] r = dfs(root);
    return Math.max(r[0], r[1]);
}
// returns {best if we SKIP this node, best if we ROB this node}
private static int[] dfs(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return new int[]{0, 0};
    int[] L = dfs(node.left), R = dfs(node.right);
    int skip = Math.max(L[0], L[1]) + Math.max(R[0], R[1]); // children free to choose
    int rob  = node.val + L[0] + R[0];                      // children must be skipped
    return new int[]{skip, rob};
}

Tree [3, 2, 3, null, 3, null, 1] → 7 (3 + 3 + 1). Time O(n) (each node visited once), space O(h) for the recursion, where h is the tree height.

Bitmask DP: Travelling Salesman for small n

When n is at most about 20, a set of visited items fits in the bits of an int: bit i set means item i is in the set. 1 << n masks times n endpoints is small enough.

  • State: dp[mask][v] = cheapest path that starts at city 0, visits exactly the cities in mask, and ends at v.
  • Transition: from (mask, v) go to an unvisited u: dp[mask | 1<<u][u] = min(…, dp[mask][v] + dist[v][u]).
static int tsp(int[][] dist) {
    int n = dist.length, FULL = 1 << n, INF = Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2;
    int[][] dp = new int[FULL][n];
    for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
    dp[1][0] = 0;                              // start at city 0; mask = {0}
    for (int mask = 1; mask < FULL; mask++) {
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (dp[mask][v] == INF || (mask & (1 << v)) == 0) continue;
            for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
                if ((mask & (1 << u)) != 0) continue;          // already visited
                int next = mask | (1 << u);
                dp[next][u] = Math.min(dp[next][u], dp[mask][v] + dist[v][u]);
            }
        }
    }
    int best = INF;
    for (int v = 1; v < n; v++)                                // return home to 0
        best = Math.min(best, dp[FULL - 1][v] + dist[v][0]);
    return best;
}

For the classic 4-city matrix {{0,10,15,20},{10,0,35,25},{15,35,0,30},{20,25,30,0}} it returns 80. Time O(2^n · n^2), space O(2^n · n) — compared with O(n!) for trying every order. Masks increase numerically, and adding a bit always makes a larger number, so smaller masks are always ready first.

State machine: Buy and Sell Stock

Give each day a few states (holding a share, just sold, resting) and write how you move between them. Each state keeps the best profit so far in that state.

buy (-price) sell (+price) REST ----------------> HOLD ----------------> SOLD ^ \__ rest __/ \__ hold __/ | | | +------------------ cooldown (1 day) --------------+
// One transaction (LC 121): track the cheapest price seen so far. O(n), O(1)
static int maxProfitOne(int[] prices) {
    int minPrice = Integer.MAX_VALUE, best = 0;
    for (int p : prices) {
        minPrice = Math.min(minPrice, p);
        best = Math.max(best, p - minPrice);
    }
    return best;
}

// Unlimited transactions with a 1-day cooldown after selling (LC 309). O(n), O(1)
static int maxProfitCooldown(int[] prices) {
    int hold = Integer.MIN_VALUE / 2;   // own a share
    int sold = Integer.MIN_VALUE / 2;   // just sold today (tomorrow is cooldown)
    int rest = 0;                       // no share, free to buy
    for (int p : prices) {
        int prevHold = hold, prevSold = sold, prevRest = rest;
        hold = Math.max(prevHold, prevRest - p);   // keep holding, or buy from rest
        sold = prevHold + p;                       // sell what we held
        rest = Math.max(prevRest, prevSold);       // stay resting, or cooldown ends
    }
    return Math.max(sold, rest);
}

// Unlimited transactions, fee per sale (LC 714). O(n), O(1)
static int maxProfitFee(int[] prices, int fee) {
    int hold = -prices[0], cash = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i < prices.length; i++) {
        int p = prices[i];
        int newCash = Math.max(cash, hold + p - fee);  // sell today
        int newHold = Math.max(hold, cash - p);        // buy today
        cash = newCash; hold = newHold;
    }
    return cash;
}

Results: [7,1,5,3,6,4] one transaction → 5; [1,2,3,0,2] with cooldown → 3; [1,3,2,8,4,9] with fee 2 → 8. For "at most k transactions" (LC 188), add a dimension: hold[j] and cash[j] for j = 1..k, giving O(n·k).

Pitfalls across all patterns: (1) Using Integer.MAX_VALUE as infinity and then adding 1 — it overflows to a negative number; use amount + 1 or MAX_VALUE / 2. (2) Off-by-one between "first i items" (size n+1) and "index i" (size n) — pick one meaning and write it as a comment. (3) Wrong loop direction in 1D knapsack. (4) Returning dp[n-1] when the answer is the max over all dp[i] (LIS). (5) Forgetting to save updated values at the same time in state machines — copy the old values first.

26. Shortest Paths

In plain words

A shortest path is the route between two nodes with the smallest total weight (the cost written on each edge: distance, time, price). With no weights, "shortest" just means fewest edges.

The central move in every algorithm here is relaxation: "I know a way to reach u costing dist[u]. If going u → v gives v a cheaper cost than it has now, update dist[v]." The algorithms differ only in the order they relax edges.

Water poured at the source spreads through pipes of different lengths. It reaches the nearest junctions first, and each junction is "wet" at the earliest moment any route could get there. Dijkstra simulates this: always process the closest unfinished node next.

Which algorithm when

GraphAlgorithmTimeNotes
Unweighted (all edges cost 1)BFSO(V + E)First time you reach a node is the shortest
Weights only 0 or 10-1 BFS (deque)O(V + E)0-edges to the front, 1-edges to the back
Non-negative weights, one sourceDijkstra (heap)O((V + E) log V)The default choice
Negative weights, one source; or "at most K edges"Bellman-FordO(V · E)Detects negative cycles
All pairs, small V (up to ~400)Floyd-WarshallO(V^3)Handles negative edges; 3 nested loops
DAG (no cycles), any weightsTopological order + relaxO(V + E)See section 27

Dijkstra — the idea

Keep a min-heap of (node, distance). Pop the closest node. Because all weights are non-negative, nothing found later can make it cheaper — its distance is final. Relax its outgoing edges and push any improved neighbours.

heap (node,dist) pop action dist A B C D E [(A,0)] (A,0) B = 4, C = 1 0 4 1 - - [(C,1),(B,4)] (C,1) B: 1+2=3 < 4 update; D = 1+5 = 6 0 3 1 6 - [(B,3),(B,4),(D,6)] (B,3) D: 3+1=4 < 6 update 0 3 1 4 - [(B,4),(D,4),(D,6)] (B,4) stale (4 > dist[B]=3), skip 0 3 1 4 - [(D,4),(D,6)] (D,4) E = 4+3 = 7 0 3 1 4 7 [(D,6),(E,7)] (D,6) stale, skip [(E,7)] (E,7) no out-edges 0 3 1 4 7

Dijkstra — reusable Java template

// graph.get(u) = list of {v, w}. Returns dist[] from src (MAX_VALUE = unreachable).
static int[] dijkstra(List<List<int[]>> graph, int src) {
    int n = graph.size();
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[src] = 0;
    // heap entries are {node, distance}, smallest distance first
    PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    pq.offer(new int[]{src, 0});
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        int[] top = pq.poll();
        int u = top[0], d = top[1];
        if (d > dist[u]) continue;                 // stale entry: a shorter path was already found
        for (int[] e : graph.get(u)) {
            int v = e[0], nd = d + e[1];
            if (nd < dist[v]) {                    // relax
                dist[v] = nd;
                pq.offer(new int[]{v, nd});
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

On the graph above it returns [0, 3, 1, 4, 7]. Time O((V + E) log V): each edge can push once, each push/pop costs log. Space O(V + E).

  • Lazy deletion: Java's PriorityQueue has no fast "decrease key", so we push a new entry and skip the old one when it pops (the d > dist[u] line). Without that check the code is still correct but slower.
  • Use Integer.compare, not a[1] - b[1]: subtraction can overflow with large distances and sort wrongly.
  • Use long distances if a path can exceed about 2.1 billion.
  • For one target, you may stop as soon as the target is popped.

Why Dijkstra fails with negative edges

Dijkstra's promise — "the popped node is final" — assumes a longer route can never become cheaper later. A negative edge breaks that. Take edges A→B 1, A→C 5, C→B -10, B→D 1. The textbook version (mark a node "done" when popped) finalises B = 1 and D = 2, then later finds A→C→B = -5 — but B is already done. Running it gives [0, 1, 5, 2]; the true answer (Bellman-Ford) is [0, -5, 5, -4].

Subtle point: the lazy template above happens to fix this small case because it lets nodes be re-relaxed, but with negative edges it loses its guarantee and can take exponential time. The rule stays simple: negative edges mean Bellman-Ford (or SPFA), never Dijkstra.

0-1 BFS

If every weight is 0 or 1, use a deque instead of a heap: a 0-edge keeps the same distance, so push it to the front; a 1-edge goes to the back. The deque stays sorted, like a heap that never needs log. Typical problem: minimum walls to remove, minimum cost to make a grid path valid (LC 1368).

static int[] zeroOneBfs(List<List<int[]>> graph, int src) {
    int[] dist = new int[graph.size()];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[src] = 0;
    Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dq.offerFirst(src);
    while (!dq.isEmpty()) {
        int u = dq.pollFirst();
        for (int[] e : graph.get(u)) {
            int v = e[0], w = e[1];                // w is 0 or 1
            if (dist[u] + w < dist[v]) {
                dist[v] = dist[u] + w;
                if (w == 0) dq.offerFirst(v);      // free edge: same level, go to front
                else        dq.offerLast(v);
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

Time O(V + E), space O(V).

Bellman-Ford

Relax every edge, V - 1 times. After round k, every shortest path that uses at most k edges is correct. A shortest path without cycles has at most V - 1 edges, so that many rounds suffice. If a V-th round still improves something, a negative cycle exists (you could loop forever and keep getting cheaper).

// edges: {u, v, w}. Returns null if a negative cycle is reachable from src.
static int[] bellmanFord(int n, int[][] edges, int src) {
    final int INF = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (int round = 1; round <= n - 1; round++) {
        boolean changed = false;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (dist[e[0]] != INF && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]]) {   // skip unreachable u
                dist[e[1]] = dist[e[0]] + e[2];
                changed = true;
            }
        }
        if (!changed) break;                               // already settled: stop early
    }
    for (int[] e : edges)                                  // one more round: still improving?
        if (dist[e[0]] != INF && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]])
            return null;                                   // negative cycle
    return dist;
}

Time O(V · E), space O(V). The != INF check matters: INF + negative would look like a real small distance.

Floyd-Warshall (all pairs)

d[i][j] = shortest distance from i to j using only nodes 0..k as stepping stones. Each round of k asks: "is going through k better?" It is really a DP over k.

static long[][] floydWarshall(int n, int[][] edges) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;                   // room to add two INFs safely
    long[][] d = new long[n][n];
    for (long[] row : d) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i][i] = 0;
    for (int[] e : edges) d[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(d[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                  // k MUST be the outermost loop
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                if (d[i][k] + d[k][j] < d[i][j])
                    d[i][j] = d[i][k] + d[k][j];
    return d;                                    // d[i][i] < 0 means i is on a negative cycle
}

Time O(V^3), space O(V^2). Row A of the result for the example graph is 0 3 1 4 7, matching Dijkstra. Putting k in an inner loop is the classic bug — it gives wrong answers silently.

Problem: Network Delay Time (LC 743)

A signal starts at node k; times[i] = {u, v, w}. How long until every node has it? That is the largest shortest distance from k, or -1 if some node is unreachable.

static int networkDelayTime(int[][] times, int n, int k) {
    List<List<int[]>> g = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());   // nodes are 1..n
    for (int[] t : times) g.get(t[0]).add(new int[]{t[1], t[2]});
    int[] dist = new int[n + 1];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[k] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    pq.offer(new int[]{k, 0});
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        int[] top = pq.poll();
        int u = top[0], d = top[1];
        if (d > dist[u]) continue;
        for (int[] e : g.get(u)) {
            if (d + e[1] < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = d + e[1];
                pq.offer(new int[]{e[0], dist[e[0]]});
            }
        }
    }
    int ans = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        if (dist[i] == Integer.MAX_VALUE) return -1;   // someone never gets the signal
        ans = Math.max(ans, dist[i]);
    }
    return ans;
}

times = [[2,1,1],[2,3,1],[3,4,1]], n = 4, k = 2 → 2. Time O(E log V), space O(V + E). Why it works: every node receives the signal at its shortest-path time, so the last one to receive it decides the answer.

Problem: Cheapest Flights Within K Stops (LC 787)

K stops means at most K + 1 flights (edges). Bellman-Ford's "after round r, paths with at most r edges are correct" is exactly this limit — run just K + 1 rounds.

static int findCheapestPrice(int n, int[][] flights, int src, int dst, int k) {
    final int INF = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round <= k; round++) {           // k stops = at most k+1 flights
        int[] next = dist.clone();                       // read ONLY last round's values
        for (int[] f : flights) {
            int u = f[0], v = f[1], price = f[2];
            if (dist[u] != INF && dist[u] + price < next[v])
                next[v] = dist[u] + price;
        }
        dist = next;
    }
    return dist[dst] == INF ? -1 : dist[dst];
}

With flights 0→1 100, 1→2 100, 0→2 500: k = 1 → 200, k = 0 → 500. Time O(K · E), space O(V).

Why the copy? Without dist.clone(), a round can chain several edges (update 1, then immediately use the new 1 to update 2) and exceed the stop limit. Plain Dijkstra on cost also fails here: the cheapest way to reach a middle city may use too many stops, while a pricier route with fewer stops is the one that works.

Problem: Path With Minimum Effort (LC 1631)

On a height grid, a path's effort is the largest height difference between consecutive cells. Minimise it. Dijkstra still works when the path cost is "max of edges" instead of "sum of edges", because extending a path never makes its cost go down.

static int minimumEffortPath(int[][] heights) {
    int R = heights.length, C = heights[0].length;
    int[][] effort = new int[R][C];
    for (int[] row : effort) Arrays.fill(row, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    effort[0][0] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2])); // {r, c, effort}
    pq.offer(new int[]{0, 0, 0});
    int[][] dirs = {{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}};
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        int[] cur = pq.poll();
        int r = cur[0], c = cur[1], e = cur[2];
        if (r == R - 1 && c == C - 1) return e;          // first time popped = final answer
        if (e > effort[r][c]) continue;
        for (int[] d : dirs) {
            int nr = r + d[0], nc = c + d[1];
            if (nr < 0 || nc < 0 || nr >= R || nc >= C) continue;
            int ne = Math.max(e, Math.abs(heights[nr][nc] - heights[r][c])); // cost = max step
            if (ne < effort[nr][nc]) {
                effort[nr][nc] = ne;
                pq.offer(new int[]{nr, nc, ne});
            }
        }
    }
    return 0;
}

[[1,2,2],[3,8,2],[5,3,5]] → 2. Time O(R·C·log(R·C)), space O(R·C). Alternatives: binary search on the answer + BFS, or union-find adding edges in increasing weight until corner meets corner.

Pitfalls: building an undirected graph with edges in only one direction; 1-indexed node labels with a 0-indexed array of size n; comparator overflow; forgetting unreachable nodes (MAX_VALUE) when taking a max or sum.

27. Topological Sort, MST & More

In plain words

A topological sort lists the nodes of a directed graph so that every edge u → v has u before v. It only exists when the graph has no directed cycle — a DAG (directed acyclic graph).

A minimum spanning tree (MST) picks edges of a connected, undirected, weighted graph so that every node is connected, there are no cycles, and the total weight is as small as possible. It always has exactly V - 1 edges.

Topological sort is getting dressed: socks before shoes, shirt before tie before jacket. Any order that respects those rules works. An MST is laying cable between towns: connect every town with the least total cable, and never lay a redundant loop.

Topological sort — Kahn's algorithm (BFS on indegree)

The indegree of a node is how many edges point into it — how many things must happen before it. Start with every node whose indegree is 0 (no prerequisites). Take one, "finish" it, and lower the indegree of everything it points to. Anything that drops to 0 is now ready.

// Edge {u, v} means u must come before v.
// Returns a topological order, or an empty array if there is a cycle.
static int[] topoKahn(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indeg = new int[n];
    for (int[] e : edges) { g.get(e[0]).add(e[1]); indeg[e[1]]++; }

    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (indeg[i] == 0) q.offer(i);   // no prerequisites
    int[] order = new int[n];
    int idx = 0;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = q.poll();
        order[idx++] = u;
        for (int v : g.get(u))
            if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.offer(v);   // its last prerequisite is done
    }
    return idx == n ? order : new int[0];      // fewer than n placed => cycle
}
nodes: 0 shirt, 1 tie, 2 jacket, 3 pants, 4 shoes edges: shirt->tie, tie->jacket, pants->shoes, pants->jacket indegree: shirt 0, tie 1, jacket 2, pants 0, shoes 1 queue [shirt, pants] pop shirt -> tie 1->0, enqueue queue [pants, tie] pop pants -> shoes 1->0, jacket 2->1 queue [tie, shoes] pop tie -> jacket 1->0, enqueue queue [shoes, jacket] pop shoes queue [jacket] pop jacket queue [] order: shirt, pants, tie, shoes, jacket (5 of 5 placed: no cycle)

Running it gives [0, 3, 1, 4, 2]. Time O(V + E), space O(V + E). Cycle detection for free: nodes on a cycle never reach indegree 0, so fewer than V nodes get placed.

Topological sort — DFS with three colours

Colour each node: white = not visited, gray = on the current DFS path (still exploring its descendants), black = completely finished. Reaching a gray node means you walked back into your own path: a cycle. When a node turns black, push it on a stack; the stack from top to bottom is a topological order (reverse post-order).

static final int WHITE = 0, GRAY = 1, BLACK = 2;
static List<List<Integer>> G;
static int[] color;
static Deque<Integer> stack;

static List<Integer> topoDfs(int n, int[][] edges) {
    G = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) G.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) G.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
    color = new int[n];
    stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (color[i] == WHITE && hasCycle(i)) return List.of();   // cycle: no order
    return new ArrayList<>(stack);             // top of stack first
}
// true if a cycle is reachable from u
static boolean hasCycle(int u) {
    color[u] = GRAY;                           // on the current path
    for (int v : G.get(u)) {
        if (color[v] == GRAY) return true;     // back edge => cycle
        if (color[v] == WHITE && hasCycle(v)) return true;
    }
    color[u] = BLACK;                          // fully explored
    stack.push(u);                             // push AFTER all descendants
    return false;
}

On the clothing graph it returns [3, 4, 0, 1, 2] (pants, shoes, shirt, tie, jacket) — a different but equally valid order. Time O(V + E), space O(V) plus recursion depth.

Why two colours are not enough for directed graphs: with just "visited", reaching an already-visited node might be a harmless cross edge (a diamond A→B→D, A→C→D), not a cycle. Only an edge back to a node still on the current path (gray) proves a cycle. In an undirected graph, "visited and not my parent" is enough.

Problem: Course Schedule I & II (LC 207, 210)

prerequisites[i] = {a, b} means "take b before a" — so the edge goes b → a. Getting this direction backwards is the most common bug. Course Schedule I asks "can you finish?" (is it a DAG?); II asks for an order.

static int[] findOrder(int numCourses, int[][] prerequisites) {
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indeg = new int[numCourses];
    for (int[] p : prerequisites) {            // p = {course, prereq}: prereq -> course
        g.get(p[1]).add(p[0]);
        indeg[p[0]]++;
    }
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) if (indeg[i] == 0) q.offer(i);
    int[] order = new int[numCourses];
    int idx = 0;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = q.poll();
        order[idx++] = u;
        for (int v : g.get(u)) if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.offer(v);
    }
    return idx == numCourses ? order : new int[0];
}

static boolean canFinish(int numCourses, int[][] prerequisites) {
    return findOrder(numCourses, prerequisites).length == numCourses;
}

canFinish(2, [[1,0]]) → true; canFinish(2, [[1,0],[0,1]]) → false. findOrder(4, [[1,0],[2,0],[3,1],[3,2]]) → [0, 1, 2, 3]. Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).

Problem idea: Alien Dictionary (LC 269)

Words are sorted in an unknown alphabet; find the letter order. Compare each pair of adjacent words: the first position where they differ tells you one rule, x comes before y. Those rules are edges; the answer is a topological sort of the letters.

static String alienOrder(String[] words) {
    Map<Character, Set<Character>> g = new HashMap<>();
    Map<Character, Integer> indeg = new HashMap<>();
    for (String w : words)
        for (char c : w.toCharArray()) { g.putIfAbsent(c, new HashSet<>()); indeg.putIfAbsent(c, 0); }
    for (int i = 0; i + 1 < words.length; i++) {
        String a = words[i], b = words[i + 1];
        if (a.length() > b.length() && a.startsWith(b)) return "";  // "abc" before "ab": invalid
        for (int j = 0; j < Math.min(a.length(), b.length()); j++) {
            char x = a.charAt(j), y = b.charAt(j);
            if (x != y) {                                           // first difference decides
                if (g.get(x).add(y)) indeg.merge(y, 1, Integer::sum); // count each edge once
                break;
            }
        }
    }
    Deque<Character> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (var e : indeg.entrySet()) if (e.getValue() == 0) q.offer(e.getKey());
    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        char c = q.poll();
        sb.append(c);
        for (char nx : g.get(c)) if (indeg.merge(nx, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) q.offer(nx);
    }
    return sb.length() == indeg.size() ? sb.toString() : "";      // cycle => ""
}

["wrt","wrf","er","ett","rftt"] → "wertf"; ["z","x","z"] → "" (cycle). Time O(total characters), space O(alphabet^2) at most for edges.

Minimum spanning tree

Both MST algorithms rely on the cut property: split the nodes into any two groups; the cheapest edge crossing between them is safe to include in some MST. Kruskal and Prim are two ways of repeatedly finding such an edge.

Kruskal (sort edges + union-find)

Sort all edges by weight. Walk through them cheapest first; take an edge if it connects two different components, skip it if both ends are already connected (it would make a cycle). Union-find answers "already connected?" in almost O(1).

static class DSU {
    int[] parent, rank;
    DSU(int n) {
        parent = new int[n]; rank = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
    }
    int find(int x) {
        if (parent[x] != x) parent[x] = find(parent[x]);   // path compression
        return parent[x];
    }
    boolean union(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;                       // already connected
        if (rank[ra] < rank[rb]) { int t = ra; ra = rb; rb = t; }
        parent[rb] = ra;                                  // union by rank
        if (rank[ra] == rank[rb]) rank[ra]++;
        return true;
    }
}

static int kruskal(int n, int[][] edges) {                // edges {u, v, w}
    int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
    Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));   // cheapest first
    DSU dsu = new DSU(n);
    int cost = 0, used = 0;
    for (int[] e : sorted) {
        if (dsu.union(e[0], e[1])) {                      // joins two different trees?
            cost += e[2];
            if (++used == n - 1) break;                   // a tree has n-1 edges
        }
    }
    return used == n - 1 ? cost : -1;                     // -1: graph is disconnected
}

Returns 11 on the graph above. Time O(E log E) for the sort; space O(V + E). Best for sparse graphs or when edges come as a list.

Prim (grow one tree with a heap)

Start from any node. Repeatedly add the cheapest edge that leaves the current tree. It looks like Dijkstra, but the heap key is the edge weight, not the total distance from the start.

static int prim(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> g = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) {                              // undirected: add both ways
        g.get(e[0]).add(new int[]{e[1], e[2]});
        g.get(e[1]).add(new int[]{e[0], e[2]});
    }
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
    PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1])); // {node, edgeWeight}
    pq.offer(new int[]{0, 0});
    int cost = 0, count = 0;
    while (!pq.isEmpty() && count < n) {
        int[] top = pq.poll();
        int u = top[0];
        if (inTree[u]) continue;                         // already joined via a cheaper edge
        inTree[u] = true;
        cost += top[1];
        count++;
        for (int[] e : g.get(u))
            if (!inTree[e[0]]) pq.offer(new int[]{e[0], e[1]});
    }
    return count == n ? cost : -1;
}

Also 11. Time O(E log V), space O(V + E).

Problem: Min Cost to Connect All Points (LC 1584)

Every pair of points is an edge with cost = Manhattan distance |x1-x2| + |y1-y2|. That is a complete graph with about n^2 / 2 edges, so the array version of Prim — no heap, just scan for the closest point each step — is the best fit: O(n^2) time and only O(n) memory.

static int minCostConnectPoints(int[][] points) {
    int n = points.length, cost = 0;
    int[] best = new int[n];                   // cheapest edge from the tree to each point
    Arrays.fill(best, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
    best[0] = 0;
    for (int step = 0; step < n; step++) {
        int u = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)            // pick the closest point not yet in the tree
            if (!inTree[i] && (u == -1 || best[i] < best[u])) u = i;
        inTree[u] = true;
        cost += best[u];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {          // does u offer anyone a cheaper link?
            if (inTree[v]) continue;
            int d = Math.abs(points[u][0] - points[v][0]) + Math.abs(points[u][1] - points[v][1]);
            if (d < best[v]) best[v] = d;
        }
    }
    return cost;
}

[[0,0],[2,2],[3,10],[5,2],[7,0]] → 20. Why it works: each step adds the cheapest edge crossing the cut between "in tree" and "not in tree", which the cut property says is safe. Kruskal would need to build and sort all n^2 edges: O(n^2 log n) time and O(n^2) memory.

Strongly connected components — Kosaraju in easy words

In a directed graph, a strongly connected component (SCC) is a group where every node can reach every other node. Squash each SCC into one node and the graph becomes a DAG.

  1. DFS the whole graph and record nodes in the order they finish.
  2. Reverse every edge.
  3. Take nodes in reverse finish order; each DFS on the reversed graph from an unvisited node collects exactly one SCC.

Why: the node that finishes last belongs to a "source" component. In the reversed graph, that component cannot leak out into others, so one DFS grabs just it.

static int kosaraju(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>(), rg = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { g.add(new ArrayList<>()); rg.add(new ArrayList<>()); }
    for (int[] e : edges) { g.get(e[0]).add(e[1]); rg.get(e[1]).add(e[0]); }
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    Deque<Integer> finish = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (!seen[i]) dfs1(i, g, seen, finish);  // pass 1: finish order
    Arrays.fill(seen, false);
    int count = 0;
    while (!finish.isEmpty()) {                                          // pass 2: reversed graph
        int u = finish.pop();                                            // latest finisher first
        if (!seen[u]) { dfs2(u, rg, seen); count++; }                    // one SCC per tree
    }
    return count;
}
static void dfs1(int u, List<List<Integer>> g, boolean[] seen, Deque<Integer> finish) {
    seen[u] = true;
    for (int v : g.get(u)) if (!seen[v]) dfs1(v, g, seen, finish);
    finish.push(u);                                  // pushed when u is finished
}
static void dfs2(int u, List<List<Integer>> rg, boolean[] seen) {
    seen[u] = true;
    for (int v : rg.get(u)) if (!seen[v]) dfs2(v, rg, seen);
}

Edges 0→1, 1→2, 2→0, 1→3, 3→4 give 3 SCCs: {0,1,2}, {3}, {4}. Time O(V + E), space O(V + E). Tarjan's algorithm finds SCCs in one pass using the same low values as below.

Bridges and articulation points — Tarjan's idea

In an undirected graph, a bridge is an edge whose removal disconnects the graph; an articulation point is such a node. They show up as "critical connections in a network" (LC 1192), single points of failure, and road networks.

During DFS, give each node a discovery time disc[u] and compute low[u] = the earliest discovery time reachable from u's subtree using at most one back edge. If child v has low[v] > disc[u], nothing under v can climb back above u without the edge u–v: it is a bridge. (For articulation points the test is low[v] >= disc[u], with a special rule for the root: it is one only if it has two or more DFS children.)

static int timer;
static List<List<Integer>> criticalConnections(int n, List<List<Integer>> connections) {
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (List<Integer> c : connections) { g.get(c.get(0)).add(c.get(1)); g.get(c.get(1)).add(c.get(0)); }
    int[] disc = new int[n], low = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(disc, -1);                               // -1 = not visited
    List<List<Integer>> bridges = new ArrayList<>();
    timer = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (disc[i] == -1) bridgeDfs(i, -1, g, disc, low, bridges);
    return bridges;
}
static void bridgeDfs(int u, int parent, List<List<Integer>> g, int[] disc, int[] low, List<List<Integer>> out) {
    disc[u] = low[u] = timer++;
    for (int v : g.get(u)) {
        if (v == parent) continue;                       // don't reuse the edge we came in on
        if (disc[v] == -1) {
            bridgeDfs(v, u, g, disc, low, out);
            low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
            if (low[v] > disc[u]) out.add(List.of(u, v)); // v's subtree can't reach above u
        } else {
            low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);          // back edge
        }
    }
}

A triangle 0-1-2 plus edge 1-3 gives [[1, 3]]. Time O(V + E), space O(V + E). For large inputs, recursion depth can reach V; convert to an iterative DFS or run in a thread with a bigger stack.

Pitfalls: wrong edge direction for prerequisites; using two-colour visited for directed cycle detection; running an MST algorithm on a directed graph (that is a different problem, "arborescence"); forgetting that MST needs a connected graph — check you used V - 1 edges.

28. String Algorithms

In plain words

Pattern matching means finding where a short string (the pattern, length m) appears inside a long one (the text, length n). The simple way re-checks the same characters again and again; the clever algorithms remember what they already matched so they never go backwards in the text.

Looking for "abab" in a book: after reading "aba" and hitting a mismatch, a smart reader does not jump back to the next letter and start over — they notice the "a" they just read could already be the start of a new match, and carry on from there. KMP is exactly that habit, written down as a table.

Naive matching and why it is slow

// Try every start position. O(n·m) worst case, O(1) space
static int naiveSearch(String text, String pat) {
    int n = text.length(), m = pat.length();
    for (int i = 0; i + m <= n; i++) {          // every possible start
        int j = 0;
        while (j < m && text.charAt(i + j) == pat.charAt(j)) j++;
        if (j == m) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

Text "aaaaaaaaab", pattern "aaab": at every start the loop matches three a's, then fails on b, then slides by one and re-reads those same a's. That is up to m comparisons at each of n starts: O(n·m). On random text it is usually fine, and Java's String.indexOf works this way — but interviewers test with the bad case.

KMP (Knuth–Morris–Pratt)

KMP precomputes, for the pattern alone, the LPS array: lps[i] = length of the longest proper prefix of pat[0..i] that is also a suffix of it ("proper" = not the whole string). On a mismatch after matching j characters, we already know the last lps[j-1] characters of the text equal the start of the pattern — so we continue from there, and the text pointer never moves back.

Traced for pattern "ababaca":

icharcompare with pat[len]what happenslps[i]
0a—always 00
1bpat[0] = amismatch, len stays 00
2apat[0] = amatch, len = 1 ("a")1
3bpat[1] = bmatch, len = 2 ("ab")2
4apat[2] = amatch, len = 3 ("aba")3
5cpat[3] = bmismatch: len = lps[2] = 1; pat[1] = b, mismatch: len = lps[0] = 0; pat[0] = a, mismatch0
6apat[0] = amatch, len = 11
// lps[i] = length of the longest proper prefix of p[0..i] that is also a suffix of it
