A cousin of this is the sweep line: split every interval into a "start" event and an "end" event, sort all events by time, and walk through them keeping a running count of how many intervals are open.

Tidying a pile of receipts for trips. Sort them by departure date, then go through the stack: if a trip starts before the previous one ended, staple them together; otherwise start a new bundle.

How to recognise it

  • The input is a list of pairs [start, end]: meetings, bookings, time slots, ranges, balloons.
  • "Merge overlapping", "insert into a sorted list of intervals", "find the free time".
  • "Minimum number of rooms / platforms / machines", "max number of overlapping events at any moment".
  • "Remove the minimum number so the rest don't overlap", "minimum arrows / points to cover all".
  • "Intersection of two lists of intervals".

How it works

Everything rests on one test: when do two intervals overlap? There are six ways B can sit relative to A. Four of them overlap; only "B entirely before A" and "B entirely after A" don't. So the overlap test is simply "neither is entirely before the other":

After sorting by start, cur.start >= last.start is already true, so the test shrinks to one comparison: cur.start <= last.end means overlap. When merging, the new end is max(last.end, cur.end): case 3 (B inside A) shows why you can't just take cur.end.

merge [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] (already sorted by start) [1,3] result [[1,3]] [2,6] 2 <= 3 merge result [[1,6]] [8,10] 8 > 6 new result [[1,6],[8,10]] [15,18] 15 > 10 new result [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]
Sort byWhenExamples
StartYou merge or group intervals togetherMerge Intervals, Meeting Rooms, Insert Interval
EndGreedy: keep as many non-overlapping as possible, or cover with fewest pointsNon-overlapping Intervals, Minimum Arrows, activity selection

Java template

public int[][] sweep(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));  // 1. sort by start
    List<int[]> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        if (result.isEmpty() || result.get(result.size() - 1)[1] < cur[0]) {
            result.add(cur);                         // 2. no overlap: start a new group
        } else {
            int[] last = result.get(result.size() - 1);
            last[1] = Math.max(last[1], cur[1]);     // 3. overlap: CHANGES: merge / count / skip
        }
    }
    return result.toArray(new int[0][]);
}

Sort with Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]), not a[0] - b[0]: subtraction overflows when values are near Integer.MIN_VALUE or MAX_VALUE and gives the wrong order.

Classic problems

Merge Intervals, LC 56 (Medium)

Merge all overlapping intervals and return the non-overlapping result.

Key insight: sort by start; each interval either extends the last merged one or starts a new one. Touching intervals like [1,4] and [4,5] count as overlapping here, hence < for "no overlap".

public int[][] merge(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    List<int[]> merged = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        int[] last = merged.isEmpty() ? null : merged.get(merged.size() - 1);
        if (last == null || last[1] < cur[0]) {
            merged.add(new int[]{cur[0], cur[1]});
        } else {
            last[1] = Math.max(last[1], cur[1]);   // max: cur may sit fully inside last
        }
    }
    return merged.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
// [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] -> [[1, 6], [8, 10], [15, 18]]
// [[1,4],[4,5]]                -> [[1, 5]]
// [[1,10],[2,3],[4,5]]         -> [[1, 10]]

Time O(n log n), Space O(n) for the output (plus the sort's O(log n)).

Insert Interval, LC 57 (Medium)

Intervals are already sorted and non-overlapping. Insert a new one and merge if needed.

Key insight: no sort needed. Three phases: copy everything that ends before the new one starts, swallow everything that overlaps it (growing start and end), copy the rest.

public int[][] insert(int[][] intervals, int[] newInterval) {
    List<int[]> result = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0, n = intervals.length;
    int start = newInterval[0], end = newInterval[1];
    while (i < n && intervals[i][1] < start) result.add(intervals[i++]);   // 1. fully left
    while (i < n && intervals[i][0] <= end) {                              // 2. overlapping
        start = Math.min(start, intervals[i][0]);
        end = Math.max(end, intervals[i][1]);
        i++;
    }
    result.add(new int[]{start, end});
    while (i < n) result.add(intervals[i++]);                              // 3. fully right
    return result.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
// [[1,3],[6,9]], [2,5]                      -> [[1, 5], [6, 9]]
// [[1,2],[3,5],[6,7],[8,10],[12,16]], [4,8] -> [[1, 2], [3, 10], [12, 16]]

Time O(n), Space O(n).

Non-overlapping Intervals, LC 435 (Medium)

Return the minimum number of intervals to remove so the rest don't overlap (touching is allowed).

Key insight: flip it: keep the maximum number of non-overlapping intervals. Sorting by end and always keeping the one that finishes first is the classic greedy: it leaves the most room for everything after.

public int eraseOverlapIntervals(int[][] intervals) {
    // Greedy: keep the interval that ENDS first; it leaves the most room for the rest.
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    int removed = 0, lastEnd = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        if (cur[0] >= lastEnd) {
            lastEnd = cur[1];      // keep it (touching at the edge is fine here)
        } else {
            removed++;             // overlaps the last kept one: remove it
        }
    }
    return removed;
}
// [[1,2],[2,3],[3,4],[1,3]] -> 1
// [[1,2],[1,2],[1,2]]       -> 2
// [[1,2],[2,3]]             -> 0

Time O(n log n), Space O(1) extra.

Meeting Rooms, LC 252 (Easy) and Meeting Rooms II, LC 253 (Medium)

I: can one person attend every meeting? II: what is the minimum number of rooms needed?

Key insight (I): after sorting by start, any meeting that starts before the previous one ends is a clash.

public boolean canAttendMeetings(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    for (int i = 1; i < intervals.length; i++) {
        if (intervals[i][0] < intervals[i - 1][1]) return false;  // starts before previous ends
    }
    return true;
}
// [[0,30],[5,10],[15,20]] -> false,  [[7,10],[2,4]] -> true

Time O(n log n), Space O(1) extra.

Key insight (II), heap: process meetings by start time and keep a min-heap of the end times of rooms in use. If the room that frees up first is free by now, reuse it (poll); either way the new meeting takes a room (offer). The heap's size is the number of rooms.

public int minMeetingRooms(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    PriorityQueue<Integer> endTimes = new PriorityQueue<>();   // end time of each busy room
    for (int[] m : intervals) {
        if (!endTimes.isEmpty() && endTimes.peek() <= m[0]) {
            endTimes.poll();                  // earliest-ending room is free: reuse it
        }
        endTimes.offer(m[1]);
    }
    return endTimes.size();                   // rooms ever opened and still counted
}
// [[0,30],[5,10],[15,20]]    -> 2
// [[7,10],[2,4]]             -> 1
// [[1,5],[2,6],[3,7],[6,8]]  -> 3

Sweep-line version: sort all starts and all ends separately. Walk the starts; before each one, release every meeting that has already ended. The running count's peak is the answer.

public int minMeetingRoomsSweep(int[][] intervals) {
    int n = intervals.length;
    int[] starts = new int[n], ends = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        starts[i] = intervals[i][0];
        ends[i] = intervals[i][1];
    }
    Arrays.sort(starts);
    Arrays.sort(ends);
    int rooms = 0, best = 0, e = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        while (ends[e] <= starts[s]) {        // meetings that ended free their rooms
            rooms--;
            e++;
        }
        rooms++;
        best = Math.max(best, rooms);
    }
    return best;
}
// same answers: 2, 1, 3

Both: Time O(n log n), Space O(n).

Interval List Intersections, LC 986 (Medium)

Two lists of sorted, disjoint intervals. Return all their intersections.

Key insight: two pointers. The intersection of two intervals is [max(starts), min(ends)], valid if lo <= hi. Then advance whichever interval ends first: it can't meet anything else in the other list.

public int[][] intervalIntersection(int[][] first, int[][] second) {
    List<int[]> result = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < first.length && j < second.length) {
        int lo = Math.max(first[i][0], second[j][0]);
        int hi = Math.min(first[i][1], second[j][1]);
        if (lo <= hi) result.add(new int[]{lo, hi});   // they overlap on [lo, hi]
        if (first[i][1] < second[j][1]) i++;           // drop the one that ends first
        else j++;
    }
    return result.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
// first  = [[0,2],[5,10],[13,23],[24,25]]
// second = [[1,5],[8,12],[15,24],[25,26]]
// -> [[1, 2], [5, 5], [8, 10], [15, 23], [24, 24], [25, 25]]

Time O(n + m), Space O(n + m) for the output.

Minimum Number of Arrows to Burst Balloons, LC 452 (Medium)

Balloons are horizontal ranges [xstart, xend]. A vertical arrow at x bursts every balloon with xstart <= x <= xend. Find the fewest arrows.

Key insight: sort by end and shoot each arrow at the end of the first unburst balloon: that's as far right as the arrow can go while still hitting it, so it catches the most later balloons. A balloon that starts after the arrow needs a new one. Touching counts as a hit, hence >.

public int findMinArrowShots(int[][] points) {
    // Compare with Integer.compare: a[1] - b[1] can overflow for values near 2^31.
    Arrays.sort(points, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    int arrows = 1;
    int arrowX = points[0][1];                 // shoot at the end of the first balloon
    for (int[] p : points) {
        if (p[0] > arrowX) {                   // this balloon starts after the arrow: new arrow
            arrows++;
            arrowX = p[1];
        }
    }
    return arrows;
}
// [[10,16],[2,8],[1,6],[7,12]] -> 2
// [[1,2],[3,4],[5,6],[7,8]]    -> 4
// [[1,2],[2,3],[3,4],[4,5]]    -> 2

Time O(n log n), Space O(1) extra.

Common mistakes: forgetting to sort; sorting with a[0] - b[0] (overflow, LC 452 has values near 2^31); setting last.end = cur.end instead of max(last.end, cur.end); getting touching intervals wrong (< vs <=) for the specific problem; sorting by start in the greedy "keep the most" problems, where sorting by end is what makes the greedy correct; in Meeting Rooms II, comparing against the most recent meeting instead of the room that frees up earliest (that's why it's a min-heap).

Practice list

  • LC 228 Summary Ranges (Easy)
  • LC 1854 Maximum Population Year (Easy)
  • LC 1288 Remove Covered Intervals (Medium)
  • LC 646 Maximum Length of Pair Chain (Medium)
  • LC 729 My Calendar I (Medium)
  • LC 1094 Car Pooling (Medium) — sweep line / difference array
  • LC 759 Employee Free Time (Hard)
  • LC 2402 Meeting Rooms III (Hard)

36. Cyclic Sort

In plain words

When an array holds numbers from a known small range, like 1 to n, every number has a natural home: value v belongs at index v - 1. Cyclic sort walks the array and keeps swapping the current number into its home until the current slot holds the right number (or a number that has no home). Then it moves on.

After that, one more scan finds whatever is wrong: a slot whose number doesn't match its index points straight to a missing number (the index) or a duplicate (the value in it). All in O(n) time and O(1) extra space, with no HashSet and no real sorting.

Handing back numbered exam papers in a classroom where seat 1 is for student 1, seat 2 for student 2, and so on. Pick up the paper on the first desk, walk it to its owner's seat, pick up the paper that was lying there, walk that one to its seat, and so on. Empty seats at the end tell you who was absent.

How to recognise it

  • The array contains numbers in the range 1..n or 0..n, where n is (about) the array length.
  • "Find the missing number(s)", "find the duplicate(s)", "find the smallest missing positive", "set mismatch".
  • "O(1) extra space" and "O(n) time", ruling out both a HashSet and sorting.
  • You are allowed to modify the input array (if not, look at fast & slow pointers or bit tricks instead).

How it works

Stay on index i and keep swapping nums[i] to its home until the value at i is at home, or its home already holds the same value (a duplicate), or it has no home (out of range). Only then do i++.

Why is it O(n) with a while that doesn't always move i? Every swap puts at least one number into its final home, and a number at home is never moved again. So there are at most n swaps in total, plus n increments of i: under 2n steps.

The check nums[i] != nums[correct] (compare the values, not i != correct) is what stops an infinite loop on duplicates: if the home already holds the same value, swapping would change nothing, forever.

Java template

public void placeEach(int[] nums) {
    int i = 0;
    while (i < nums.length) {
        int correct = nums[i] - 1;                 // CHANGES: home index (value - 1, or value)
        if (inRange(nums[i], nums.length)          // CHANGES: skip values with no home
                && nums[i] != nums[correct]) {     // home not already holding this value
            swap(nums, i, correct);                // send it home; DON'T move i yet
        } else {
            i++;                                   // placed, duplicate, or out of range
        }
    }
    // Then scan: the first index i with nums[i] != i + 1 tells you what's missing / extra.
}

private boolean inRange(int v, int n) { return v >= 1 && v <= n; }

private void swap(int[] a, int i, int j) {
    int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[j]; a[j] = t;
}
After sorting, if nums[i] != i + 1It means
i + 1is a missing number
nums[i]is a duplicate (its real home is taken by another copy)

Classic problems

Cyclic Sort (Easy)

An array holds each number from 1 to n exactly once, in any order. Sort it in place in O(n). (The core loop behind every problem below; it uses swap from the template.)

Key insight: you already know where each value goes, so you don't need comparisons between elements: just send each one home.

public void cyclicSort(int[] nums) {        // nums holds each of 1..n exactly once
    int i = 0;
    while (i < nums.length) {
        int correct = nums[i] - 1;
        if (nums[i] != nums[correct]) swap(nums, i, correct);
        else i++;
    }
}
// [3,1,5,4,2]   -> [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
// [2,6,4,3,1,5] -> [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Missing Number, LC 268 (Easy)

nums holds n distinct numbers from 0..n. Return the one that is missing.

Key insight: here the range starts at 0, so value v lives at index v. The value n has no slot, so leave it wherever it lands. The first index that doesn't hold its own number is the answer; if all match, the answer is n.

public int missingNumber(int[] nums) {      // n numbers from 0..n, one missing
    int i = 0, n = nums.length;
    while (i < n) {
        int correct = nums[i];                          // value v belongs at index v
        if (correct < n && nums[i] != nums[correct]) swap(nums, i, correct);
        else i++;                                       // value n has no slot: leave it
    }
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i) return i;
    }
    return n;                                           // 0..n-1 all present
}
// [3,0,1] -> 2,  [0,1] -> 2,  [9,6,4,2,3,5,7,0,1] -> 8

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Worth knowing: the XOR trick solves this one without touching the array, since x ^ x = 0:

public int missingNumberXor(int[] nums) {
    int x = nums.length;                    // start with n
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) x ^= i ^ nums[i];
    return x;                               // every present number cancels itself
}
// [9,6,4,2,3,5,7,0,1] -> 8

Find All Numbers Disappeared in an Array, LC 448 (Easy)

n numbers, each in 1..n, some appearing twice. Return all numbers in 1..n that don't appear.

Key insight: run cyclic sort. Duplicates get stuck in slots whose owner is missing, so every mismatched index i means i + 1 is missing.

public List<Integer> findDisappearedNumbers(int[] nums) {
    cyclicSort(nums);   // same loop; duplicates just stop when their home is taken
    List<Integer> missing = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) missing.add(i + 1);
    }
    return missing;
}
// [4,3,2,7,8,2,3,1] -> [5, 6]
// [1,1]             -> [2]

Time O(n), Space O(1) extra (the output list doesn't count).

Find the Duplicate Number, LC 287 (Medium)

n + 1 numbers, each in 1..n, one value repeated. Return it.

Key insight: while sending a number home, if its home already holds the same value, you've found the duplicate. Note: LC 287 forbids modifying the array, so in that exact problem use Floyd's cycle detection; the cyclic-sort version is the right answer when changes are allowed.

public int findDuplicate(int[] nums) {      // n + 1 numbers from 1..n
    int i = 0;
    while (i < nums.length) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) {             // not at home
            int correct = nums[i] - 1;
            if (nums[i] == nums[correct]) return nums[i];   // home already taken by a copy
            swap(nums, i, correct);
        } else {
            i++;
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
// [1,3,4,2,2] -> 2,  [3,1,3,4,2] -> 3,  [3,3,3,3,3] -> 3

Time O(n), Space O(1).

First Missing Positive, LC 41 (Hard)

Unsorted array of any integers. Return the smallest positive integer that is not present, in O(n) time and O(1) space.

Key insight: with n slots, the answer is always in 1..n+1. So only values 1..n matter: send them home and ignore negatives, zeros and big values. The first slot not holding i + 1 is the answer.

public int firstMissingPositive(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length, i = 0;
    while (i < n) {
        int v = nums[i];
        // only 1..n can matter: the answer is always in 1..n+1
        if (v >= 1 && v <= n && nums[v - 1] != v) swap(nums, i, v - 1);
        else i++;
    }
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) return i + 1;
    }
    return n + 1;
}
// [1,2,0] -> 3,  [3,4,-1,1] -> 2,  [7,8,9,11,12] -> 1,  [1,1] -> 2

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Set Mismatch, LC 645 (Easy)

The set 1..n got corrupted: one number was copied over another. Return [duplicate, missing].

Key insight: after cyclic sort there is exactly one mismatched slot: it holds the duplicate, and its index + 1 is the missing number.

public int[] findErrorNums(int[] nums) {    // one number duplicated, one missing
    cyclicSort(nums);
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) return new int[]{nums[i], i + 1};  // {duplicate, missing}
    }
    return new int[]{-1, -1};
}
// [1,2,2,4]     -> [2, 3]
// [1,1]         -> [1, 2]
// [3,2,3,4,6,5] -> [3, 1]

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Common mistakes: writing if (i != correct) instead of if (nums[i] != nums[correct]), which loops forever on duplicates; doing i++ after a swap (the new value at i hasn't been placed yet); an off-by-one between "value v at index v" (range 0..n) and "value v at index v - 1" (range 1..n); forgetting to skip values that have no home (0, negatives, values > n), causing ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException; using it when the problem says the array must not be modified.

Practice list

  • LC 1920 Build Array from Permutation (Easy)
  • LC 1539 Kth Missing Positive Number (Easy)
  • LC 442 Find All Duplicates in an Array (Medium)
  • LC 565 Array Nesting (Medium) — follows the same "index to value" cycles
  • LC 2471 Minimum Number of Operations to Sort a Binary Tree by Level (Medium) — count swaps with cycles
  • LC 765 Couples Holding Hands (Hard) — swap each partner into place

37. In-place Linked List Reversal

In plain words

To reverse a linked list you don't copy anything: you walk along it once and flip each arrow so it points backwards. The only difficulty is that the moment you flip curr.next, you lose your way to the rest of the list. So you keep three pointers: prev (the part already reversed), curr (the node being flipped) and next (a bookmark to the rest). That "three-pointer dance" is the whole pattern.

Harder problems reverse only part of the list (a sublist, every group of k, every pair), and the extra work is all about reconnecting the reversed piece to the nodes before and after it. A dummy node in front of the head makes that reconnection the same everywhere, even at the very start.

Turning around a line of people holding the shoulder of the person in front. You go down the line and ask each person to let go and hold the person behind instead. Before they let go, you note who was in front of them, so you know who to ask next.

How to recognise it

  • "Reverse a linked list", "reverse from position left to right", "reverse every k nodes", "swap nodes in pairs".
  • "Reorder", "rotate", "fold" a list, or "compare the list with its reverse" (palindrome list).
  • "Add two numbers" where digits are stored most-significant first (reverse, add, reverse back).
  • "Do it in place", "O(1) extra memory": no ArrayList of nodes, no stack.

How it works

Every loop step does the same four moves in the same order: save next = curr.next, flip curr.next = prev, advance prev = curr, advance curr = next. When curr falls off the end, prev is sitting on the old tail, which is the new head.

Useful facts after a reversal loop: prev is the new head of the reversed piece, curr is the first node that was not reversed, and the node you started from is now the tail of the reversed piece. Keeping a reference to that starting node is how you reconnect in the sublist and k-group problems.

Sublist reversal (reverse positions 2..4 of 1->2->3->4->5), head-insertion style: before = node 1, first = node 2 (it will end up as the sublist's tail) move 3 to the front: 1 -> 3 -> 2 -> 4 -> 5 move 4 to the front: 1 -> 4 -> 3 -> 2 -> 5 (right - left = 2 moves, done)

Java template

The node class used in this section (the LeetCode one):

class ListNode {
    int val;
    ListNode next;
    ListNode(int val) { this.val = val; }
    ListNode(int val, ListNode next) { this.val = val; this.next = next; }
}
// Reverse the list starting at head; returns the new head.
ListNode reverse(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null;          // already-reversed part (starts empty)
    ListNode curr = head;          // node we are about to flip
    while (curr != null) {         // CHANGES: stop after k nodes / at a given node
        ListNode next = curr.next; // 1. save the rest of the list
        curr.next = prev;          // 2. flip the arrow
        prev = curr;               // 3. grow the reversed part
        curr = next;               // 4. step forward
    }
    return prev;                   // prev = new head; curr = first node NOT reversed
}

For partial reversals add a dummy node: ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);, work from dummy, and return dummy.next. Then "the node before the reversed piece" always exists, even when the piece starts at the head.

Classic problems

Reverse Linked List, LC 206 (Easy)

Reverse a singly linked list and return the new head.

Key insight: the template, exactly. Know the recursive version too: interviewers often ask for both.

public ListNode reverseList(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null, curr = head;
    while (curr != null) {
        ListNode next = curr.next;
        curr.next = prev;
        prev = curr;
        curr = next;
    }
    return prev;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> [5, 4, 3, 2, 1],  [1,2] -> [2, 1],  [] -> []

Time O(n), Space O(1).

public ListNode reverseListRecursive(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;  // 0 or 1 node: already reversed
    ListNode newHead = reverseListRecursive(head.next);   // reverse everything after head
    head.next.next = head;     // the node after head now points back to head
    head.next = null;          // head becomes the tail
    return newHead;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> [5, 4, 3, 2, 1]

Time O(n), Space O(n) for the call stack, so the iterative version is preferred (and a list of 100,000 nodes can overflow the stack recursively).

Reverse Linked List II, LC 92 (Medium)

Reverse only the nodes from position left to right (1-indexed), in one pass.

Key insight: walk to the node before position left (a dummy makes this work when left = 1). Then do right - left "head insertions": take the node after first and move it to the front of the sublist. first slides toward the end on its own and stays connected to the rest.

public ListNode reverseBetween(ListNode head, int left, int right) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode before = dummy;
    for (int i = 1; i < left; i++) before = before.next;   // node just before position left

    // Head insertion: repeatedly move the node after 'first' to the front of the sublist.
    ListNode first = before.next;                          // becomes the sublist's tail
    for (int i = 0; i < right - left; i++) {
        ListNode moved = first.next;
        first.next = moved.next;
        moved.next = before.next;
        before.next = moved;
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], left=2, right=4 -> [1, 4, 3, 2, 5]
// [5], 1, 1 -> [5],  [3,5], 1, 2 -> [5, 3]

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Reverse Nodes in k-Group, LC 25 (Hard)

Reverse the list k nodes at a time. A final group with fewer than k nodes stays as it is.

Key insight: for each group, first check that k nodes exist (find the k-th). Reverse the group with prev starting at groupNext instead of null: that way the group's new tail already points at the next part. Then hook the previous part to the group's new head (kth) and move groupPrev to the group's new tail.

public ListNode reverseKGroup(ListNode head, int k) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode groupPrev = dummy;                 // node before the current group
    while (true) {
        ListNode kth = groupPrev;               // find the k-th node of this group
        for (int i = 0; i < k && kth != null; i++) kth = kth.next;
        if (kth == null) break;                 // fewer than k nodes left: leave them

        ListNode groupNext = kth.next;          // first node after the group
        ListNode prev = groupNext, curr = groupPrev.next;
        while (curr != groupNext) {             // standard reversal, but prev starts at
            ListNode next = curr.next;          // groupNext so the tail links onward
            curr.next = prev;
            prev = curr;
            curr = next;
        }
        ListNode oldFirst = groupPrev.next;     // it is now the group's last node
        groupPrev.next = kth;                   // kth is the group's new first node
        groupPrev = oldFirst;
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], k=2 -> [2, 1, 4, 3, 5]
// [1,2,3,4,5], k=3 -> [3, 2, 1, 4, 5]
// [1,2,3],     k=1 -> [1, 2, 3]

Time O(n) (each node is visited twice: once to count, once to reverse), Space O(1).

Swap Nodes in Pairs, LC 24 (Medium)

Swap every two adjacent nodes (change links, not values).

Key insight: it's k-group with k = 2, small enough to write the three pointer changes by hand. Draw prev -> a -> b -> rest and rewire to prev -> b -> a -> rest.

public ListNode swapPairs(ListNode head) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode prev = dummy;
    while (prev.next != null && prev.next.next != null) {
        ListNode a = prev.next, b = a.next;
        a.next = b.next;        // a points past the pair
        b.next = a;             // b points back to a
        prev.next = b;          // the node before the pair now points to b
        prev = a;               // a is now second: it's the "before" of the next pair
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4] -> [2, 1, 4, 3],  [1,2,3] -> [2, 1, 3],  [] -> []

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Rotate List, LC 61 (Medium)

Rotate the list to the right by k places.

Key insight: no reversal needed, just relinking. Find the length n and the tail, reduce k %= n (k can be huge), join tail to head to make a ring, then cut the ring after the (n - k)-th node.

public ListNode rotateRight(ListNode head, int k) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    int n = 1;
    ListNode tail = head;
    while (tail.next != null) { tail = tail.next; n++; }   // length and tail
    k %= n;
    if (k == 0) return head;
    tail.next = head;                        // close into a ring
    ListNode newTail = head;
    for (int i = 1; i < n - k; i++) newTail = newTail.next;  // (n - k)-th node
    ListNode newHead = newTail.next;
    newTail.next = null;                     // cut the ring
    return newHead;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], k=2 -> [4, 5, 1, 2, 3]
// [0,1,2],     k=4 -> [2, 0, 1]

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Reorder List, LC 143 (Medium)

Reorder L0 -> L1 -> ... -> Ln into L0 -> Ln -> L1 -> Ln-1 -> ... in place.

Key insight: three patterns glued together: fast & slow to find the middle, reverse the second half (the template's reverse), then weave the two halves, one node from each.

public void reorderList(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return;
    // 1. middle (first middle, so the first half is not shorter)
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast.next != null && fast.next.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
    }
    // 2. reverse the second half and cut it off
    ListNode second = reverse(slow.next);
    slow.next = null;
    // 3. weave: one from the front, one from the back
    ListNode first = head;
    while (second != null) {
        ListNode n1 = first.next, n2 = second.next;
        first.next = second;
        second.next = n1;
        first = n1;
        second = n2;
    }
}
// [1,2,3,4]   -> [1, 4, 2, 3]
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> [1, 5, 2, 4, 3]

Time O(n), Space O(1).

Common mistakes: flipping curr.next before saving it (the rest of the list is lost); returning head or curr instead of prev; forgetting that the old head is now the tail and must point to null (or to the next part), which creates a cycle; not using a dummy node, so the case "reverse starting at the head" needs special code; in k-group, reversing a final group that has fewer than k nodes; in Rotate List, forgetting k %= n. Tip: draw the boxes and arrows before coding, and trace a 2-node and a 3-node list by hand.

Practice list

  • LC 234 Palindrome Linked List (Easy)
  • LC 2130 Maximum Twin Sum of a Linked List (Medium)
  • LC 445 Add Two Numbers II (Medium)
  • LC 328 Odd Even Linked List (Medium)
  • LC 1721 Swapping Nodes in a Linked List (Medium)
  • LC 2074 Reverse Nodes in Even Length Groups (Medium)
  • LC 86 Partition List (Medium)
  • LC 2816 Double a Number Represented as a Linked List (Medium)

38. Tree BFS & DFS

In plain words

Almost every binary tree question is solved by walking the tree in one of two ways. BFS (breadth-first search) visits the tree level by level, top to bottom, using a queue. DFS (depth-first search) goes as deep as possible down one branch before coming back, using recursion (the call stack).

The pattern is not "know BFS and DFS" — you already do from section 12. The pattern is knowing which one fits and having a template ready: BFS when the question talks about levels or "closest to the root"; DFS when it talks about paths, depths, or answers built from subtrees.

BFS is a building inspector who checks every room on floor 1, then every room on floor 2, and so on. DFS is a person exploring a cave: follow one tunnel to its end, come back to the last fork, try the next tunnel.

How to recognise it

  • "Level order", "each level", "row by row", "average / max of each level", "zigzag" — BFS.
  • "Right side view", "left view", "nodes you can see", "minimum depth" (first leaf you reach) — BFS (stop at the first answer).
  • "Root-to-leaf path", "path sum", "all paths", "sum of numbers along the path" — DFS top-down (carry the path/sum down).
  • "Height", "diameter", "balanced?", "maximum path sum", "subtree ..." — DFS bottom-up (children report up, parent combines).
  • "Good nodes", "max so far on the path from root", "ancestor" — DFS carrying state down.

How it works

BFS with a size snapshot. Put the root in a queue. At the start of each round, write down size = queue.size(). Exactly those size nodes belong to the current level — the children you add while processing them wait for the next round. This one line is what turns "BFS" into "BFS that knows where each level ends".

DFS: two styles. Decide which direction information flows.

StyleInformation flowsYou writeUse for
Top-down (preorder thinking)From parent to child, through parametersvoid dfs(node, stateSoFar)Path sums, max-so-far on the path, depth of each node, building a path list
Bottom-up (postorder thinking)From children to parent, through return valuesint dfs(node) returns an answer for that subtreeHeight, diameter, balanced check, max path sum, subtree sums

Trace of bottom-up height on the same tree: h(15)=1, h(7)=1, so h(20)=1+max(1,1)=2; h(9)=1; h(3)=1+max(1,2)=3. Each node is visited once, and the answer "bubbles up" to the root.

Java template

List<List<Integer>> levelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return result;
    Deque<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();              // snapshot: nodes on THIS level
        List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            level.add(node.val);              // CHANGES: what you do with each node
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        result.add(level);                    // CHANGES: what you keep per level
    }
    return result;
}
// Top-down: pass state DOWN as parameters (depth, path, running sum, max so far)
int best = 0;
void topDown(TreeNode node, int depth) {
    if (node == null) return;
    if (node.left == null && node.right == null)
        best = Math.max(best, depth);         // CHANGES: act at a leaf / at each node
    topDown(node.left, depth + 1);            // CHANGES: how state is updated
    topDown(node.right, depth + 1);
}

// Bottom-up: children RETURN answers, parent combines them
int bottomUp(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;               // CHANGES: value for an empty tree
    int left = bottomUp(node.left);
    int right = bottomUp(node.right);
    return 1 + Math.max(left, right);         // CHANGES: how to combine
}
Which DFS style? Ask: "can a node decide its answer from what its ancestors tell it?" Then top-down. "Does a node need to know something about its whole subtree first?" Then bottom-up. Many hard problems use both: state comes down in parameters, results go up in the return value (see Count Good Nodes).

Classic problems

Binary Tree Level Order Traversal — LC 102 (Medium)

Return the node values level by level: [3,9,20,null,null,15,7] gives [[3],[9,20],[15,7]].

Key insight: this is the BFS template above, unchanged. Everything else in this section is a small edit of it.

Time O(n), Space O(w) where w is the widest level (up to n/2 for a full tree).

Binary Tree Zigzag Level Order Traversal — LC 103 (Medium)

Like level order, but alternate direction: left-to-right, then right-to-left. Same tree gives [[3],[20,9],[15,7]].

Key insight: do not change the order you visit nodes — that gets messy. Visit normally and just fill the level list from the front on odd levels.

List<List<Integer>> zigzagLevelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return result;
    Deque<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    boolean leftToRight = true;
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();
        LinkedList<Integer> level = new LinkedList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            if (leftToRight) level.addLast(node.val);
            else level.addFirst(node.val);    // fill backwards on odd levels
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        result.add(level);
        leftToRight = !leftToRight;
    }
    return result;
}

Time O(n), Space O(w).

Binary Tree Right Side View — LC 199 (Medium)

Return the values you would see standing on the right of the tree. [1,2,3,null,5,null,4] gives [1,3,4].

Key insight: "what you see from the right" = the last node of each level. A common wrong answer is "keep going right" — that misses a left child that sticks out lower than every right node (the 5 above would be missed if 4 were absent).

List<Integer> rightSideView(TreeNode root) {
    List<Integer> view = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return view;
    Deque<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            if (i == size - 1) view.add(node.val);   // last node of the level
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
    }
    return view;
}

Time O(n), Space O(w). (A DFS alternative: visit right child first and record the first node you reach at each new depth.)

Path Sum II — LC 113 (Medium)

Return every root-to-leaf path whose values add up to targetSum. For [5,4,8,11,null,13,4,7,2,null,null,5,1] and target 22 the answer is [[5,4,11,2],[5,8,4,5]].

Key insight: top-down DFS carrying the remaining sum and the current path. Add the node on the way in, remove it on the way out (backtracking), and copy the path when you save it.

List<List<Integer>> pathSum(TreeNode root, int targetSum) {
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    dfs(root, targetSum, new ArrayList<>(), result);
    return result;
}

private void dfs(TreeNode node, int remaining, List<Integer> path,
                 List<List<Integer>> result) {
    if (node == null) return;
    path.add(node.val);                                  // choose
    remaining -= node.val;
    if (node.left == null && node.right == null && remaining == 0)
        result.add(new ArrayList<>(path));               // copy, not the live list
    dfs(node.left, remaining, path, result);
    dfs(node.right, remaining, path, result);
    path.remove(path.size() - 1);                        // un-choose (backtrack)
}

Time O(n²) worst case (n nodes, and copying a path of up to n values at each leaf), Space O(h) for recursion plus the path.

Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum — LC 124 (Hard)

A path is any chain of connected nodes (it need not pass through the root and can bend once at some node). Find the largest sum. [-10,9,20,null,null,15,7] gives 42 (15 → 20 → 7).

Key insight: bottom-up DFS where each node answers two different questions:

  • Returned to the parent: the best "one-armed" path starting here and going down one side — because a parent can only extend a path that doesn't already bend.
  • Recorded in a global: the best path that bends here: node + left + right.

Negative branches are clamped to 0 ("don't take that branch").

int maxSum = Integer.MIN_VALUE;

int maxPathSum(TreeNode root) {
    gain(root);
    return maxSum;
}

// returns the best sum of a path that STARTS at node and goes down one side
private int gain(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int left = Math.max(0, gain(node.left));    // drop negative branches
    int right = Math.max(0, gain(node.right));
    maxSum = Math.max(maxSum, node.val + left + right);  // path bends here
    return node.val + Math.max(left, right);    // parent can use only one side
}

Time O(n), Space O(h). Start maxSum at Integer.MIN_VALUE, not 0 — for the single-node tree [-3] the answer is -3.

Count Good Nodes in Binary Tree — LC 1448 (Medium)

A node is "good" if no node on the path from the root to it has a bigger value. [3,1,4,3,null,1,5] gives 4.

Key insight: pass the max seen so far down (top-down), and add up the counts on the way back (bottom-up) — both styles in one function.

int goodNodes(TreeNode root) {
    return count(root, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
}

private int count(TreeNode node, int maxSoFar) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int good = node.val >= maxSoFar ? 1 : 0;       // top-down: state flows in
    int newMax = Math.max(maxSoFar, node.val);
    return good + count(node.left, newMax) + count(node.right, newMax);  // bottom-up sum
}

Time O(n), Space O(h).

Common mistakes: (1) Reading queue.size() inside the for condition — it changes as you add children; snapshot it first. (2) Adding the live path list to the result instead of new ArrayList<>(path) — every saved answer ends up empty. (3) Forgetting to remove the last element after recursing (the "un-choose" step). (4) Treating "leaf" as "node == null" — a leaf is a node with both children null; checking null fires twice per leaf and wrongly counts nodes with one child. (5) Initialising a max to 0 when all values can be negative. (6) Recursion depth: a skewed tree of 105 nodes can overflow the Java stack; mention BFS or an explicit stack as the fix.

  • LC 111 Minimum Depth of Binary Tree (Easy) — BFS, stop at the first leaf
  • LC 112 Path Sum (Easy)
  • LC 637 Average of Levels in Binary Tree (Easy)
  • LC 116 Populating Next Right Pointers in Each Node (Medium)
  • LC 129 Sum Root to Leaf Numbers (Medium)
  • LC 236 Lowest Common Ancestor of a Binary Tree (Medium)
