45. Design Problems

In plain words

"Design a data structure" questions give you a class with a few methods and a required speed for each — usually O(1) or O(log n). No single built-in structure meets every requirement, so you combine two: one that is fast at finding things (a HashMap) plus one that keeps the order or ranking you need (a linked list, a heap, a TreeMap, an array list).

The recipe: list each operation and its target cost, pick a structure that gives each one, then make sure every structure stays in sync on every write.

A library with a card catalogue and shelves. The catalogue (hash map) tells you exactly where a book is in one step; the shelves (the list) keep books in an order. Neither alone is enough — the catalogue has no order, and scanning shelves is slow.

How to recognise it

  • "Implement the XYZ class" with methods like get, put, push, getMin, getRandom.
  • "Each operation must run in O(1) average time" — hash map plus something ordered.
  • "Least recently used", "least frequently used", "evict" — cache design.
  • "Most recent 10", "news feed", "timeline" — heap merge of per-user lists.
  • "Value at a given timestamp", "latest version <= t" — TreeMap.floorEntry or binary search.

How it works

You needReach for
Find by key in O(1)HashMap
Move / remove any known item in O(1), keep an orderDoubly linked list (with the node stored in the map)
Pick a random item in O(1)ArrayList (index access) + map from value to index
Min / max so far, with undoStack of pairs (value, min at that point)
Top k / most recent across many listsPriorityQueue (k-way merge, section 39)
Nearest key <= x or >= xTreeMap: floorKey, ceilingKey (section 14)
Prefix lookupsTrie (section 15)

Java template

class Design {
    // 1. One field per requirement, e.g.
    //    Map<Key, Node> index;     -> O(1) find
    //    ordering structure        -> list / heap / TreeMap
    // 2. Every public method:
    //    a. look up via the map
    //    b. update the ordering structure
    //    c. update the map (add / remove) so both agree
    // 3. Private helpers for the fiddly pointer work: remove(node), addFirst(node)
}

Classic problems

LRU Cache — LC 146 (Medium)

Fixed capacity. get(key) returns the value or -1; put(key, value) inserts or updates, and when full evicts the least recently used key. Both in O(1).

Key insight: a doubly linked list keeps keys in "recently used" order: the front is most recent, the back is the next to evict. Moving a node to the front, or removing the back, is O(1) — if you can find the node instantly. The HashMap from key to node gives that. Two sentinel nodes (dummy head and tail) remove every null check.

class LRUCache {
    private class Node {
        int key, val;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int k, int v) { key = k; val = v; }
    }

    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Node> map = new HashMap<>();   // key -> node: O(1) find
    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0);                 // sentinel: most recent side
    private final Node tail = new Node(0, 0);                 // sentinel: least recent side

    public LRUCache(int capacity) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    public int get(int key) {
        Node node = map.get(key);
        if (node == null) return -1;
        remove(node);                         // touched: move to the front
        addFirst(node);
        return node.val;
    }

    public void put(int key, int value) {
        Node node = map.get(key);
        if (node != null) {                   // update existing
            node.val = value;
            remove(node);
            addFirst(node);
            return;
        }
        if (map.size() == capacity) {         // full: evict least recently used
            Node lru = tail.prev;
            remove(lru);
            map.remove(lru.key);              // this is why Node stores its key
        }
        Node fresh = new Node(key, value);
        addFirst(fresh);
        map.put(key, fresh);
    }

    private void remove(Node n) {             // unlink: O(1) because we know prev
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    private void addFirst(Node n) {           // insert right after head
        n.next = head.next;
        n.prev = head;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }
}

Capacity 2: put(1,1), put(2,2), get(1) returns 1; put(3,3) evicts key 2; get(2) returns -1; put(4,4) evicts key 1; then get(1), get(3), get(4) return -1, 3, 4. Time O(1) per operation, Space O(capacity).

Why each node stores its key: when you evict tail.prev, you must also delete it from the map — and the map is keyed by key.

The LinkedHashMap shortcut. Java's LinkedHashMap is a hash map plus a doubly linked list. Constructed with accessOrder = true, every get moves the entry to the end; override removeEldestEntry to evict. Mention it — then offer to write the manual version, which is what most interviewers want to see.

class LRUCacheShort extends LinkedHashMap<Integer, Integer> {
    private final int capacity;

    LRUCacheShort(int capacity) {
        super(16, 0.75f, true);               // true = ordered by ACCESS, not insertion
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    public int get(int key) { return getOrDefault(key, -1); }

    public void put(int key, int value) { super.put(key, value); }

    @Override
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> eldest) {
        return size() > capacity;             // called after every put: evict if too big
    }
}

LFU Cache — LC 460 (Hard)

Evict the key used the fewest times; break ties by least recently used. All in O(1).

Key idea: group keys by use count. buckets[f] is an insertion-ordered set of keys used exactly f times (oldest first), and minFreq tracks the smallest non-empty count. Using a key moves it from bucket f to f+1; if bucket f was the minimum and is now empty, minFreq++. A new key always has count 1, so it resets minFreq = 1. Evict the first key in buckets[minFreq].

class LFUCache {
    private final int capacity;
    private int minFreq = 0;
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> vals = new HashMap<>();     // key -> value
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();     // key -> use count
    // use count -> keys with that count, in LRU order (LinkedHashSet keeps insertion order)
    private final Map<Integer, LinkedHashSet<Integer>> buckets = new HashMap<>();

    LFUCache(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

    public int get(int key) {
        if (!vals.containsKey(key)) return -1;
        touch(key);
        return vals.get(key);
    }

    public void put(int key, int value) {
        if (capacity == 0) return;
        if (vals.containsKey(key)) {
            vals.put(key, value);
            touch(key);
            return;
        }
        if (vals.size() == capacity) {        // evict: lowest count, then oldest
            Iterator<Integer> it = buckets.get(minFreq).iterator();
            int victim = it.next();
            it.remove();
            vals.remove(victim);
            freq.remove(victim);
        }
        vals.put(key, value);
        freq.put(key, 1);
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(1, f -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
        minFreq = 1;                          // a brand-new key always has count 1
    }

    private void touch(int key) {             // move key from bucket f to bucket f+1
        int f = freq.get(key);
        freq.put(key, f + 1);
        buckets.get(f).remove(key);
        if (f == minFreq && buckets.get(f).isEmpty()) minFreq++;
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(f + 1, x -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
    }
}

Capacity 2: put(1,1), put(2,2), get(1) → 1; put(3,3) evicts 2 (count 1); get(2) → -1, get(3) → 3; put(4,4): keys 1 and 3 both have count 2, 1 is older, so evict 1; get(1), get(3), get(4) → -1, 3, 4. Time O(1) average per operation.

Min Stack — LC 155 (Medium)

A stack with push, pop, top and getMin, all O(1).

Key insight: the minimum only changes on push and pop, in stack order. So store, with every entry, the minimum at the moment it was pushed. Popping automatically restores the previous minimum.

class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // {value, min so far}

    public void push(int val) {
        int min = stack.isEmpty() ? val : Math.min(val, stack.peek()[1]);
        stack.push(new int[]{val, min});
    }

    public void pop() { stack.pop(); }

    public int top() { return stack.peek()[0]; }

    public int getMin() { return stack.peek()[1]; }       // every entry remembers the min below it
}

push(-2), push(0), push(-3), getMin() → -3; pop(), top() → 0; getMin() → -2. Time O(1), Space O(n).

Implement Trie — LC 208 (Medium)

insert, search and startsWith in O(length of word). Each node holds an array of 26 children and an "end of word" flag. The full implementation and variations (wildcard search, word search II) are in section 15 — in a design round, reach for it whenever the question says "prefix" or "autocomplete".

Design Twitter — LC 355 (Medium)

postTweet, follow, unfollow, and getNewsFeed(userId) returning the 10 most recent tweet ids from the user and everyone they follow, newest first.

Key insight: each user's tweets are already sorted by time (you append them). The feed is a k-way merge of those lists — start a max-heap with each followed user's newest tweet, pop 10 times, and after each pop push that user's next-older tweet. A global counter gives the timestamps.

class Twitter {
    private int time = 0;                                  // global clock: bigger = newer
    private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Integer, List<int[]>> tweets = new HashMap<>();   // user -> {time, tweetId}

    public void postTweet(int userId, int tweetId) {
        tweets.computeIfAbsent(userId, u -> new ArrayList<>()).add(new int[]{time++, tweetId});
    }

    public List<Integer> getNewsFeed(int userId) {
        Set<Integer> users = new HashSet<>(follows.getOrDefault(userId, Set.of()));
        users.add(userId);                                 // your own tweets count too
        // K-way merge: heap entry = {time, tweetId, userId, index in that user's list}
        PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(b[0], a[0]));
        for (int u : users) {
            List<int[]> list = tweets.get(u);
            if (list != null && !list.isEmpty()) {
                int i = list.size() - 1;                   // newest tweet of this user
                heap.offer(new int[]{list.get(i)[0], list.get(i)[1], u, i});
            }
        }
        List<Integer> feed = new ArrayList<>();
        while (!heap.isEmpty() && feed.size() < 10) {
            int[] top = heap.poll();
            feed.add(top[1]);
            int i = top[3] - 1;                            // that user's next-older tweet
            if (i >= 0) {
                int[] t = tweets.get(top[2]).get(i);
                heap.offer(new int[]{t[0], t[1], top[2], i});
            }
        }
        return feed;
    }

    public void follow(int followerId, int followeeId) {
        if (followerId != followeeId)
            follows.computeIfAbsent(followerId, u -> new HashSet<>()).add(followeeId);
    }

    public void unfollow(int followerId, int followeeId) {
        Set<Integer> s = follows.get(followerId);
        if (s != null) s.remove(followeeId);
    }
}

postTweet(1,5), getNewsFeed(1) → [5]; follow(1,2), postTweet(2,6), getNewsFeed(1) → [6, 5]; unfollow(1,2), getNewsFeed(1) → [5]. Feed cost O(F + 10 log F) for F followees; post/follow O(1).

Time Based Key-Value Store — LC 981 (Medium)

set(key, value, timestamp) and get(key, timestamp) returning the value with the largest timestamp <= the given one (or "").

Key insight: "largest key <= t" is exactly TreeMap.floorEntry(t). (LeetCode guarantees timestamps arrive in increasing order, so an ArrayList per key with your own binary search also works and is a common follow-up.)

class TimeMap {
    private final Map<String, TreeMap<Integer, String>> store = new HashMap<>();

    public void set(String key, String value, int timestamp) {
        store.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new TreeMap<>()).put(timestamp, value);
    }

    public String get(String key, int timestamp) {
        TreeMap<Integer, String> history = store.get(key);
        if (history == null) return "";
        Map.Entry<Integer, String> e = history.floorEntry(timestamp);  // latest time <= timestamp
        return e == null ? "" : e.getValue();
    }
}

set("foo","bar",1); get("foo",1) → "bar"; get("foo",3) → "bar"; set("foo","bar2",4); get("foo",4) → "bar2"; get("foo",5) → "bar2"; get("foo",0) → "". Time O(log n) per call, Space O(n).

Insert Delete GetRandom O(1) — LC 380 (Medium)

A set with insert, remove and getRandom (each element equally likely), all average O(1).

Key insight: getRandom needs index access, so values live in an ArrayList. But removing from the middle of a list is O(n) — so swap the victim with the last element and remove from the end. A map from value to index makes finding the victim O(1), and you must update the moved element's index.

class RandomizedSet {
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> index = new HashMap<>();  // value -> position in list
    private final List<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Random rnd = new Random();

    public boolean insert(int val) {
        if (index.containsKey(val)) return false;
        index.put(val, values.size());
        values.add(val);                      // append: O(1)
        return true;
    }

    public boolean remove(int val) {
        Integer i = index.get(val);
        if (i == null) return false;
        int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
        values.set(i, last);                  // move the last value into the hole
        index.put(last, i);
        values.remove(values.size() - 1);     // remove from the END: O(1)
        index.remove(val);
        return true;
    }

    public int getRandom() {
        return values.get(rnd.nextInt(values.size()));   // needs an index: that's why a list
    }
}

Time O(1) average for all three, Space O(n).

Common mistakes: (1) Updating one structure but not the other — e.g. evicting from the list but leaving the key in the map. Walk through every method and check both. (2) In LRU, forgetting that put on an existing key must also move it to the front. (3) In the linked list, updating pointers in the wrong order and losing the rest of the list — set the new node's pointers first, then its neighbours'. (4) In RandomizedSet, removing by index with values.remove(i) in the middle (O(n)), or forgetting to update the moved element's index. (5) Min Stack with a single min variable — after popping the minimum you can't recover the previous one. (6) Not stating the complexity of each method — that is the whole point of the question.

Pattern Cheat Sheet — if you see X, try Y

In plain words

Problem statements leave clues: the shape of the input, the words in the question, and the size limits. Each clue below points to a pattern that usually works. Treat it as a first guess to test, not a rule — say the guess out loud, then check it against the examples.

If you see…Try…Section
Sorted array + find a pair / triplet with a sumTwo pointers from both ends30
Remove duplicates / partition / move zeros in placeTwo pointers (slow writer, fast reader)30
Longest / shortest contiguous subarray or substring with a conditionSliding window (grow right, shrink left)31
Subarray sum equals k, negatives allowed; many range-sum queries, no updatesPrefix sum + hash map32
Add a value to many ranges, then read onceDifference array32
Linked list cycle, middle of list, "happy number"Fast & slow pointers33
Reverse a list / a part of it / in groups of kIn-place reversal (prev, cur, next)37
Next greater / next smaller element, span, histogram area, temperaturesMonotonic stack34
Max / min of every window of size kMonotonic deque34
Overlapping intervals, meeting rooms, merge rangesSort by start, then sweep (or a min-heap of end times)35
Array holds numbers in 1..n; find missing / duplicateCyclic sort (put each value at index value - 1)36
Valid parentheses, undo, evaluate expression, nested structureStack9
Seen before? count occurrences? pair with complement?HashMap / HashSet7
Tree: anything per level, "closest to root", right side viewTree BFS with size snapshot38
Tree: paths, heights, subtree answersTree DFS (top-down or bottom-up)38
BST: k-th smallest, validate, rangeIn-order traversal (gives sorted order)13
k-th largest / top k / k most frequent / k closestHeap of size k (min-heap for largest); quickselect; bucket sort39
Merge k sorted lists / arrays; k-th smallest in sorted matrixK-way merge with a heap39
Median of a stream / sliding window medianTwo heaps40
All subsets / combinations / permutations / arrangementsBacktracking (start index or used[])41
Place items under constraints (N-Queens, Sudoku, word search)Backtracking with pruning21
Sorted / rotated input, or "O(log n) required"Binary search20, 42
"Minimum capacity / speed / largest minimum such that possible"Binary search on the answer + greedy check42
Dependencies, prerequisites, build orderTopological sort (Kahn's)43
Grid of land / water, regions, flood fillDFS / BFS on the grid44
"Spreads from many sources", distance to nearest XMulti-source BFS44

Count ways / min cost / max value with choices, overlapping subproblemsDynamic programming24, 25
Two strings: edit distance, LCS, matching2D DP over (i, j)25
Pick items under a capacity / target sumKnapsack DP25
Local best choice is provably safe (intervals by end time, jumps)Greedy22
Split in halves, combine (count inversions, merge sort)Divide & conquer23
Find a pattern in text fast, repeated substringsKMP / Z-function / rolling hash28
Single number, XOR tricks, subsets as bitmasks, power of twoBit manipulation5
GCD, primes, modular arithmetic, "answer modulo 10^9+7"Math / number theory29
"Design a class with O(1) operations"HashMap + list / heap / TreeMap45

Constraints tell you the complexity

A judge runs roughly 108 simple operations per second. Put n into your complexity and you know if the idea will pass before you write a line.

n up toComplexity that fitsUsually means
10–12O(n!), O(n · n!)Permutations, brute force over orderings
20–25O(2n), O(n · 2n)Subsets, backtracking, bitmask DP
100O(n4)Several nested loops, small interval DP
500O(n3)Floyd-Warshall, interval DP
5,000O(n2)2D DP, all pairs, nested loops
105–106O(n log n) or O(n)Sorting, heap, binary search, two pointers, sliding window, hashing
108 and moreO(log n) or O(1)Binary search on the answer, math formula

Complexity Cheat Sheet

In plain words

The costs to know without thinking. "Average" for hash structures assumes a decent hash function; "amortized" means an occasional expensive step (like resizing) averaged over many cheap ones.

Data structure operations

Structure (Java)Access by indexSearchInsertDeleteNotes
Array int[]O(1)O(n); O(log n) if sortedO(n)O(n)Fixed size
ArrayListO(1)O(n)O(1) amortized at end; O(n) in middleO(1) at end; O(n) in middleResizes by 1.5x
LinkedListO(n)O(n)O(1) at ends / at a known nodeO(1) at ends / at a known nodeRarely the best choice in Java
ArrayDeque (stack / queue)—O(n)O(1) amortized at both endsO(1) at both endsUse instead of Stack and LinkedList
HashMap / HashSet—O(1) averageO(1) averageO(1) averageWorst O(log n) per bucket (tree bins) in Java 8+
TreeMap / TreeSet—O(log n)O(log n)O(log n)Sorted; floor / ceiling / first / last in O(log n)
PriorityQueue (binary heap)peek O(1)O(n)O(log n)poll O(log n); remove(x) O(n)Building from a collection: O(n)
Trie—O(L)O(L)O(L)L = word length; memory O(total chars × alphabet)
Union-Find (path compression + union by rank)—find ~O(1) (α(n))union ~O(1)—α is the inverse Ackermann function, <= 4 in practice
Fenwick / segment tree—range query O(log n)point update O(log n)—Build O(n)

Learning Path & Must-Do Problems

In plain words

Learn a topic, then immediately practise its pattern. Each week below pairs data structures and algorithms with the patterns that use them. About 10–15 hours a week is enough; the order matters more than the speed. If you have less time, do weeks 1–8 and the "must-do" list — that covers most interview questions.

12-week plan

WeekLearnPatterns to practise
1Approach, Big-O, recursion, Java toolkit, bits (1–5)Write every Java collection from memory; 5 easy problems
2Arrays & strings, hashing (6, 7)Two pointers (30), prefix sum (32)
3Sliding window, cyclic sortSliding window (31), cyclic sort (36)
4Linked lists, stacks, queues (8–10)Fast & slow (33), list reversal (37), monotonic stack (34)
5Sorting, binary search (19, 20)Merge intervals (35), modified binary search (42)
6Binary trees, BSTs, TreeMap (12–14)Tree BFS & DFS (38)
7Heaps (11)Top K & k-way merge (39), two heaps (40)
8Backtracking, tries (21, 15)Subsets & permutations (41)
9Graphs, union-find (16, 17)Matrix & islands (44), topological sort (43)
10Shortest paths, MST, greedy (26, 27, 22)Dijkstra problems, interval greedy
11Dynamic programming (24, 25)1D DP, knapsack, 2D grid / string DP
12Leftovers: divide & conquer, strings, math, segment trees (23, 28, 29, 18); design (45)Mock interviews, mixed random problems, redo old mistakes

That is 79 problems. If time is short, do the first two of every row first (about 35), then come back for the rest. The pattern sections above each end with a longer practice list.

How to practise

  • Timebox. Give a new problem 25–30 minutes (45 for a Hard). If you have no working idea by then, read the solution — but only the idea, then close it and write the code yourself.
  • Name the pattern first. Before coding, say which pattern from the cheat sheet you are using and why. If you can't, that is the thing to study.
  • Redo after 3 days, then after 2 weeks. Keep a list of problems you needed help with. Solve them again from a blank editor. A problem is "done" only when you can solve it cold.
  • Explain aloud. Talk through the approach, the complexity, and one edge case as if an interviewer were listening. Record yourself or practise with a friend — silent solving does not train the interview.
  • Test by hand. Before running code, trace a small example and the edge cases: empty input, one element, all equal, negative numbers, overflow.
  • Mix it up in the last weeks. Doing ten sliding-window problems in a row tells you the pattern in advance. Random mixed sets train the real skill: recognising which pattern to use.
The goal is not to memorise 300 answers. It is to know about 20 patterns so well that a new problem feels like a variation of one you have already solved. When you finish a problem, write one line in your notes: "signal → pattern → key trick". That line is what you will remember in the interview.
